|------|------|----------|----------|
| Loewy登上Time封面 | 媒体事件 | 1949年10月31日 | 工业设计名人文化的顶点 |
| MoMA Good Design首次展览 | 设计事件 | 1950年1月，芝加哥Merchandise Mart | 精英审美标准对商业化设计的挑战 |
| Bel Geddes办公室关闭 | 商业事件 | 1947年（财务问题） | 1930年代最耀眼先驱的商业陨落 |

### 概念/运动实体
| 名称 | 类型 | 简要说明 | 章中角色 |
|------|------|----------|----------|
| "Good Design" | 精英审美标准 | MoMA定义——以"缺乏装饰"为核心 | 博物馆试图控制"设计质量"的定义权 |
| Dreyfuss Five Points | 设计方法论 | 便利/维护/成本/销售/外观 | 对MoMA单一美学标准的系统性反驳 |
| Design Piracy | 行业问题 | Horwitt vs. Movado（27年诉讼） | 工业设计知识产权保护的困境 |

### 地点实体
| 名称 | 类型 | 简要说明 | 章中角色 |
|------|------|----------|----------|
| Palm Springs, CA | 居住地 | Loewy的沙漠住宅Tierra Caliente | 工业设计师名人生活方式的象征 |
| South Bend, IN | 工业城市 | Loewy Studebaker工作室所在地 | 独立汽车设计的中心 |
| 芝加哥Merchandise Mart | 商业建筑 | Good Design展览主场 | MoMA设计话语权的"商业前线" |

## 九、与前后章关联 L###

### 承接上一章
第10章SID的成立和战后消费繁荣为第11章Loewy的名人化提供了制度基础和市场条件。第10章设计教育的争论在本章延续为Dreyfuss五要素——对"什么是好的工业设计"的实践性回答。

### 为下一章铺垫
1. **MoMA Good Design计划**引发的争议在第12章中将持续发酵。
2. **韩国战争**（章末暗示——"another war began"）在第12章中威胁材料供应。
3. **Loewy组织的庞大化**在第12章中将面临新的挑战。

### 叙事弧线位置
本章位于全书**"名人化顶峰"**——工业设计先驱一代在公众认知和社会地位上达到了历史最高点（Loewy的Time封面），但精英博物馆的反制（Good Design）和内部署名政治的复杂性（1949 Ford的真正设计者）揭示了行业光鲜表面下的深层次矛盾。

# 第12章分析报告：《Competition and Controversy》

## 一、章节定位与功能 L###

本章为全书第12章（共14章），对应历史时期约为1950年至1959年。承担**"成熟期的竞争与争议"**叙事功能——工业设计行业在战后走向成熟的同时，面临多重挑战：韩国战争材料限制、IDI vs. ASID的组织竞争、"有计划废止"引发的道德争议（Vance Packard攻击）、Detroit过度设计的批判、以及欧洲和日本设计竞争的兴起。

## 二、结构分析 L###

**第一段（1950-52年）**：韩国战争引发材料限制→Müller-Munk呼吁减少装饰。设计组织竞争：ADI更名为IDI（1951，回应"缺乏工业设计"的批评），CSID并入。IDI启动首个全国设计奖（首次将个体设计师署名）。Loewy出版《Never Leave Well Enough Alone》。

**第二段（1953-55年）**：韩国战争结束。汽车设计：Corvette（1953，首个玻璃纤维量产车身）、Studebaker Starliner（Bourke设计，获MoMA罕见赞扬）。Bel Geddes 1958年去世（65岁）。ASID十周年（1954年成立，153名会员）。Industrial Design杂志创刊（1954）。Dreyfuss出版《Designing for People》（1955）引入Joe和Josephine人体工程学图表。

**第三段（1956-57年）**：Noyes为IBM设计首个美国"企业形象"项目。WDTA设计莫斯科Gorky Park美国馆。Stevens提出"planned obsolescence"术语。Packard出版《The Hidden Persuaders》（1957），攻击设计师"操纵"消费者→行业被迫公开回应。Edsel灾难（1958，Ford损失$3.5亿）。Federal Interstate Highway动工（1956，Bel Geddes Futurama的全面实现）。

**第四段（1958-59年）**：Earl退休（1958年12月），Mitchell接任——"尾鳍时代"结束。Fortune杂志调查：300个设计组织，120个企业内部部门，180个咨询公司。竞争加剧，明星设计师的高昂收费时代结束。MoMA Good Design展览终止（1955）。

## 三、内容分析 L###

### 核心论题
1950年代的工业设计面临"成功的代价"——行业在商业上取得了空前成功，但引发了道德争议（Packard的"planned obsolescence"攻击），遭受了审美批判（Detroit尾鳍的过度设计），遭遇了国际竞争（欧洲和日本设计的复苏），并目睹了第一代先驱者（Bel Geddes 1958年去世）的逐渐离场。

### 关键论点
1. **"Planned obsolescence"争议揭示了工业设计内在的伦理张力**——促进消费 vs. 操纵消费者的边界究竟在哪里？
2. **Detroit过度设计的危机（Edsel灾难，1958）验证了工业设计师长期以来的批评**——"styling"（装饰性造型）已经取代"industrial design"（综合性设计）。
3. **1950年代标志着工业设计从"美国独霸"向"全球竞争"的转型**——欧洲（Braun）、日本和德国设计的崛起开始挑战美国主导地位。
4. **第一代先驱者开始离场**——Bel Geddes（1958）、Van Doren（1957）、Jensen（1954）、Kostellow（1954）、Budlong（1955）、Swainson（1955）——1930年代的英雄一代正在退出历史舞台。

### 关键案例/人物
1. **Dreyfuss《Designing for People》（1955）**：引入Joe和Josephine人体工程学图表——将"人体因素/人机工程学"确立为工业设计的科学基础。
2. **Edsel灾难（1958）**：Ford损失$3.5亿——"垂直进气口像一个张开嘴的人脸在尖叫"——市场调查无法替代设计判断的终极教训。
3. **Bel Geddes去世（1958，65岁）**：Federal Interstate Highway System（1956开工）是其1939年Futurama的全面现实化——他生前看到了自己最大胆的梦想成真。
4. **Vance Packard《The Hidden Persuaders》（1957）**：称Stevens为"planned obsolescence的皇太子"——工业设计面临最严峻的公共形象危机。
5. **Eliot Noyes为IBM设计首个"企业形象"（1956）**：产品、图形、展览、包装、建筑全面统一——"首个美国'house style'项目"。
6. **Earl退休（1958）**：42英寸高的1959 Cadillac尾鳍是其时代的最后宣言——"尾鳍时代结束"。

## 四、逻辑梳理 L###

### 主要论证链条
1950年韩国战争→材料限制→呼吁减少装饰→1953年战争结束→消费繁荣→Detroit过度设计（尾鳍竞赛）→设计师批评Detroit→1957年Packard攻击整个行业→行业被迫为"planned obsolescence"辩护→1958年Edsel灾难验证了设计师的批评→Earl退休→1959年Fortune调查显示竞争加剧→明星收费时代终结→1950年代后期先驱者逐渐离世。

## 五、材料使用方式 L###

**设计师著作**（Dreyfuss 1955、Bel Geddes 1960年遗著）、**行业调查**（Fortune 1959年300家设计机构统计）、**批评文献**（Packard 1957年《The Hidden Persuaders》）、**企业史料**（Edsel损失$3.5亿）、**组织档案**（ADI→IDI更名记录、ASID十周年）。

## 六、论辩与阐述方法 L###

作者对Packard的批评采取"为其辩护但承认其问题"的平衡立场——承认"planned obsolescence"可以作为产品真正改进的正当理由，但批评"虚假淘汰"（phony obsolescence）。对Detroit的批判则立场鲜明（引用Teague："lousy, ugly, ostentatious, impractical, and excessively costly"）。

## 七、语言与文风 L###

### 原文摘录

**摘录一**（Teague 1959年对Detroit设计的愤怒批评）：
> "'I agree heartily with millions of others that our characteristic car design has in recent years become lousy, ugly, ostentatious, impractical, and excessively costly; and what is worse from management's point of view, this kind of design has come a spectacular cropper in the market.'"

译文："'我衷心同意数百万人的看法：我们典型的汽车设计近年来已变得糟糕、丑陋、炫耀、不切实际且过于昂贵；而更糟糕的是，从管理层的角度看，这种设计在市场上遭到了惨败。'"

**摘录二**（Dreyfuss的设计哲学）：
> "'We bear in mind that the object being worked upon is going to be ridden in, looked at, talked into, activated, operated, or in some other way used by people individually or en masse.'"

译文："'我们始终牢记，正在被设计的物品将被人乘坐、观看、对话、激活、操作，或以其他方式被个人或群体使用。'"

## 八、实体清单 L###

## 九、与前后章关联 L###

### 承接上一章
第11章Loewy的Time封面名人化和MoMA Good Design计划在本章发展为更广泛的行业争议（Packard攻击、"planned obsolescence"辩护、Detroit批评）。第11章末尾暗示的战争（韩国）在本章开篇即成为现实。

### 为下一章铺垫
1. **先驱者离世浪潮**（Bel Geddes 1958）在第13章中将继续。
2. **冷战设计外交**（莫斯科美国馆）在第13章中扩大——1959年莫斯科美国国家展览。
3. **计算机时代的到来**（IBM 702/企业形象）在第13章中加速发展。
4. **设计全球化和竞争**在第13章中深化。

### 叙事弧线位置
本章处于**"成熟期危机"**——工业设计行业在1950年代后期同时面临道德正当性（Packard）、审美方向（Detroit过度设计）、代际更替（先驱者去世）和国际竞争（欧洲日本复兴）四重挑战。1930年代建立起来的"无辜的商业乐观主义"在此章首次受到系统性质疑。

# 第13章分析报告：《Cold War, Mergers, and Computers》

## 一、章节定位与功能 L###

本章为全书第13章（共14章），对应历史时期约为1959年至1980年。核心叙事功能为**"冷战全球化与世代更替"**——工业设计在冷战背景下成为文化外交工具（莫斯科美国国家展览，1959），三大设计组织合并为IDSA（1965），先驱一代大规模离世（Teague 1960, Dreyfuss 1972, Loewy 1986），计算机时代的到来开启了全新的设计疆域。

## 二、结构分析 L###

**第一段（1959-1963年，冷战与设计外交）**：1959年莫斯科美国国家展览（Nelson/Eames设计，Nixon-Krushchev"厨房辩论"）。Teague去世（1960年12月5日），ASID设立Walter Dorwin Teague奖学金。IDI国家设计奖启动个体设计师署名。Loewy设计Air Force One（1963年肯尼迪遇刺时所用）。

**第二段（1964-1968年，代际更替加速）**：1964年纽约世博会——Dreyfuss的Futurama II（Bel Geddes 1939年Futurama的致敬/模仿）。IDSA成立（1965年，IDI+ASID+IDEA三大组织合并），Dreyfuss首任主席，Vassos首任董事长。第一代大规模离世：Teague, Dreyfuss夫妇, Müller-Munk, Platt, Earl等。Fortune 1968年"工业设计师的衰落"——引发行业反思。

**第三段（1969-1980年，新秩序建立）**：Fuller登上Time封面。Dreyfuss出版Symbol Sourcebook（1972）。Papanek《Design for the Real World》（1972）对工业设计发起道义攻击。Loewy设计Skylab空间站（1973）——"他职业生涯中最重要的项目"。Postmodern建筑挑战国际风格。IDSA年度设计奖（IDEA）于1980年重启。

## 三、内容分析 L###

### 核心论题
1960-1980年间，工业设计经历了三重转型：从"美国独白"到"全球化"（冷战设计外交+国际竞争），从"个人英雄"到"制度化专业"（IDSA合并+企业设计部门的崛起），从"机械时代"到"信息时代"（计算机的出现改变了设计的工具和对象）。而第一代先驱者的大规模离世标志着一个时代的终结。

### 关键论点
1. **设计成为冷战文化外交的工具**——1959年莫斯科展览中Nixon与Krushchev的"厨房辩论"，其隐含信息是："资本主义设计>共产主义设计"。
2. **IDSA的成立（1965）终结了持续20年的组织分裂**——AD(MDI)→ASID+IDE两个组织体系最终统一。
3. **Papanek的攻击（1972）代表了1960年代反文化运动对工业设计商业性的道德挑战**。
4. **Loewy的Skylab项目（1973）标志着工业设计从"消费商品"向"人类生存环境"的边界扩展**。

### 关键案例/人物
1. **1959年莫斯科美国国家展览**：Nelson/Eames设计，Fuller的geodesic dome——设计作为冷战宣传工具。
2. **Teague去世（1960）**：最受尊敬的先驱者之一，其公司至今仍以Teague为名运营。
3. **Dreyfuss设计Futurama II（1964）**：对Bel Geddes 1939年原版的致敬——时隔25年的二代版本。
4. **IDSA成立（1965）**：Dreyfuss首任主席——"为美国工业设计提供统一的声音"。
5. **Papanek《Design for the Real World》（1972）**："工业设计将谋杀置于批量生产的基础之上"——最严厉的道德指控。
6. **Loewy设计Skylab（1973）**：NASA空间站——"他职业生涯中最重要的项目"。

### 核心概念/术语
- **IDSA**：1965年成立，IDI+ASID+IDEA三合一——美国工业设计的统一专业组织。
- **Cold War Design Diplomacy**：设计作为美苏意识形态竞争的工具。
- **Postmodern Architecture**：1970年代起挑战国际风格的建筑运动——将逐渐影响产品设计。

## 四、逻辑梳理 L###

### 主要论证链条
冷战加剧→设计成为文化外交工具（1959莫斯科展）→三大设计组织酝酿合并→1965年IDSA成立→先驱一代大规模离世（Teague 1960→Earl 1969→Dreyfuss 1972→Loewy 1986）→计算机时代来临→1970年代社会运动→Papanek道德攻击→设计行业自我反思→IDEA奖重启（1980）。

## 五、材料使用方式 L###

**政府档案**（USIA莫斯科展览记录）、**设计师著作**（Dreyfuss Symbol Sourcebook、Papanek Design for the Real World）、**IDSA组织记录**（1965年合并文件）、**Fortune杂志**（1968年"衰落"文章）、**NASA项目记录**（Skylab设计）。

## 六、论辩与阐述方法 L###

作者对Papanek攻击的处理值得注意——不否认其提出的社会问题（环境保护、残障设计、第三世界需求），但批评其将工业设计的全部历史简化为"刑事疏忽"的极端立场。对先驱者离世的处理带有明显的挽歌色彩。

## 七、语言与文风 L###

### 原文摘录

**摘录一**（Papanek对工业设计的激进指控）：
> "'Today, industrial design has put murder on a mass production basis.'"

译文："'今天，工业设计已将谋杀置于批量生产的基础之上。'"

**摘录二**（Huxtable 1979年对机器时代设计师的重新评价）：
> "'While the tastemakers were in hot pursuit of a mythical 'machine art' realized largely in handmade prototypes or limited production for educated users, the industrial designers were successfully creating mass-produced objects for a universal market.'"

译文："'当品味制定者们热衷于追逐在手工原型或面向受教育用户的限量生产中实现的虚幻'机器艺术'时，工业设计师们正在成功地为大众市场创造批量生产的物品。'"

## 八、实体清单 L###

### 人物实体
| 名称 | 类型 | 简要说明 | 章中角色 |
|------|------|----------|----------|
| Walter Dorwin Teague | 工业设计师 | 1960年12月5日去世（77岁） | 最受尊敬的先驱者，ASID设立其名奖学金 |
| Henry Dreyfuss | 工业设计师 | IDSA首任主席，1972年与妻子共同自杀 | Symbol Sourcebook作者，人体工程学先驱 |
| Raymond Loewy | 工业设计师 | Skylab空间站设计（1973），1986年去世 | 最后离世的"三巨头"之一 |
| Buckminster Fuller | 发明家/设计师 | Time封面人物，geodesic dome | 冷战设计外交的标志性人物 |
| Victor Papanek | 设计批评家/教育者 | Design for the Real World（1972） | 对工业设计发起最严厉道德攻击的批评者 |

## 九、与前后章关联 L###

### 承接上一章
第12章先驱者开始离世（Bel Geddes 1958等）在第13章演变为大规模离世浪潮。第12章ASID的两个组织分立在1965年通过IDSA合并而终结。第12章末尾的"冷战"在第13章成为设计外交的推动力。

### 为下一章铺垫
1. **Postmodern建筑的出现**（Pompidou Center 1977）在第14章发展为Memphis和产品设计中的后现代运动。
2. **计算机时代的开始**在第14章发展为CAD和数字设计工具的革命。
3. **最后一批先驱者的离世**在第14章中完成——全书以"他们现在都已离我们而去了"的挽歌结束。

### 叙事弧线位置
本章处于全书的**"黄昏与转型"**阶段——1930年代的英雄一代在此章大量辞世，其创建的组织（IDSA）和事务所（Teague, Sundberg-Ferar等）转型为新一代领导的制度化实体。工业设计从"个人英雄主义"的黄金时代进入"制度化和全球化"的成熟时代。

# 第14章分析报告：《Postmodern and Passages》

## 一、章节定位与功能 L###

本章为全书第14章（最后一章），对应历史时期约为1981年至2014年（本书出版年份）。核心叙事功能为**"终章：风格轮回与先驱者的最后告别"**——以Memphis后现代设计运动（1981）为起点，以"他们现在都已离我们而去了"为结束，追踪最后一批先驱者的离世、工业设计的全球化转型，以及对机器时代开创者遗产的最终评价。

## 二、结构分析 L###

**第一段（1981-1990年，后现代设计运动）**：意大利Memphis集团（1981）——"反功能、荒谬、时髦"的设计故意挑战Bauhaus传统。美国教育者提出"产品语义学"。先驱者继续离世：Fuller（1983）、Nelson（1986）、Loewy（1986）、Deskey（1989）。

**第二段（1990-2000年，全球化与数字化）**：Brooklyn Museum《The Machine Age in America, 1918–1941》回顾展（1986-1988）——对1930年代工业设计历史地位的学术确认。CAD取代手绘渲染。IDSA奖项国际化（2004年）。中国设计崛起（2006年胡锦涛"创新是竞争力的核心"）。

**第三段（Legacy，遗产总结）**：2011年美国邮政发行12位工业设计师纪念邮票。众多先驱者事务所至今仍在运营（Teague, Sundberg-Ferar, Brooks Stevens, PMMA, Nessen Lighting）。全书以1939年世博会指南的引文作为终章——"二十世纪真正的诗人是设计师、建筑师和工程师。"

## 三、内容分析 L###

### 核心论题
后现代设计的兴起（Memphis, 1981）完成了对Bauhaus/国际风格的反叛闭环——具有深刻讽刺意味的是，1930年代因"肤浅"和"商业主义"被MoMA批判的流线型和摩天楼风格，在后现代视角下被重新评价为"只不过提前了50年"。全书以工业设计先驱者遗产的全面肯定收束——他们的创造在今天以两种形式存在：仍在运营的事务所和他们建立的全球化专业体系。

### 关键论点
1. **Memphis（1981）完成了1950年代"Good Design"所压抑的反叛**——装饰、色彩、情感和"荒谬"重新成为设计的合法元素。
2. **流线型和摩天楼风格在后现代视角下被重新评价**——"只不过是提前了50年！"
3. **CAD/计算机革命彻底改变了设计工具**——但Gantz强调："过程保持不变。"
4. **中国设计的崛起（2000年代）为全球设计版图增加了新的权力中心**。

### 关键案例/人物
1. **Memphis集团（1981）**：Casablanca餐边柜——以摇滚乐为背景的"反功能"家具设计。
2. **Brooklyn Museum《The Machine Age in America》展（1986-1988）**：对1930年代美国现代设计的学术正名——"对机器人时代这一重要时期的大师级回顾"。
3. **2011年美国邮政12位设计师纪念邮票**：Rhead, Teague, Bel Geddes, Loewy, Deskey, Rohde, von Nessen, Wright, Dreyfuss, Müller-Munk, Chapman, Noyes。
4. **尚在运营的先驱者事务所**：Teague（250设计师，2012年）、Sundberg-Ferar、Brooks Stevens Inc.、PMMA、Nessen Lighting。
5. **Raymond Loewy（1986年去世，93岁）**：最后一位"三巨头"——在其法国里维埃拉家中离世。

### 核心概念/术语
- **Postmodern / Memphis**：强调表面装饰、色彩、情感和"荒谬"——对Bauhaus/Good Design传统的反叛。
- **Product Semantics（产品语义学）**：设计作为语言——"人造形式在其使用语境中的象征品质研究"。
- **CAD (Computer-Aided Design)**：计算机辅助设计——彻底改变了设计的工具，但未改变设计的核心过程。

## 四、逻辑梳理 L###

### 主要论证链条
Bauhaus/"Good Design"的简约几何形式延续30年→设计界疲劳→1981年Memphis反叛（装饰/色彩/情感回归）→1986-88年《The Machine Age in America》展为1930年代流线型/摩天楼风格正名→后现代视角下流线型被重新评价——"只是提前了50年"→计算机/全球化改造设计工具和市场→2011年邮票纪念→先驱者全部离世但遗产永存。

## 五、材料使用方式 L###

**博物馆展览**（Brooklyn Museum 1986-88、Cooper-Hewitt Dreyfuss回顾展1997）、**设计运动文献**（Memphis、产品语义学）、**行业数据**（2004年IDSA调查：设计提升47%销量，仅增加24%成本）、**邮票/纪念物**（2011年美国邮政12位设计师邮票）、**事务所运营资料**（Teague 250位设计师、Sundberg-Ferar 15000平方英尺工作室）。

## 六、论辩与阐述方法 L###

本章是全书的"收束"——带有强烈的挽歌和肯定双重色彩。对后现代设计的叙述承认了风格循环的不可避免，但最终将论证收束于一个核心判断：1930年代先驱者发明的不是某一种风格（流线型或有机或Good Design），而是**工业设计的核心过程**本身——"这个过程保持不变"，即使工具（从手绘到CAD）已经改变。

## 七、语言与文风 L###

### 原文摘录

**摘录一**（Huxtable 1979年对机器时代设计师的最终正名）：
> "'While the tastemakers were in hot pursuit of a mythical "machine art" realized largely in handmade prototypes or limited production for educated users, the industrial designers were successfully creating mass-produced objects for a universal market.'"

译文："'当品味制定者们热衷于追逐在手工原型或面向受教育用户的限量生产中实现的虚幻"机器艺术"时，工业设计师们正在成功地为大众市场创造批量生产的物品。'"

**摘录二**（全书结尾，1939年世博会指南的引用）：
> "'The true poets of the twentieth century are the designers, architects, and the engineers who glimpse some inner vision, create some beautiful figment of the imagination, and then translate it into valid reality for the world to enjoy.'"

译文："'二十世纪真正的诗人是那些捕捉到内在愿景、创造出想象中美丽幻象、然后将其转化为可供世人享用的真实存在的设计师、建筑师和工程师。'"

**摘录三**（作者对后现代风格与流线型的比较）：
> "Ironically, these stylistic modifications were little different conceptually from the highly criticized (by elite modernists of the 1950s) streamlining or skyscraper styles of the 1930s, which were simply 50 years ahead of their time!"

译文："具有讽刺意味的是，这些风格上的变化在概念上与1950年代被精英现代主义者大肆批判的1930年代流线型或摩天楼风格几乎毫无区别——后者只不过是提前了50年！"

## 八、实体清单 L###

### 人物实体

|------|------|----------|----------|

## 九、与前后章关联 L###

### 承接上一章
第13章先驱者大规模离世、Postmodern建筑出现、计算机时代来临→第14章完成先驱者的最后告别、Postmodern进入产品设计（Memphis）、CAD改造设计工具。第13章的设计全球化趋势在第14章加速——中国成为全球设计新力量。

### 全书收束
作为全书最后一章，本章完成了三个核心收束：**人物收束**（"他们现在都已离我们而去了"——所有先驱者的最终离世）、**评价收束**（2011年邮票和Brooklyn Museum展——历史地位的制度化确认）、**遗产收束**（仍在运营的事务所+IDSA+持续的设计实践——"过程保持不变"）。

### 叙事弧线位置
本章是全书**"尾声：评价与遗产"**——从1930年代大萧条中数个先驱者在狭小工作室中开始的旅程，到2014年一个拥有50,000名从业者的全球专业体系的恢宏叙事，在此最终闭合。作者Gantz以一个从业者的身份完成了对先驱者们的历史性致敬。

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\00_Introduction.txt`

- sha256: `23dc7fa8bcb205abe92b391e7441e539a31606379bbc724bfd3ed8899d6d323b`

# Introduction For those who are unfamiliar with the general subject matter of this book, it may be helpful to summarize the overall context of the time. The “Machine Age” is a generic term that refers not just to machines, which had been around in industry for a century before, but to the new way in which the public perceived them. In the middle of the 1920s, people suddenly realized that mechanical machines were not just for large-scale industrial purposes, but that they actually could purchase machines such as cars, washing machines, refrigerators and vacuum cleaners at affordable prices for personal use around the home. They began to regard machines as personal possessions, rather than abstract technology. At the same time, people also became aware of style, a quality previously considered only in decorative products for display in the home, but of little significance in functional, mechanical products, such as their new household appliances. Now that they were able to afford such products, and regarded them as personal possessions, however, they were extremely proud of them and wanted them to be seen by friends and neighbors as evidence of their increasing financial success. So they wanted them in the latest styles, just like some of the recent European designs being promoted by upscale department stores as fashionable. In addition to these new perceptions, people began to realize that they could influence the way these products looked and functioned by expressing their preference of competing products on the market through purchase choice, or through customer surveys, commonly used by advertising agencies. Such public demand soon caused some manufacturers of functional, mechanical products to pay attention to the appearance of their products by engaging artists to redesign them. Most, however, saw no such need, since business was booming, and changing tooling was costly. In the midst of these interrelated developments, the Great Depression hit in 1929, and manufacturers not only lost half of their sales volume, but faced going out of business completely, which many in fact did. They became desperate, and when they learned of industrial designers in 1934, they rushed to locate a design consultant, only to find that the few available were already over their heads with work. Manufacturers' demand for such designers was so intense that it attracted designers from numerous other fields to fill the need, and the industrywide transformation of industrial products into modern styles had begun. It has not stopped since. They were, and are, called industrial designers. Today there are more than 50,000 industrial designers in the United States. By the time the profession of industrial design first became well known, in 1935, there were only about 36 pioneers who were practicing in the field. They came from all walks of life and businesses, mostly in the creative arts, to address a national problem: the economy was as bad as it had ever been, unemployment was at 25 percent, and industry was on life support because consumers were not spending. America was far behind Europe in design, particularly in massproduced electromechanical consumer products, because manufacturers of these products relied solely on engineers for their design. Mechanical products were devoid of humanistic attributes such as attractive appearance, convenience of use, or the latest styles. The pioneer “machine designers," primarily commercial artists, stage designers, and craftsmen, changed all this within a decade, by working with engineers, salesmen, and the public to transform the man-made landscape into what we now call the modern world by redesigning mechanical products to become sleek, stylish, shiny, and attractive. Their creative work attracted consumers, who began spending the money they had been hoarding because of bad economic times. Increased sales reinvigorated industry, brought America out of the Depression, and led to the highest standard of living in the world after World War II. As will be described later, it was the first successful marriage of art and industry, a visionary objective sought by many European design educators since the beginning of the 20th century to provide well-designed products to the masses. Before that, 19th-century craftsmen had dreamed of providing ordinary people with handcrafted designs previously only affordable by the wealthy. Now, in the midst of the Great Depression, the machine designers accomplished this dream by founding a new profession, which created affordable and artistic designs available in mass-produced quantities. The original pioneer designers continued to work diligently, and by the end of the 1930s they dominated the rapidly growing field. After World War II, some headed huge consulting firms or corporate design departments to meet the enormous postwar consumer demand for new and better products. As the leaders of the profession, the pioneers confirmed the status of their activity as a legal profession, established professional organizations, recommended educational programs, implemented codes of ethics, and initiated national annual design awards programs. A few became international celebrities and led the formation of international design organizations, or assisted in the economic and design development of war-torn nations after World War II as design consultants abroad. By the time some of the pioneers approached retirement, they had established large, successful organizations that continue to this day. This is the story of those few dozen pioneers who founded modern industrial design in America and changed the landscape of consumer and industrial products within a decade, one product at a time. 

---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\01_Chapter_01.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `2ba6796394a565ee3bd4f7e95f1f17d298fe6e1105a67906e7eb7e21ea7fdc1c`

## CHAPTER 1 ## The "Artless" and "Art" Industries The Machine Age (1918–1941), as it was known even at the time, not only included the Great Depression, but also the preceding decade after World War I when America led the world in industrialization, science, technology, engineering, and consumerism. Except for a brief postwar depression from 1920 to 1921, as President Warren Harding took office, the 1920s in America were a period of economic boom and prosperity, as American industry returned to peacetime production to meet consumer demand for the many new mechanical and electrical products then becoming available on the market for the first time in high quantities and at affordable prices. Among these were automobiles, led by Ford's Model T, which by 1918 comprised 50 percent of the auto market and which epitomized American mass production, also known then as Fordism. Many home appliances used electricity, which, although only half the country was electrified, mostly in urban areas, was becoming more widespread. Machines, large and small, were the leading edge of technology. Refrigerators were replacing iceboxes, ranges were replacing woodstoves, and vacuum cleaners were replacing carpet sweepers. There were electrical appliances such as mixers, fry pans, coffee pots, waffle irons, juicers, irons, roasters, and toasters. To many consumers, many of whom were women, these industrial products for the kitchen were perceived to be as magical a technology as personal home robots would seem to us today. Prices sound very reasonable today (\$260 for the Model T and \$52 for a vacuum cleaner in 1918), but in 2013 dollars, the Ford would cost \$4,020 and the vacuum cleaner \$804. Still, many households had good wages (\$2,000 per year in 1918, or \$30,700 in 2013 dollars), and since almost 50 percent of working-class wives worked for wages, many were two-income families. Credit buying was also available. Can you imagine paying 40 percent of your annual salary for a vacuum cleaner? Still, to many families, and with installment plans becoming quite common, it seemed like the American Dream coming true. After 50 years of invention and development of a massive industrial infrastructure, including gigantic steam engines, hydroelectric plants, electrified cities, factories, railroads, bridges, and paved roads, average citizens were finally seeing the products of mass production and industrialization in their own homes. Technology had finally reached their personal, daily lives. The production system had been refined, improved, and made more efficient by Philadelphia engineer Frederick Winslow Taylor (1856-1915), who in 1911 published The Principles of Scientific Management, a system of efficiency experts using stopwatches to measure every step of production in order to reduce time and labor. This process of industrial organization became known as Taylorism, and was known everywhere as the companion to Fordism, the twin jewels in the crown of mass production. All mass-produced, new, electrical, and mechanical consumer products were invented, designed, and built by engineers, the backbone and champions of 20th-century industrialization. Many were famous and known worldwide. Herbert Hoover, who would become U.S. president in 1928, was himself a wealthy mining engineer. Thomas Edison (1847–1931), Henry Ford (1863–1947), and Orville Wright (1871–1948) were still alive and being celebrated as American heroes as late as 1929. They had brought electric light, the phonograph, the automobile, and the airplane to the world. All engineers were highly respected and admired as heroes of the age. The number of engineers had increased exponentially since 1870, when there were only a hundred. By 1900 there were 45,000, and by 1930, half a million. They were building modern bridges, dams, roads, skyscrapers, airplanes, ships and trains, as well as consumer products such as automobiles, cash registers, telephones, appliances, industrial machinery, clocks, tractors, shovels and pitchforks. Engineers were trained in hundreds of technical schools and were familiar with a range of materials, stresses, structures, manufacturing processes, costs, and the many technologies, such as electricity, steam, and combustion engines, that were required to power the manufacturing equipment or the mechanical devices. The overriding objective of all engineers was to create a physical structure which, whether a suspension bridge, an airplane, a vacuum cleaner, or a toaster, was strictly functional, economical, and efficient. These were, after all, machines intended to provide humans with transportation, power, and labor saving, and these were the functions expected, highly admired and acclaimed by the public. Most consumers were delighted with any of the new mass-produced electromechanical goods being made available, despite the fact that they were generally rather mechanical looking and ungainly in appearance, and although for many they were still rather expensive. But their mechanistic look was initially a positive image of technology. The main thing was that they performed their functional objective, were available at reasonable prices, and offered a higher standard of everyday living. For those many new consumers who had never had a vacuum cleaner before, its function, not its appearance, was of primary interest and the greatest source of user satisfaction. Functional performance, power, and labor saving were the coins of the realm, and all the new appliance manufacturers competed successfully on that basis. Therefore, there were few “artists" needed or desired in the new mechanical product industries, other than those that applied a few surface decorations to the finished product, often just pinstripes or flourishes suggesting a token “human” or “artistic” touch. It is no surprise then that these products, which included autos, locomotives, cash registers, clocks, optical goods, sewing machines, washing machines, fountain pens, refrigerators, and electrical/mechanical machinery, were generally categorized at the time as the “artless industries," because they were generally designed and produced by engineers, not artists. When we talk about the Machine Age, it is this specific category of mass-produced products, the “artless industries," that is referred to, not handcrafted artistic products such as decorative objects or crafts with limited production. Not only did machines of mass production manufacture these mechanical products in the millions, but in individual function and character, the products themselves were in fact mechanical, or machinelike devices, often with multiple moving components, such as motors, mechanisms, or engines, and were often operated by electricity. When we talk about "design," we mean the formal definition: "a plan or drawing produced to show the look and function of an object before it is built or made." A designer, of course, is the person producing such a plan or drawing. Designers differ frorn artists in that they create work that has a purpose other than that of individual self-expression or esthetic beauty. Often the purpose is to meet the needs of an individual, a company, an audience, a functional operation, or all combined. When we talk about “machine designers," the primary subjects of this book, we are talking about a small, innovative group of artistic individuals in the 1930s, who accomplished what no other artists in history had done: they abandoned the artist's traditional personal freedom of self-expression and applied their professional, artistic, creative, and analytical skills to massproduced products that previously were designed solely by engineers, in order to make them more attractive to consumers, to make them more convenient and comfortable for users, to use more appropriate and economical materials, and yet to accommodate the normal business interests of cost, schedules, and manufacturing ease. To achieve this, these artists had not only needed to create attractive, hand drawn, concept sketches of their ideas to communicate them to others, but to produce mechanical drawings to instruct engineers in the specific dimensions and final shape to be manufactured by production tools. They had to work, not as an individual, but as a team member with other specialized industry disciplines, such as engineering, marketing, manufacturing, and sales. In short, they became multitalented artists-designers-engineers-businessmen-salesmen, inventing a brand new profession that as yet had no specific name. The term “artless industries" had evolved to distinguish certain mechanical products from the traditional “art industries," as defined by the Twelfth Exhibition of Contemporary American Industrial Art at the Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York, October 12 to November 22, 1931.' The “art industries” were the only industrial “art" supported, promoted, and exhibited by museums. These were industries producing decorative arts, which included ceramics, glassware, enamelwork, furniture, leatherwork, printing, jewelry, art metalwork, lighting fixtures, carpets, drapes, silverware, textiles, and wallpaper, or any product intended as a decorative object for the home. A number of these were in fact functional as well as decorative, so their designers were necessarily involved with form and function as well as surface decoration. By and large, these “decorative arts" industries grew out of the Arts and Crafts movement of the 19th century, where skilled craftspeople originally selected appropriate materials and made both decorative and functional objects individually by hand, shaping and/or decorating the surfaces manually or by hand-operated machines of the era. The craftsman was in control of all the physical and functional aspects of a product, as well as its final appearance. Even after machines were developed to produce such products in quantity, many were still designed before manufacture by craftspeople, who therefore still controlled both the functional form and its external decorative treatments. This personal involvement of a single individual craftsman in the total design of a particular product was common historically, and resulted in famous craftsmen names such as Paul Revere (silverware), Thomas Chippendale (furniture), Eli Terry (clocks), and Duncan Phyfe (furniture). Ever since Leonardo DaVinci (1452–1519), art and engineering had gone hand in hand. From the beginning of industrialization, art and industry were closely related. In the early 19th century, the “mechanic arts" or “useful arts,"² as they were called, included a wide range of technical skills, such as mechanical design, decorative design, mechanical drawing, materials analysis, crafts, machine design, production planning, art, factory design, physics, mathematics, etc. Over the century, as industrialization advanced and as educational institutions tried to teach such comprehensive technical skills, these all became individually named specialties and were separated from one other. Thus did art and engineering become separate disciplines, with specialized skills and differing motives. As 20th-century manufacturing produced more and more consumer products in greater and greater quantities, and as factories become more complex technologically, more impersonal, and more industrial in character, fewer and fewer American craftspeople or artists were interested in the artless industries dominated by engineers, dirty machinery, time clocks, and crass commercialism. Even the art industries held limited appeal to artists because such applied art generally went unrecognized and required working nine to fve in a factory environment that was less than clean, quiet, or dignified. So even though many educational institutions offered programs in “industrial art” or "applied art," most aspiring artists tended to prefer the “fine arts," such as painting or sculpture, where they could receive personal recognition and prestige as exhibitors in galleries or museums and could work in their own personal studios. This was the essential conclusion of a \$120,000 comprehensive study of industrial arts education from 1920 to 1922 by Charles R. Richards of the Cooper Union, funded by the General Education Board of the federal government.3 At the time, the terms “industrial art" and “applied art" were the most commonly used terms for art careers in industry, and as noted above, they were not popular choices for aspiring artists. But there was another, more obscure term used occasionally in the fine arts field since 1839: "industrial design," also referring to the use of artists for industrial purposes. This term had more recently been specifically defined for industrial purposes in 1913 by the U.S. commissioner of patents, Edward B. Moore, who urged modifications of existing regulations to protect the property of“industrial design, the distinguishing form of products that have marketable value" (italics in original). The existing regulations he referred to were design patents, which protected the exterior appearance of an object against copying since 1842, when they were first initiated by the patent office for "any new and original shape or configuration of any article of manufacture not known or used by others before."⁴Many museums encouraged the copying of European products by American manufacturers because they were not protected outside of the country of origin. Commissioner Moore was concerned that even U.S. design patents were not being enforced or upheld aggressively in America, thus discouraging innovative designs by Americans. America found itself in a conflicted situation in the 1920s. It commanded the most productive manufacturing industry in the world, as well as the most lucrative market for consumer products. Factories were making as many products as possible, and a stable economy enabled a mass market of middle-class consumers to buy as many products as industry could produce. Nevertheless, a few art museums and organizations, who were familiar with the European potential to dominate world markets, were warning that America was far behind Europe in applied arts and was in desperate need of more future designers in industry to compete globally. In 1918, Walter Sargent (1868–1927), director of the Department of Fine and Industrial Art at the University of Chicago, had written, The situation regarding industrial design in the United States is improving, but so far as we can estimate ... we shall need after the war about fifty thousand more industrial designers [author's italics] than are now available or in training, and probably few can be imported. Each country will need its own ... we shall have to depend on ourselves more than in the past, not only for designers, but also for styles of design.⁵ Meanwhile, during the same time, the situation in Europe was quite different. While America enjoyed the postwar benefits of mass production and a strong economy, Europeans were suffering from wartime devastation and inflation, with virtually no industrial production to restore economic health and recovery, and practically no consumer markets. America was fortunate in that it had no foreign competition. Europeans had seen the American industrial model as the key to the future for some years before the war. In 1909, Italian poet Filippo Tommaso Marinetti (1876–1944) founded a fine arts movement called Futurism, which promoted speed, technology, youth, automobiles, and airplanes, but also, sadly, fascism and war. Followers believed that the power of art and technology could change the social structures of the world and produce a dynamic, aggressive, and strong Italy, but political upheaval, scarce national resources, and World War II would prevent European countries from competing effectively with U.S. manufacturing for another 30 years. In Germany, the march of Nationalist Socialism would within ten years lead inexorably to Hitler and German rearmament, and in Russia, the Bolsheviks would transform the country into communism, where nationalized industry would support the government, not individuals. However, Europeans still led the world in modern art. In fact, the modernist movement had begun in the last part of the 19th century. France, for example, had introduced Impressionism and led Art Nouveau at the turn of the century. Prominent artists such as Pablo Picasso, Henri Matisse, Fernand Léger, Georges Braque, Marcel Duchamp, Francis Picabia, and André Derain led a parade of new interpretations of art. From 1907 to 1911, Picasso, Léger, and Braque introduced Cubism, an art movement in which objects are analyzed, broken up, and reassembled in an abstract form, often depicting objects from a multitude of viewpoints. Cubism has been considered the most influential art movement of the 20th century affecting art, sculpture, architecture, and music. The works of many of these European artists and a few Americans were first shown in America at the International Exhibition of Modern Art in New York in 1913, the so-called Armory Show, since it was held at the city's 69th Infantry Regiment Armory, at Lexington and 26th Street. Most American viewers, including President Theodore Roosevelt, rejected the new styles as obscene or primitive, certainly not art, and most American artists continued to paint or sculpt in highly traditional styles. America was not yet ready for modern art. In 1914, as World War I began, modern artist Duchamp (1887–1968) took a step that challenged the very definition of art. He took a common manufactured wire bottle rack, signed his name to it, and declared that it was art because he said it was art. In 1915, he and Francis Picabia (1879–1953) both visited New York and were publicized as prominent French artists who for the first time were, as they said, "seeking America in matters of art." Picabia praised what he saw: I adore New York. There is much about it, which is like the Paris of the old days.... I have been profoundly impressed by the vast mechanical development in America. The machine has become more than a mere adjunct of human life.... I intend to work on until I attain the pinnacle of mechanical symbolism.6 Americans were flattered to be recognized and admired by famous French artists, and Picabia was true to his word. He soon produced a number of drawings inspired by spark plugs, meshing gears, pistons, and cylinders that were published by Alfred Steiglitz's art journal, 291. Picabia's work clearly assumed that mechanical objects were worthy as works of art, a concept that at the time most Americans failed to share. The unconventional work of European artists had no effect whatsoever upon the 1915 Panama-Pacific International Exposition in San Francisco, which had been planned since 1911, primarily to celebrate the completion of the Panama Canal, but also to herald the recovery of San Francisco from the disastrous effects of its 1906 earthquake. The architecture, sculpture, and murals featured were clearly in the traditions of the 19th century, with highly realistic, classical, but allegorical statues and murals, mostly nudes, suggesting abstract ideas of abundance, progress, conquest, creation, power, etc., and historical depictions of conquistadors, pirates, and miners. The art at the exposition was a public display of America's imitation of European art as it was in the 1870s, not an appropriate expression of America's 20th-century leadership in technology, science, or manufacturing. It was art of the past. A more effective global advertisement of America's obsolescence in art and design could not be imagined. Yet despite criticism from traditionalists, modern art marched forward. In 1917 Duchamp submitted a mass-produced ceramic urinal, titled Fountain, to the Independent Artists Exhibition in New York as “art," which aroused outraged protests but nevertheless established a fundamental principle of modern art: art is whatever the artist chooses to define as art. In this case, it also suggested that common utilitarian objects could be viewed as art, which may have been a surprise to the manufacturer of the urinal, or any company making similar utilitarian products. Other European countries evolved independent styles of modern art. In 1917, Piet Mondrian (1872–1944) and Theo van Doesburg (1893-1931) initiated the Dutch movement of de Stijl (the Style), which featured primary colors arranged asymmetrically in rectangular paintings, or abstract furniture. In Russia after the 1917 Revolution, Alexandr Rodchenko (1891–1956) and Varvara Stepanova (1894–1958) founded an artistic movement called Constructivism in 1921, and in 1922 published their manifesto to “set itself the task of finding the communistic expression of material structures."7 Leon Trotsky (1879–1940) inspired Communist Party encouragement of avant-garde art. He rejected fine art but promoted the more “productive" and “constructive" fields of architecture and mass production. So artists were asked to “go into the factories, where the real body of life is made."8 Rodchenko and Stepanova inspired the founding of an avant-garde art and design school called Vkhutemas (an abbreviation ofits formal title: Higher State Artistic and Technical Workshops). After the war, Europe also led in architecture and design, which was often inspired by the American model of mass production. In the particular category of consumer product design, the primary focus of this book, the English Arts and Crafts movement dominated the latter 19th century. As the 20th century began, Arts and Crafts leadership broadened to other European countries, as France dominated Art Nouveau. In Austria in 1903, the Wiener Werkstätte (Viennese Workshops) were formed by architect Josef Hoffmann (1870–1956) and Koloman Moser (1868–1918) who, like most Arts and Crafts leaders, were opposed to mass production and rejected the evils of what they saw as cheap and shoddy products produced under abhorrent conditions. They wanted to counteract this machine-made trend by making handcrafted products that were both innovative in design and superior in quality and workmanship. Arts and Crafts workshops in America followed this same philosophy, which drew a sharp line between handmade crafts and industrial mass production, segregating art from industry on the basis of morality and artistic superiority. The core belief of the Arts and Crafts movement was that production by cold machines and unskilled labor (often under poor working conditions), even though inexpensive, had no humanistic value, and in fact was a form of slavery. However, acceptance of mass production was growing. In 1906, a member of the Dresden (Germany) Workshops, Richard Riemerschmid (1868–1957), designed a line of Machinenmöbel (machine furniture), where individual pieces were precut and assembled in a production process, rather than in the one-at-a-time tradition of craftsmen. Still, most Arts and Crafts workshops continued to oppose mass production as a threat to their livelihood, while innovative German applied arts educators radically sought a final solution (sorry—not the best term) to the problem by literally marrying art and industry. In 1907, German applied arts educator Hermann Muthesius (1861-1927) formed the Deutscher Werkbund (German Union of Work), a trade association. It consisted of 12 industrialists and 12 artists/architects, dedicated to dominating global product markets for Germany. That same year, to consummate this intended marriage of art and industry, Werkbund architect member Peter Behrens (1868-1940) was appointed as “artistic consultant" to Allgemeine Elekricitäts Gesellschaft (AEG), the German equivalent of America's General Electric, where he designed corporate graphics, products, and buildings, the first such collaboration of art and industry in practice. His designs were well in advance, designwise, of comparable products in the United States. It would be 50 years before an American corporation engaged a similar “artistic consultant” to integrate and control a single corporation's entire visual “identity' (IBM, 1956). Muthesius in 1907 also proposed the development of a new Machinenstil (Machine Style) esthetic of product forms dictated by the purpose they serve, and as the “visible expression of inner driving force of the age,9 that is, to generate new types of forms that visually communicated their intended function, as well as their manufacture by machine processes. The significant purpose of this proposal was to restore the craftsman's role in shaping mass-produced consumer products by an artistic hand and eye, a role that had been lost at the beginning of the century when engineers took over the entire process of mass production. This had ended the Arts and Crafts era and relegated artistic contribution in manufacturing to mere surface decoration then known as applied art. The innovative concept of a Machine Style would soon be advanced by one of Germany's leading applied arts schools. In 1908, Bruno Paul (1874– 1968), one of the 12 artist/architects who founded the German Werkbund, developed a Typenmöbel (standard furniture), which permitted an unlimited number of combinations of standardized, machine-made elements. After the war in 1919, Walter Gropius (1883–1969), an architect, member of the Deutscher Werkbund, and former employee and protégé of Peter Behrens in Berlin, returned from military service to take over as director of the Weimar Kunstgewerbeschule (Weimar Arts and Crafts School), founded in 1902 by Belgian architect Henry Van de Velde (1863–1957), an Arts and Crafts leader who had become a member of the Werkbund, but as a foreign national was forced to leave the country and abandon the school during the war. Gropius renamed the school Das Staatliches Bauhaus (State School of Building), commonly known only as the Bauhaus. Gropius was an avid admirer of American Fordism and Taylorism, and of American grain silos for their architectural simplicity and monumentality. He shared the belief of many in postwar Germany that setting new standards of industrial art would secure German global design leadership in the economic recovery. There at the Bauhaus, over the next decade, an avant-garde art and design faculty and their students developed principles for the design of modern architecture and products that featured simple, undecorated, geometrical modern forms, which were exquisitely crafted in quality materials. Although staffed by many of Europe's most renowned modern artists, the school in Weimar was patterned after medieval craft guild workshops. Professors were known as masters, and students progressed from Lehrlinge (apprentices) to Gesellen (journeymen) to Jungenmeister (young masters). Gropius wanted to elevate Arts and Crafts to the level of fine art. In 1923, Làszló Moholy-Nagy (1895–1946) joined the faculty as master of the metal workshop. He was born in Hungary, studied law in Budapest, and later served in World War I. Convalescing from a war wound, he took up painting and worked his way across Germany as a letterer and sign painter, arriving in Berlin in 1921, where he took up painting and experimental photography. In 1922 he participated in the Dada and Constructivist conference organized by Theo van Doesburg in Weimar. In general, many of these student designs met the idealistic standard of Muthesius's Machinenstil, and a few would be manufactured in limited quantities for the commercial market. Although the school was plagued by financial and political difficulties, it would flourish until 1924, when political demonstrations and Weimar budget cuts forced Gropius to relocate the school to Dessau, where it would come under increasing pressure by the Nazis, who were determined to rid the country of forward-looking (avant-garde) intellectuals. Another former employee and protégé of Peter Behrens, Swiss-born architect Charles Edouard Jenneret Gris (1887–1965), was also intrigued by American Fordism and Taylorism, only he used it in reverse. Designing a slaughterhouse in Paris in 1916, he included a conveyor system to “disassemble" the animals. This practice, used in the United States since 1867, was often claimed by Ford to be the inspiration of his assembly line for automobiles. In 1917 Gris established a small brick-making factory, and by 1920 he considered himself an industrialist as well as an architect. It was then that he adopted his pseudonym, “Le Corbusier" (the Raven), under which he began publishing L'Esprit Nouveau (The New Spirit), a periodical with themes of order, rationality, engineering, and technology. Twenty-eight issues were published over the next five years. As an architect, Corbusier dreamed of producing houses the way Ford produced automobiles. In 1921 he published sketches of a house called Maison Citrohan (Citrohan House), using the analogy of France's mass-produced car based on Ford's assembly line, the Citroën. His concept, he declared, could complete houses in three days made of artificial materials such as concrete. Over the next few years, Corbusier designed a number of private homes in Paris based on his Citrohan House concept. They featured stark interior white walls, furniture made of tubular steel, and single bare bulbs in light fixtures. The effect was pure, simple, modernist design that followed the German Bauhaus style and which he called “a machine for living in." In 1922, Corbusier developed plans for a “Contemporary City" for three million inhabitants that he hoped would reorganize society along lines of American industrial models. In 1923, he published Vers une Architecture (Toward an Architecture), a book summarizing his philosophy of a future world functioning like a machine. Most Americans, enjoying the first fruits of mass production in a new consumer culture, had no idea of the extensive European design or art theories that had evolved on the continent either before or after the war. Modern art was simply not part of American culture. Art, to most Americans, was pretty landscape or portrait paintings on museum walls; Greek sculptures in gardens; or expensive, handcrafted, decorative home furnishings in traditional styles imported from Europe. The concept of combining functional, mechanical products and art was literally unimaginable. By 1925, Europe had not yet recovered economically, but Paris was not only still at the leading edge of modern art, attracting scores of aspiring artists from around the world, including young Americans, but had also become the center of fashion, both in style and entertainment. African American entertainer Josephine Baker (1906–1975) became a stage sensation for her exotic dancing at the Folies Bergére cabaret and would later popularize a dance step called the Charleston. Jazz was the most popular music of the time, but the term “jazz” also meant anything exciting and fun. George Gershwin's composition of 1928, "An American in Paris," evoked the sights and sounds of Paris in the 1920s, and constituted most Americans' sole impression of Paris. Gabrielle "Coco" Chanel (1883–1971) had opened her boutique in Paris in 1919, setting global fashion trends in women's clothing with her "little black dress" and borrowed men's clothing accessories, such as suits, ties, and shirt collars, for feminine fashions in the mid-1920s. She introduced Chanel No. 5 in 1924, the first perfume to feature a designer's name. She epitomized Paris of the 1920s, where women tasted new freedoms to wear what they liked, to cut ("bob") their hair short, to wear short skirts (above the knees), to wear straight dresses without waistlines, to wear makeup and perfume, to expose bare legs, to wear high heels, to wear "cloche" (helmet-type) felt hats, to drive automobiles, or to smoke in public. Whatever happened in Paris soon became fashionable around the world. Young American women of the "Roaring Twenties," who had only recently been granted the right to vote (19th Amendment to the Constitution, May 19, 1919), shortly after being deprived of the legal right to buy alcohol (Prohibition: 18th Amendment to the Constitution, January 19, 1919), were influenced by these new Paris fashions. Many who followed the new styles were called “flappers," and their behavior was perceived pejoratively by many during Prohibition as outlandish, irresponsible, frivolous, sexually provocative, and in general as flouting social and sexual "norms." Retrospectively, of course, women were merely expressing their freedom from residual Victorian restraints against women, and at the same time establishing "fashion” as a driving social force of the 20th century, particularly for women as they became consumers of the new household appliances now becoming available. Such appliances were featured in France in 1923 at the Salon des Arts Ménages (Salon of Domestic Science). French manufacturers collaborated with domestic designers by paying them to develop new styles for presentation, and also by paying them royalties for each reproduction of their design, thus sharing risks as well as rewards. Many of the new designs were based on the basic geometric shapes of Cubist modern art. By and large, such products were not produced in large quantities, as manufacturing in Europe had not yet recovered from the war, nor had it evolved to the level required for significant competition with America. But Europe still retained its historical reputation as the center of art and design, and in fact Europeans have a number of legitimate historical claims for initiating what we now call industrial design because of the many Arts and Crafts groups that advanced the quality of design esthetically in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. However, what we call industrial design today is more broadly defined than as simply designing consumer products in a modern style, but as the process of designing products for mass production by machines in collaboration with engineering, marketing, and manufacturing, for the purpose of consumer acceptance and affordability, as well as for corporate profit, as it is practiced today. It is the Machine Age in America, in the 1920s and 1930s, that defined, pioneered, and implemented this modern process in reality, rather than in European theoretical prototypes that were designed without any manufacturing, cost, or sales considerations, and were therefore only of academic, or esthetic value. Before that, as we have seen, the only major industries in the United States involving artist/designers were the “art industries" in general, and of course there were many designers in these fields for decades before. Some of these designers made the transition to the new field of industrial design before others and could claim, as some did, to be the first “industrial designers" in the United States, long before it became well known as a specific profession. A number of them were veterans of World War I. One of the art industries was the manufacture of decorative lamps, and in about 1920, 24-year-old Chauncey Eugene (Chick) Waltman (1896–1962) was already well known for his lamp designs for the Art Lamp Company on Wabash Avenue in Chicago, where he served as designer, salesman, and advertising manager. The most successful lamp he designed at about this time was the Almco lamp. Waltman was born near Columbus, Indiana, the son of a minister. The family moved to Cincinnati, Ohio, and lived near the famed Rookwood Pottery, where Waltman, as a boy, was inspired by artists he saw working there. The family moved several times around Michigan, ending up in Grand Rapids, where Waltman was at the top of his art class in high school. While still in school, he worked in the decorating department of the Berkery and Guy Furniture Company, and by 1912 he started a decorating department at the Imperial Furniture Company. After high school he took a job in Chicago with John A. Colby and studied decorating and furniture design at night school, but after the start of World War I in 1917, he joined the Air Force and was trained at Eastman Kodak, in Rochester, New York, to prepare maps with photos and hand sketching from airplanes. In 1918, during the flu epidemic, he was sent to Cornell University for further training and to earn a commission as second lieutenant. But suddenly the war was over and he returned to Chicago and resumed night school. He soon got a job as a lamp designer for Valentine and Seaver and later with the Art ![](images/d78c066d32eb5d9c9ad45353619bf1ea71b8e932fba331858286bb4728b54f8a.jpg) Chauncey "Chick" Waltman, ca. 1930s (courtesy William Waltman). Lamp Company, where, as advertising manager, he used ads with borders by Walter Dorwin Teague (more about him later). In 1920, he and his first wife built a home in Oak Park, the upscale Chicago suburb, near where Frank Lloyd Wright's home and studio were located. But the marriage foundered and he took a new position with the Kaplan Lamp and Shade Company. In 1924, Waltman made a lucrative business deal with Kaplan. For half his normal weekly salary of \$150, he would go to Paris to study art, and spend two days a week drawing lamp designs in the new styles and send them to Kaplan for manufacture. For about a year, he studied at the Grande Chamber des Arts, biked through England and France, and traveled to Italy and North Africa. When he returned to Chicago in 1925, he set up his own freelance office, designing in wood, cast metal, wrought iron, and glass.1º After industrial design became well known in the 1930s, his office would participate as Waltman Associates, at 165 Chicago Avenue. Also among the art industries was the furniture industry, which almost exclusively produced traditional and historical styles (many still do). Among designers in this field was Kem Weber (1889–1963) who began in 1921 at age 32 to work in the design of furniture for the Barker Brothers store in Los Angeles, then the largest furniture retailer in the United States. Weber had been born in Germany as Karl Emanuel Martin (K.E.M.) Weber, apprenticed as a cabinetmaker and had graduated from the Berlin Kunstgewerbemuseum (Berlin Academy of Fine Arts) in 1912, studying under the well-known Bruno Paul (1874–1968) and after graduation working for Paul. In 1914 Paul sent him as a designer for Germany's exhibit at the 1915 Panama-Pacific Exposition in San Francisco, but he was stranded there by the start of World War I. At first he opened a modern design studio in Berkeley. But after the sinking of the Lusitania in May 1915, American sentiment turned against Germany, and as a German, he had trouble finding clients, so he worked as a lumberjack in the High Sierra. Toward the end of the war he operated a chicken farm in Los Gatos with his young wife Erika. In 1918 they settled in Santa Barbara, California, where Weber taught art classes and opened a small factory in an old church to produce modern and period furniture, but he met with only modest success. When Barker Brothers offered him a position in its design department in 1921, he jumped at the chance and moved to Los Angeles." Other artists were in the advertising business, which promoted mass-produced artless industry consumer products such as automobiles, appliances, furniture, machinery, tools, and other functional products designed by engineers. Gradually, as the demand for more attractive products grew, these artists suggested ideas for product improvement to their advertising clients. One such individual was New Zealander Joseph “Jo” Sinel, who in 1923 at age 34 was working for the Lennon Mitchell advertising agency in New York, and who designed graphics and products for clients, although specific product designs by him are not known until 1929. Sinel became a prominent industrial designer and educator, and we will later describe his career in more detail. ![](images/c11832d3f552dfebe058e23c05413f2330ea15efa49c01b59b7e3257d4081481.jpg) John Vassos, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine). Another artist who would become legendary in industrial design, John Vassos (1893–1985), designed a face lotion bottle in 1924 that became popular (with the lotion removed) when used as a hip flask during Prohibition, and sales increased dramatically by 700 percent. Vassos was born John Plato Vassacopoulos in Romania. His family moved to Constantinople where his father became a Greek publisher and diplomat. As a teenager, young Vassos drew a cartoon in his father's newspaper that offended the Turkish senate, and he was forced to leave the country in 1909 at age 16. He fled on a British ship to Palestine. During World War I, he served as a cabin steward on a Belgian steamer, which was torpedoed. He was rescued by the U.S. Navy and landed in London in 1915. He obtained papers and traveled to Boston in 1919 where his older brother Alex lived. There, he studied at the Fenway Art School; studied art and illustration with John Singer Sargent (1856–1925), the leading portrait artist of his generation; and assisted with stage designs for the Boston Opera Company. He came to New York in 1924, became an American citizen, changed his name to Vassos, opened a commercial studio called the New York Display Company, studied at the Art Students League, worked as an illustrator for Harper's and New Yorker magazines, and illustrated and published books. Furniture designers became prominent in the emerging field of industrial design as more modern designs were demanded. Among these was Leo Jiranek (1900–1990), who in 1924 took over his father's furniture design business at age 24 and became one of the industry's first freelancers. In fact, he was considered by many to be the "Dean of Furniture Designers." Jiranek, known as Jerry, was born in Grand Rapids, Michigan, and after prep school at Western Military Academy, he spent two years at Norwich University, then transferred to Princeton University in 1920, where he graduated as an engineer in 1922. Over the years, he would contribute to more furniture companies than any other designer. But in 1925, there was one furniture designer who abandoned all tradition by introducing a style intended to represent the vitality of modern America, inspired by the new steel towers rising all over Manhattan. Generally, his pieces were bookcase-cabinet combinations or desks, which featured unornamented block forms that rose in terraced levels and provided various functional storage boxes and shelves, often lacquered red, blue, or green. Typical were his Skyscraper cabinet and Skyscraper bookcase. Pieces were tall, but with a narrow footprint, perfect for small Manhattan apartments. The designer was 38-year-old Paul T. Frankl (1886- 1958), whose new Skyscraper furniture immediately became popular and was probably the first evidence of a modernistic style originating in America. Frankl was a furniture designer and maker, architect, painter, and writer born in Vienna, Austria, the son of a wealthy real estate dealer. He had been a member of the Wiener Sezession (Vienna Secession) that had early in the 20th century sought to create a new style that owed nothing to historical influence. After completing his architectural studies at the Berlin Polytechnic, he spent time in Berlin and Copenhagen before arriving in the United States in 1914 and settling in New York. He brought an outsider's perspective and a desire to create a uniquely American design esthetic. By 1922 he had established Frankl Galleries on 48th Street in New York, and by 1924 he had a showroom on Madison Avenue, where he sold furniture of his own design as well as imported modern textiles and wallpapers, calling his company Skyscraper Furniture. He described his attraction to the Skyscraper style: Modernity expresses itself in the energy of the vertical thrust of towering buildings that push themselves through the crumbling red husks of the city of the past.12 ![](images/05c60d22c74357f19a9094397100db13166e15438b7857ae9c1a9da5062e49a5.jpg) Paul T. Frankl, ca. 1938. In fact, Frankl knew that designing a furniture line inspired by these structures would have a mass appeal to Americans, and he was right. But he was a rare practitioner of modern design in America. Europe was where the action really was.


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\02_Chapter_02.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `b941321ec810710d62b5e0b4bcea882ff5278916158bb5961426960d5a9c1373`
- characters: 42300

# The Arrival of Art Deco France was the main attraction in the art world of the 1920s and naturally wanted to promote and reinforce its prewar reputation and culture of fashion, style, decorative arts and ine arts. By the early 1920s, many aspiring young American artists were being influenced by the dramatic impact of French modern art and considered a visit to, or study in, Paris as an essential part of their artistic education in modern art and culture, as well as a carefree, youthful experience in the good life of Europe. So from April to October of 1925, an Exposition International des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes (International Exposition of Modern Decorative and Industrial Arts) was held in Paris, the first "World's Fair” since the one in San Francisco ten years before. The lengthy French title would much later be shortened to Art Deco to describe the many European varieties of new designs and their subsequent impact around the world, but at the time, the designs were generally described as “modernistic." The exposition was unique in one key respect: former similar expositions traditionally celebrated past national accomplishments. In this one the intention of the organizers was to establish new standards. Exhibitors were required to display only "those exhibits that were not dependent of the art of the past."¹ Invitations went out to all countries except Germany, which was still regarded as the villain of the Great War. Twenty-six countries accepted, except for China and the United States. China did not accept because of disruptions in political matters, and the United States ostensibly because the State Department did not recommend appropriations to do so, due to lack of support from commercial interests. As Herbert Hoover, then secretary of commerce and in charge of the Coolidge administration's industrial policy, explained to the Fourth Annual Exposition of Women's Arts and Industries: The advice I received from manufacturers was that while we produced a vast volume of goods of much artistic value, they did not consider that we could contribute sufficiently varied design of unique character or of special expression in American artistry to warrant such a participation.² Obviously, U.S. manufacturers saw no significant consumer market potential in Europe, but more to the point, they had nothing to display that met the exposition's intent of new standards or its specific criteria of products that were “not dependent of the art of the past." Most U.S. products of the time were, in fact, copies of European designs. Hoover's decision did not set well with the cultural establishment, where many were already quite aware of how advanced European design and fashion had become. When Charles Richards, the same art educator who conducted the 1922 disappointing study of industrial arts education, asked Hoover to delegate a commission of 100 representatives óf trade associations, along with some artists and designers, to visit and report on the exposition, Hoover gracefully complied. The six-month exposition opened on April 28 with 4,000 guests at the inauguration, and thousands of visitors arrived on following days. It was located between the esplanade of Les Invalides and the entrances of the Grand Palais and Petite Palais, a 57-acre site, two-thirds of which contained French displays. Exhibits included work by famous glass and jewelry designer René Lalique (1860–1945), who also designed the exposition's crystal tower fountain. There were displays by a number of Parisian department stores such as Bon Marché, Printemps, and Magazins du Louvre, which created entire room “ensembles” in modernist styles. The work of French furniture and interior designer Émile-Jacques Ruhlmann (1879–1933) was displayed in a special, classical “Pavilion of the Collector” designed by French architect Pierre Patout (1879–1965). In a far corner of the exposition, generally ignored by the press, was Le Corbusier's Pavilion de L'Esprit Nouveau, a spare modern home interior and furnishings along the lines of his Maison Citrohan concept, that attracted both admiration and criticism for its lack of decoration. Russia had a pavilion displaying the work of Alexandr Rodchenko, Varvara Stepanova, and students of the art and design school, Vkhutemas, which they had established. The students won several prizes. The popular Swedish pavilion was simple in form and devoid of decoration, featuring modern crafts in typical Scandinavian styles inspired by Gregor Paulsson's (1889– 1977) 1919 book, More Beautiful Things for Everyday Life, which challenged the past and encouraged Arts and Crafts workers to use new forms of the modern age. Danish architect and designer Arne Jacobsen (1902–1971), still a student, won a silver medal for his chair design. Austria contributed Frederick Kiesler's (1890–1965) City in Space exhibit to house displays of the work of Josef Hoffman (1870–1956), architect, furniture designer, and one of the founders of the Vienna Secession. With 16 million people visiting the exposition, it was destined to have a lasting impact on applied arts around the world. Certainly Le Corbusier influenced architecture in what would become known as International Style in 1932. The decorative arts were influenced by the unusual new styles that were generally called modernistic but which included an entire range of imagery and motifs that have been variously described as streamlined, geometric, symmetric, or as a Machine Age look. Some imagery was clearly derived from French Cubism, German Bauhaus, Italian Futurism and Russian Constructivism, all described earlier. American critic Helen Appleton Read (1897–1974) reported on several important principles of modern design she observed at the exposition: It is characteristic of the new décor that the nature of the material is at all times respected and is allowed to dictate its treatment. Wood is wood, iron is iron; the bad taste that invariably ensues when the attempt is made to give the quality of one material to another, to make wood look [like] iron or marble like lace, is avoided. This universal tendency toward simplicity of outline should be of the greatest interest to the American designer if he will recognize that this is the fundamental note. All design in this country is governed by the factor of whether or not it can be reproduced in mass production. It costs no more to get out a good design than it does a bad one, and the fact that the best designs of the new décor are the simplest to the point of being geometric makes them so much the more easy to put on the market.³ Read's assumption that simpler forms were easier to produce may seem logical, but in fact most manufacturing processes could produce complex forms as easily as simple ones. Few design forms are as complex as the Model T or a 1922 kitchen stove, both popular designs of the time. Many manufacturers would argue that copying an existing design was much easier than investing in a new design, which required extensive design work as well expensive new tooling. They would argue that the cheapest of all was to keep producing the old design as long as possible. Read went on to explain her theory of the reasoning behind the new modern forms: The whole trend of modern design toward the geometric and the unadorned had its inception through the omnipresence of the machine. Because we had absorbed the shapes of the machine into our consciousness, we ended up by creating designs which reflected it, and which in turn are the most suitable for machine made processes.4 Read may have had a point here. Designers over a generation may have been so preoccupied with the impact of machines and mass production on society that they unconsciously felt compelled to invent new forms that suggested machines. The simplest explanation, however, may have been that as artists and designers often do, they simply made things different than the past to demonstrate progress in the creative arts, or to be more inventive than those who went before. It is always speculative to precisely pinpoint the intent of creative people. They just love to create new things! The American Commission submitted a report to the Department of Commerce, warning that the modern movement would inevitably reach the United States, and that Americans needed to initiate a parallel effort of our own upon lines calculated to appeal to the American consumer.. As a nation we now live artistically on warmed-over dishes. In a number of lines of manufacture we are little more than producing antiquarians. We copy, modify, and adapt the older styles with few suggestions of a new idea. It is true that this practice of reproducing older forms has been an invaluable education to our people. It is also true that the adaptation of old motives when performed with intelligence and skill continues and probably will continue to give us a large proportion of the decorative manifestations acceptable to American taste. It would seem equally true, on the other hand, that the richness and complexity of American life call for excursions into new fields that may yield not only innovations but examples well suited to the living conditions of our times.⁵ The ambiguity of this report clearly reflected conflicting opinions among commission members as to what to do about the obvious differences between the new European design direction and the traditional practices of design in the United States. Copying of older styles was justified as educational and acceptable, but innovation may yet be of some value. Some members doubted that the new modern styles would appeal to Americans. The report gave the Department of Commerce no clear recommendations for action, and even if it had, it is doubtful that Commerce could have done anything to change the current traditions and practices of manufacturers. Any shift in direction would have to come from the manufacturers themselves based on consumer demand, and at this moment in history, the general public was blissfully unaware of what was happening in Paris. This would soon change. Two and a half million Americans now had radios and were becoming more aware of world events. In England, John Logie Baird was even demonstrating a mechanical television system. Reports from other Americans who attended the Paris exposition quickly circulated within the art and design communities and the press. The Art-in-Trades Club issued a manifesto challenging American artists to compete in the design of modern furnishings. In January 1926, an editorial about the Paris exposition in the New York Times urged industry to change because the public would soon catch up with Europe in recognizing the competitive value of new designs: American captains of industry must soon change their traditional characters. True, the public may still cling to its conception of them as iron-jawed, level headed giants of efficiency .. but if the present international competition keeps up, we may find them, like the Germans, openly cooperating with museums and art schools; or, like the French, proclaiming themselves merchants of beauty.6 One of the attendees at the Paris Exposition had been Kem Weber, mentioned earlier, who had been working at the Barker Brothers furniture store in Los Angeles and represented their interest abroad. He had become an American citizen in 1924 and had Americanized his German name to only its initials, K.E.M. In 1926, he had become art director for Barker Brothers. Up to that time, Barker had exclusively featured period revival furniture in its five stores. Inspired by what he had seen in Paris, Weber in the fall of 1926 opened a new store-within-astore, christened“Modes and Manners," on the fourth floor of Barker's eleven-story building. Weber designed the wall treatments, display cases and furniture in a space with jagged shapes, bright colors, and wild patterns. Modern furniture by Weber's Austrian friend in New York, Paul Frankl, was also displayed. It was not only the first outlet for modern furniture and accessories in Southern California, but also one of the first in the country. Over the next decade, Weber would work to promote modernism with the furniture he designed there in his store studio, the Silver Tree. The store had its own production facilities to manufacture the furniture and soon opened a new special Hollywood branch, which Weber also designed.7 Museums were anxious to promote the new designs, and in February the American Association of Museums imported a collection of 400 French and Swedish objects from Paris, called Selected Collection of Objects from the International Exposition of Modern Decorative and Industrial Art, which was exhibited at the Metropolitan Museum of Art and subsequently sent to tour other American member museums. The exhibit explained that it was not to stimulate demand for European products, nor to encourage copying of European creations, but to bring about an understanding of this modern movement in design in the hope that a parallel movement may be initiated in our country ... to bring us to new forms appropriate to the living conditions of the twentieth century.8 In Vogue magazine, patterns of textiles and paper goods were illustrated and described as "modern designs” or of “modernistic influence." Advertisements in national magazines began to adapt Art Deco design formats and motifs to give their ads and products a sense of modernity. Even ads for the most modest, utilitarian objects could be glorified when presented in a modern artistic format. Ready-to-wear fashions quickly adopted Art Deco elements such as straight-line forms, squared necklines, triangular and diagonal accents, and geometric patterns. Such styles soon appeared in every category of fashion accessories, particularly jewelry, compacts, handbags, hats and scarves. Younger generations today probably wonder why there was such a fuss about these European designs, when fashionably designed products are available today from all over the world. It is a bit hard to relate, I know. But depending on your age, you only need to remember what it was like in the 1950s, when home television sets became available, or in the 1980s, when personal computers were introduced, or in the 2000s when iPhones hit the market. The public is always excited when a new technology, previously available but only affordable by large institutions, finally becomes inexpensive enough and available to ordinary people, or when a fashion trend, such as short skirts in the 1960s, casual wear in the 1980s, or sport shoes in the 1990s, suddenly erupts everywhere in a short period of time. In the 1920s, the latest technology was, in fact, mechanical devices, such as new electric appliances or any new household decorative accessories, furniture, or bathroom and kitchen equipment. People simply wanted to buy the most modern-looking things, and at that time, “modern” was dramatically different from the past. Or as flappers would have said in the 1920s, “Modern design is swell! The bee's knees! The cat's meow!" (Translation: Cool! Awesome! Mind-blowing!) They were no different from young consumers today. While the new modern design styles were being promoted aggressively everywhere, there were additional incentives encouraging consumers to buy. Increasing efficiencies in manufacturing over the last ten years had enabled a reduction in production and labor costs, thus offering lower consumer prices. The vacuum cleaner that cost \$52 in 1918, in 1926 was priced at \$29.95, 49 percent lower (in 2013 dollars, that's a reduction from \$804 to \$394). So the new mass-produced products were much more affordable, for many more people. In addition, more jobs were available at higher salaries. The economy was better than it had ever been since the war. People were primed to buy, and they wanted the latest styles. Now it was up to the manufacturers, many of whom had neither the inclination nor the specialized skills to provide such new styles.
