The first of the so-called artless industries of mass production in the late 1920s to recognize the public desire for new design styles was in fact the automobile industry, in which 43 firms produced four million new cars each year. Travel by car was increasing rapidly with the opening of the Lincoln Highway, the first coast-to-coast roadway, connecting San Francisco and New York (now Route 40). In 1926, the Model T, which in 1923 had commanded 52 percent of the U.S. market but was virtually unchanged since its 1913 introduction, was now challenged by Chevrolet's new 1926 model with bold new styling and colors. General Motors (GM) head Alfred P. Sloan had initiated annual styling changes because he was convinced that new styling was the key to success. Chevy sales doubled, and Ford dropped by 25 percent. Within a year, Chevrolet would win 45 percent of the market, and by 1927 Ford's share would drop to 15 percent. Ford was forced to drop the Model T and produce its redesigned Model A in 1928, designed by Edsel Ford, Henry's son. Some manufacturers figured that if Henry Ford himself, the king of Fordism, needed styling, they had better follow suit. Now, in early 1927, about when Charles Lindbergh flew solo across the Atlantic, GM's Sloan charged Harley J. Earl (1893– 1969) with designing the 1927 LaSalle, a cheaper, productionmodel version of GM's luxury Cadillac. It was to be the first massproduced car to have its appearance completely styled from bumper to bumper by a single individual, and the first to use chrome plating on the trim. Earl used full-scale clay models to preplan every detail and blend all components into an integrated form. Earl had been born in Hollywood, California, had attended the University of Southern California in 1912 and studied engineering at Stanford University from 1914 to 1917. He began his career with the firm his father had founded in 1889 as the Earl ![](images/432f7528b4b6789458ff0eaaa98b0d1e179d8a0d11f031b175295f742385ad9b.jpg) Harley J. Earl, ca. 1930s (General Motors). Carriage Works, and after 1908 called the Earl Automobile Works, which built custom car bodies for film stars starting in 1911. Clients included Roscoe “Fatty" Arbuckle, Tom Mix, Mary Pickford, Cecille B. DeMille, and Al Jolson, among others. Earl designed the first complete car produced by the firm in 1918. That year, Don Lee, a West Coast Cadillac distributor, head of Don Lee Coach and Body Works, bought the Earl Company and kept Harley on as designer and general manager of its custom body shop in 1919. In 1921 Earl patented a design that suggested the wraparound windshield he later would pioneer. In 1926, the Fisher Brothers of General Motors, impressed with Earl's technique of using modeling clay to develop the forms of his designs, hired him as a consultant to Cadillac, and he relocated to Detroit. His first assignment was to design the LaSalle, a low-cost Cadillac. The 1927 LaSalle would become an instant success and would convince Sloan to establish the first internal corporate design department at GM, called the Art and Colour Section, and to name Earl as its first director. Within a year Earl would hire a staff of 50 automotive designers. In 1927, promotion of modern design to the public continued on many fronts. The Metropolitan Museum of Art held an exhibit of Swedish industrial arts, introducing Scandinavian design to the U.S. public for the first time. In May, Jane Heap (1887–1964), editor of a literary journal called The Little Review, organized a Machine Age Exposition in Steinway Hall in Midtown Manhattan. Inspired by Le Corbusier's Pavilion de L'Esprit Nouveau, and by Soviet Constructivism, Heap displayed actual machines: a Studebaker crankshaft, a Curtis airplane engine, and a Hyde Windlass propeller, along with photographs of skyscrapers, the Bauhaus in Dessau, Soviet industrial buildings, and architecture by Walter Gropius. E. B. White (1899– 1985) wrote in the New Yorker that the exposition demonstrated “that the machine is the tutelary symbol of the universal dynamism."9 Department stores, such as Macy's and Franklin Simon of New York, Wanamaker's in Philadelphia, and Marshall Field in Chicago, inspired by the success of Paris department store ensembles during the 1925 exposition, staged exhibitions in May 1927 featuring the new Art Deco designs. Typical was Macy's Art and Trade exhibition, which was attended by 40,000 people and was hyped aggressively by radio announcements and talks by notable participants. Consumer goods manufacturers were being advised to incorporate modern design into their products by advertising agencies. In the August 1927 issue of Atlantic Monthly, Ernest Elmo Calkins (1868–1964), head of the Calkins & Holden advertising agency, who was called by many the “Dean of Advertising Men," wrote an influential article, “Beauty, the New Business Tool," in which he stated the case for more attractive products: We passed from the hand to the machine, we enjoyed our era of triumph of the machine, we acquired wealth, and with wealth, education, travel, sophistication, a sense of beauty; and then we began to miss something in our cheap but ugly products. Efficiency was not enough. The machine did not satisfy the soul. Man could not live by bread alone. And thus it came about that beauty, or what one conceived as beauty, became a factor in the production and marketing of goods.10 ## Calkins continued to define beauty: The only art that can survive and grow is art that is related to our life and our needs, and that has a sound economic foundation. It is to be hoped that manufacturers in the search for design to beautify their products will start with a clear conception of what beauty is, especially beauty in an article of use ... the surest guide in divining new beauty in machine-made things is to grasp and interpret the beauty they naturally and intrinsically possess." In other words, beauty in useful products is not extraneous artistic decoration or embellishment, but the honest and simple visual expression of its natural function through form and character. Whether Calkins knew of the Bauhaus or not, he was expressing the exact same philosophy of design, but in business terms. Calkins asked his advertising colleagues, "How could ads work, if the styling of products did not express the qualities claimed for it?"12 Many manufacturers by this time were paying attention to their advertising agencies, which were already incorporating the modern look into their ads. In many cases, such manufacturers asked their agencies for help in designing products, requests that were honored by nameless artists on the agency's staff and given no credit. Other manufacturers sought such design assistance by advertising for employees or consultants. One can imagine what such a help wanted ad for an industrial designer might have looked like in 1927: Wanted: Artistically trained industrial artist to create new modern designs for products made by company X. Applicants need to be familiar with manufacturing processes such as casting, stamping, grinding, milling, welding, fastening, and assembling, and willing to work collaboratively under chief engineers and efficiency experts in actual factories. Designs must be able to be reproduced easily and inexpensively by machines, and must be attractive to consumers in the modern style. Applicants must provide examples of mass-produced products they have designed recently. Compensation for such work is to be negotiated. An advertisement like that would have attracted few applicants, because at that time, practically no one existed with such extensive qualifications that required both art and engineering training, and who would be willing to work in mass-production facilities. As we have seen, few art students would even consider such a position, even if they had been trained in engineering or manufacturing matters, and of course none were. As noted earlier, most graduates of art schools wanted nothing to do with industry. Engineers who were trained in manufacturing processes, and may have designed massproduced products, had absolutely no art education, nor any inclination to acquire it. The industrial art position described in the ad would have been, as some would say, “neither fish nor fowl," that is, neither true artist or true engineer. Traditional separation of art and industry had, at least in America, left industry without an essential, competitive, business tool. Jane Heap, who had organized the Machine Age Exposition in New York, wrote that an effective artist “must affiliate with the creative artist in other arts and with the constructive men of his epoch: engineers, scientists, etc."13 In 1927 there were a few professionals who recognized the increasing demand for industrial design, were trained in art and design, and responded enthusiastically to the call by manufacturers, knowing full well that this would require their willing collaboration with industry, engineers, and scientists. They were the farseeing individuals who almost single-handedly became pioneers in what was to become a brand new profession. They were interesting and unusual men and women. They were the machine designers. Here's where things began to get interesting. As the demand for modern design increased both by manufacturers and by the public, professional people in a number of art-related, advertising, or commercial fields responded with interest, or were sought out and targeted by industry. Some, of course, were already in the art industries, including furniture and lighting products. One of these was Donald Deskey (1894–1989), then 33, who had just opened an office, Deskey-Vollmer, in New York in 1927, with his partner, Phillip Vollmer, specializing in the design of furniture and lighting. Deskey was born in Blue Earth, Minnesota, and had studied architecture at the University of California, Berkeley, and painting at the California School of Design, the School of the Art Institute of Chicago, and the Art Students League of New York. After military service in World War I, he started working with advertising agencies in Chicago and New York, but soon left to study painting in Paris from 1920 to 1922, where he also married. He returned to the United States to begin a career in painting; to teach art at Juniata College in Huntington, Pennsylvania; and to exhibit his work in Pittsburgh. In 1925 he returned to France to attend the Paris exposition and visited the Bauhaus in Dessau, both of which greatly influenced his approach to design, and upon his return in 1926, he settled in New York to work for furniture designer Paul Frankl, mentioned earlier. He designed furniture and lamps for Frankl, but also designed an apartment interior for Adam Gimbel, the founder of Gimbel's department store, who had bought out rival Saks & Co. and opened an uptown branch called Saks Fifth Avenue in 1923. ![](images/18d30d7f42cc6b4a1fbe650500524e2baac22f2469e50482f7cc18bd2995d6b9.jpg) Meanwhile, in California, Kem Weber left the Barker Brothers store in 1927 to open an independent design studio in Los Angeles, but continued to work as a consultant for Barker, shaping its products well into the 1930s. His design of an armchair for the John Bissinger residence in San Francisco in 1928 was in the sleek and sophisticated modernism that would come to defne the Hollywood of the 1930s. In his new office, he sought out collaboration with manufacturers and explored new materials. Deskey; Kem Weber; set designer Lee Simonson (1888–1967); set designer, architect, and artist Frederick Kiesler (1890–1965); Paul Frankl; and a few other designers were justifiably concerned with the dishonest but common practice of manufacturers copying designs, a practice that museums historically encouraged by displaying traditional European designs as superior models of design quality. At the time, there was no reason to believe that the new innovative modern designs in America would not be subjected to the same practice. Now, as early as 1927, the new modern designers realized that, for the first time, American designs could be more innovative than European ones and felt that such borrowing, as Frankl put it, “degrades the producers and corrupts the taste of the public."14 They formed an organization called the American Union of Decorative Artists and Craftsmen (AUDAC), which idealistically aimed to “cooperate with manufacturers and the public in the placing of American arts and crafts on a basis of honesty, dignity, and merit." Another lighting designer was Walter von Nessen (1889–1943), then 38, who opened Nessen Studios in New York's Murray Hill section in 1927 with his wife, Greta von Nessen (c. 1900–1975), then 27, to design and fabricate architectural lighting. His designs, such as his innovative swing-arm lamps for table, floor, and wall applications, were simple, polished metal, modern, and highly functional in allowing the lamp to be easily pushed into a range of horizontal positions without moving the floor or desk lamp base. The Nessens also designed furniture and accessories for the Chase Brass and Copper Company, including a tubular steel chair. Von Nessen was born and trained in Germany, and his wife studied at the School for Industrial Arts in Stockholm, Sweden. They both emigrated to the United States in 1925. There was also Gilbert Rohde (1894–1944), then 33, who in 1927 traveled to France and Germany and was inspired by modernism there to design and produce custom furniture in a modern style. That same year, he opened his own office in New York, designing furniture and showrooms and consulting with manufacturers such as General Electric and the Hudson Motor Car Company. Rohde was born in New York and became familiar with cabinetmaking in his German father's shop. He studied at the Art Students League and the Grand Central School of Art, both in New York. His early work was as a drama and music critic, cartoonist, reporter, and furniture illustrator. In an environment where women led the fashion industry, it seemed appropriate that some of these women would be attracted to the design of modern products. One of these was Helen Dryden (1887–1981) who, inspired by the flurry of modern design activity after the Paris Art Deco Exposition of 1925, turned her career in costume design to industrial design in 1927 at about age 40. She designed tableware and lamps for the Revere Corporation and as art director with the Dura Company in Ohio, where she designed chrome decorative objects, textiles, and glassware and was soon acclaimed as “the highest-paid woman artist." ![](images/d19d6c5c4e5fce969d1868577b54e89555a7c23e80ffdbaeba23c29e993e3232.jpg) Gilbert Rohde, ca. 1940 (courtesy Herman Miller, Inc.). Dryden was born in Baltimore with unusual artistic ability, designing and selling clothes for paper dolls that appeared in newspaper fashion sections. She trained in landscape painting under Hugh Breckenridge and studied briefly at the Philadelphia Academy of Fine Arts before moving to New York to begin her career in fashion design illustration at Vogue magazine in 1909. From 1913 to 1922, she designed dramatic and outstanding Vogue covers, as well as covers for Charm, Harper's Bazaar, House & Garden, Vanity Fair, and Harper's Weekly. From 1914 well into the 1920s, she designed costumes for Broadway shows and fabrics for Stehli Silks and Aberfoyle Fabrics. In 1925, she lived at 9 East 10th Street in New York with her 25-year-old Philippine-born cook and butler, Ricardo Lampitok. ![](images/626c8e1db5e37c43d14d5955ba5302484fbd68e5cd93e5d6f74369518ba7a596.jpg) Walter Dorwin Teague, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine. Photograph by Lazernick). Other entrants to the new profession were in the advertising business. In 1927, Walter Dorwin Teague (1883–1960), then 44, was awarded a contract with Eastman Kodak to design cameras, starting on January 1, 1928. Teague immediately added "Industrial Design” to his firm's letterhead, since he was already a highly successful advertising artist, who had opened his own typographic studio in 1912 and by 1920 had been involved in commercial packaging. Born in Decatur, Indiana, as one of six siblings, Teague's grandfather had moved from North Carolina to Pendleton, Indiana, where one of America's largest Quaker communities existed. Teague's father became a circuit-riding Methodist minister and later a tailor in Pendleton. At age 16, while still in school, Teague worked as a handyman at the local newspaper, and later as a reporter. He was inspired to become an artist by books on architecture in his high school library. At age 19, he left Indiana for New York City, where he studied painting from 1903 to 1907 at the Art Students League of New York, an art school founded in 1875 for both amateurs and professionals that offered reasonably priced classes. Students could study part or full time, but there were no degree programs or grades awarded. The school is still operating today in the same manner. Teague earned money by checking hats at the YMCA in downtown Manhattan, and also by painting signs. His skill in lettering soon evolved into drawing illustrations for mail-order catalogs, such as for neckties and shoes. This led to more elaborate advertising illustrations, which caught the attention of Walter Whitehead, an advertising executive at the Ben Hampton Advertising Agency he had met at the YMCA, and who promptly hired him. When Whitehead left the Ben Hampton Agency in 1908 and joined the more prestigious New York agency of Calkins & Holden, Teague went with him. In his four years there, Teague became known for his distinctive frames, or borders, for advertising art, which blended Baroque and Renaissance influence with a unique simplicity. After a year of freelancing, Teague left Calkins & Holden in 1912 to work from his own typographic studio, creating graphic designs for magazine ads. In 1920, when he designed frames for the famous Arrow Collar ads, he was already recognized widely for what were known as “Teague borders. By the mid–1920s, the demand for border designs weakened, and Teague became involved with commercial packaging. He was intrigued by the Paris exposition of 1925, and in June 1926 he traveled to Europe to view Bauhaus work in Italy and was inspired by the work and writings ofLe Corbusier. Upon his return, he reestablished his office devoted to package design. In 1927, Richard F. Bach (1887–1968), curator of the Metropolitan Museum of Art, recommended Teague to Adolph Stuber, a top manager of the Rochester, New York-based Eastman Kodak Company, which was considering engaging an artist to design cameras. Teague took Stuber on a tour of the modern art shops in New York, including the contemporary shop of Rena Rosenthal, in order to show him the clean lines of home furnishings that were coming into style. Teague had no knowledge of cameras or how they were made, but he proposed working on-site with Kodak's engineers, claiming that "designing according to engineering necessities ultimately leads to greater beauty and heavier sales."16 Stuber apparently agreed. Teague's relationship with Eastman would result in dozens of camera designs over many years, and he would continue to consult with Eastman for the remainder of his life. Teague's first designs for Kodak would include its 1930 Art Deco gift camera and packaging, and its Beau Brownie, both of which featured a colorful geometric pattern of rectangles and squares reminiscent of Dutch artist Piet Mondrian (1872–1944) and De Stijl art. By February 1928, Teague was already promoting the new profession of industrial design by writing an article, "Modern Design Needs Modern Merchandising," in Forbes, a business magazine. He advised, “The designer who gets results for the manufacturer plans with all departments of a business before he ever lays à pencil to drawing board."17 That same year, 1928, manufacturers were trying to make design changes in their products. But rather than changing the form of their old products, which would require expensive tooling costs, it was much easier for them to change the color of their product. In 1930, Fortune magazine recalled that in the early 1920s, “kitchen and bathroom were a porcelain and surgical white. Furniture was in natural wood tints, surfaced with a colorless finish. The stove was black. Faucets were metallic." Now, in a typical home in 1928, in the cellar stood a Redflash Boiler, more crimson than the flames of the adjacent furnace. On the door of the orange refrigerator was perched a green parrot. Not only the dishes, but the pots and pans were decked out in bright gay tints and tones. A green-tiled bathroom was set off with green tub, green towels, and, monstrously enough, green toilet paper. For afternoon tea there were blue glasses and a yellow cloth. A yellow alarm clock—a cherry garbage can, a scarlet typewriter—surfaces had turned suddenly into palettes. Fortune concluded, "The acceptance of color was undoubtedly significant from the standpoint of old traditions being abandoned and old suppressions being released."18 The word was getting around the advertising world that designing products for industry was inancially lucrative. An early example of this was the ticket scale designed for International in 1929 by Joseph “Jo" Claude Sinel (1889–1975), a 40- year-old artist working for the Lennon Mitchell advertising agency in New York. Sinel in a later interview recalled, ![](images/139ea3d8bb5f4a0ef3dc4bf4607f751847a4ee1db93886500a4757befb496cfd.jpg) Joseph “Jo" Sinel, 1928 (courtesy California College of the Arts Libraries). One of the first designs I made was the International Ticket Scale, and I made a model from a Del Monte (wooden) fruit case.... I took the model in to show the executives of the company and they paid me a fee of \$10,000 [\$136,000 in 2013 dollars] and royalties for 25 years.19 This design is well documented and is in a style that many would call “skyscraper style," with vertical and graduated geometrical elements, typical of the period. Sinel led a most unconventional life for his era. In later times, he might have been called a wandering “hippie," but his earnings in commercial art demonstrated outstanding skill and diligence in his career. He was born in Auckland, New Zealand, where his father, Thomas, ran a stevedoring operation and sired ten children. Jo's primary school's headmaster recognized his talent for drawing and urged him to attend the Elam School of Art. From 1904 to 1909, he served an apprenticeship as a lithographic artist for the New Zealand Herald newspaper. He did freelance work for printing firms in Wellington and Christchurch, then gained valuable commercial art studio experience in Australia as well as working as a harvest hand and sheep shearer. Impulsively, he left for England and arrived in Liverpool in the middle of World War I, but he had no trouble landing a job at Hudson-Scotts, England's largest lithographer, in Carlisle. ![](images/2d45e101a7e9740358929c4b2bb7fb6cc753527ebe0555f3baa940287ac84754.jpg) 1929 International Ticket Scale by Sinel (Designscape magazine, February 1977). Next was Carlton Studios, England's most prominent art studio, and after that, the C. F. Hyme advertising agency in London. The war became increasingly dangerous to Londoners due to Zeppelin air raids, so Jo shipped out as a merchant seaman during wartime to avoid military conscription. After unloading guns off a British ship in Newport News, Virginia, he landed once again in Australia where he easily obtained a high-salary art job. When Australia passed a statute saying that men of military age could not leave the country after a certain date, Sinel decided it was time to leave. In 1918 he left for the United States and landed in San Francisco at the time of the influenza epidemic, when people on the street were wearing surgical masks. Again, he landed an art position at Foster & Kleiser advertising agency but soon quit his job and set out for the high Sierras to build a cabin with an artist friend on the shores of Lake Susie. When snow arrived, he headed back to San Francisco to find several job offers waiting. The one he chose was in New York with First National Pictures, a film company in which Charlie Chaplin and Douglas Fairbanks were associated. For First National, he designed promotional graphics for film distribution, but soon, driven by wanderlust and fascinated with automobiles, bought one in 1920 and drove across America, which was virtually unpaved at the time, to see its natural wonders and cities. He had advertisements for First National Pictures painted on the side of his car that attracted photographers at many cities, fuelingJo's natural flair for publicity. Returning to San Francisco, he worked simultaneously for Foster & Kleiser and the western branch of the McCann-Erikson advertising firm. When Jo refused to do a certain job at Foster & Kleiser, they fired him, but he and three others, including Harold von Schmidt, set up an independent business to design posters for various San Francisco advertising agencies, including Foster & Kleiser. In 1921 and 1922, Sinel taught design and lettering at the California School of Arts and Crafts (CCAC, but now, California College of Arts, CCA) under its founder, Frederick Meyer. Jo would continue his association with CCAC, off and on, until his death. But in 1922 he took a position as art director for C. C. Ronalds, who was starting a new advertising agency in Montreal, Canada. After five weeks there, Sinel went to New York to do freelance work with von Schmidt in a studio in Greenwich Village, where they were members of the Society of Illustrators and the Artists' Guild. Through these contacts, Sinel landed a job with the Lennon Mitchell advertising agency in 1923. In 1924, he published a book, A Book of American Trademarks and Devices. During this period, Sinel claimed, so he probably did, work on a number of product designs, as was not uncommon for advertising agencies of those days, but documented examples have not yet been found. After his International scale design in 1929, he would design many products for industry throughout the 1930s.20


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\03_Chapter_03.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `ced799a268514820eb0e87f868e3bf3953dd377fd06280a816a863ff1d508b8f`
- characters: 46131

# Modern Design Meets the Depression Another profession attracted to the new field of modern design was the creative theatrical and stage world. The theatrical set design profession was suffering from the increasing popularity of motion pictures, which in 1927 had just introduced sound in The Jazz Singer, starring Al Jolson. Films were drawing crowds away from Broadway, and many theaters that in the past had staged live performances were now converting to movie theaters. ![](images/1f4bc5ded9f06fc31e88feeea26e250047220eb99c35d45b962d6792832133ce.jpg) Norman Bel Geddes (1893–1958), a nationally recognized set designer, saw the potential for his talents in automotive design when urged by Ray Graham, of the Graham-Paige Motor Corporation in 1927. Then 34, Bel Geddes proceeded to design a series of five brass concept car models for Graham, each design representing a 20 percent increase in modernization over the previous one, demonstrating progressive annual model changes, each of which was not to be out of style for five years. Car No. 1 was designed in 1928, intended to be prototyped in fall of 1929, but it was never built due to the stock market crash and the onset of the Depression. Despite this setback, he was to become one of the most flamboyant and imaginative pioneers of the new profession. Norman Bel Geddes, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine. Photograph by Maurice Goldberg). Bel Geddes was born in Adrian, Michigan, as Norman Melancton Geddes. Raised in New Philadelphia, Ohio, he was a high school dropout and tried unsuccessfully to acquire formal art training at the Cleveland Institute of Art and the School of the Art Institute of Chicago. In 1913 he began work as advertising illustrator and art director for the Peninsular Engraving Company in Chicago, and then for the Barnes-Crosby Advertising Agency in Detroit. He also worked in 1916-1917 for Aline Barnsdall's Little Theatre in Los Angeles as a set designer, where he was attracted to the theater. In 1916, when he married Helen Belle Schneider, they incorporated their names to form the surname Bel Geddes. In 1918, he became scene designer for the Metropolitan Opera and designed and directed a number of very successful theatrical works, as well as an ice show starring worldwide skating star Sonja Henie. Bel Geddes's daughter, Barbara Bel Geddes, was born in 1922 and would become a well-known Broadway and film actress starting in 1947. The essence of Bel Geddes's theatrical set design career was to create environments and costumes to entertain and take people into magical worlds of imagination. In the mid–1920s, Bel Geddes, for his own amusement and that of his friends, began creating elaborate table games. In the previous ten years, a public fascination with scale models had been developing. Construction of model airplanes that actually flew became popular with young boys, and electric model railroads at family Christmastime evolved into hobbyists constructing elaborate and extensive basement layouts with realistic miniature trains, bridges, lakes, mountains, trees, people and buildings. The idea of realistic miniature replications of the real world apparently also fascinated Bel Geddes. He started with a five-by-three-foot football field, with players kicking, running, and tackling. That was followed by a mechanical baseball game and an indoor golf game, built with wooden blocks, wire screen, and paste-soaked newspaper. He added green velvet and “shrubbery." The game took over the dining room table and disrupted family life. In 1925, Bel Geddes was creating film set designs for D. W. Griffith and the Cecil B. DeMille film Feet of Clay. He also designed costumes for Max Reinhardt, the Austrian-born stage and film actor and director. During this period, his wife of ten years left him and took his two children. Depressed, Bel Geddes was taken to Belmont Stakes racetrack for distraction, and he was inspired by the pageantry there to invent a new game, which he called the Nutshell Jockey Club. It was a 20-inch-wide racetrack built on a 28-foot-long table. Running times were identical to real track times. There was green velvet turf, white fencing, bleachers, a judges' stand, and boxes for stable owners. The game was electrical and featured 800 cast-iron horses, each three inches long and realistically painted. Twenty horses lined up behind the electric starting gate and were pulled by invisible silk threads connected to unseen pulleys. A “chance machine" varied the speed of individual horses to simulate an actual race. The setup took over the whole basement at his Manhattan brownstone on East 37th Street that doubled as his office, and was estimated to have cost Bel Geddes \$4,000, plus the labor of his staff. Every Saturday evening, eight races were run, announced over an amplifier, with a recording of racetrack bedlam and the sound of horses' hooves to lend atmosphere. Race betting, sale of horses and stables, and odds were conducted through a racing secretary. It was a live horse race in every respect except the weather. Within two months, nearly 100 people owned stables in the Nutshell Jockey Club. The game was a magnet for visiting Hollywood notables, since racing in California would not be legalized until 1933. They included Charlie Chaplin; Douglas Fairbanks, Jr.; Leslie Howard; Ethyl Barrymore; Adolph Menjou; and Basil Rathbone. It was so successful that New York mayor Jimmy Walker, one of the game's enthusiasts, assigned a patrolman to keep “the wrong people” out.¹ Bel Geddes was already famous. After he opened his industrial design office in 1927, Bel Geddes left the theatrical field, except for creating sets for the 1935 Broadway production of Dead End. Despite offers by gambling syndicates to operate the Nutshell Jockey Club (one offered "the blond little Leonardo ... with habitually tousled hair" a thousand dollars per night to operate the Nutshell at Saratoga Springs, New York, for a season), Bel Geddes declined and put it in storage. Department stores continued to enthusiastically promote modern design. In 1928, the B. Altman department store in New York held an exhibition called Twentieth Century Taste. Lord and Taylor announced an international competition in design and trade symbols. Franklin Simon engaged none other than Norman Bel Geddes to develop a modern approach for window displays. His windows featured dramatically lit, very modern-looking, Cubistic pyramids and metallic mannequins. When revealed to the public, they created massive traffic jams and crowded sidewalks. Within 90 days, every store on Fifth Avenue had changed their windows to the new modern style. Also in 1928, Bel Geddes was commissioned by the Simmons Company to design steel beds that would go to market in 1932. Macy's staged a dramatic International Exposition of Art in Industry in 1928, organized by the new head of its department of design, Austin Purvis. The exposition included 15 rooms, most designed by well-known European designers, such as a powder room with products from the Wiener Werkstätte (Vienna Workshops) by Josef Hoffmann, director of the Austrian Academy of Fine Art; a dining room by French jewelry designer Maurice Dufrêne; a man's study and dining room by Bruno Paul, head of the German Vereinigte Staatsschulen für Freie und Angewandete Kunst (United State Schools for Fine and Applied Arts); and a butcher shop and living room by Italian designer Gio Ponti (1891–1979), who had just founded Domus (Home), an Italian architectural and design magazine that promoted the Wiener Werkstätte as well as the Italian Novocento (Nine Hundred) group to which Ponti belonged since its founding in 1926. European émigré designer Kem Weber, who had just designed a skyscraper side table/ vanity; architect William Lescaze (1896–1969); and architect Eugene Schoen (1880–1957) designed the American rooms, which included work by German silversmith émigré Peter Müller-Munk, who later would became a leading industrial designer. European design was still the main draw. From February 12 to March 24, 1928, Richard Bach, of the Metropolitan Museum of Art, decided to emulate the 1925 Paris exposition with French room ensembles in an exhibition, Contemporary American Design, the eleventh in the series, which was intended to conclude in March, but by then, 100,000 had attended and he extended the exhibition to September 2. The subtitle, The Architect and the Industrial Arts, revealed that Bach favored prominent American architects such as John W. Root, Ralph Walker, Raymond Hood, Ely Kahn, and European architects such as Eliel Saarinen, Joseph Urban, and Eugene Schoen, to lead the new profession of industrial design, just as architects had led in Europe, and that was in fact the very purpose of the exhibition. As Bach said in the catalog, [The Museum] has secured the enthusiastic cooperation of nine architects of distinction in this and other cities, who have made the exhibition practically their own by planning every detail of it themselves, from the general design of the gallery as a whole to the minutiae of their individual exhibits. Hence the appropriateness of the special title of the exhibition, "The Architect and the Industrial Arts," as it is a fine and instructive illustration of what the architect can do outside of the field which we commonly associate with his profession, and it was gratifying to us to learn that they welcomed the opportunity of showing this very thing.² American designers and AUDAC members were understandably appalled that they were ![](images/0e53fa05293282ed792c6240e5b8d111b28239bd17067d35cf8bc6f5faee6af8.jpg) excluded from these shows in favor of European and American architects, particularly in the industrial arts arena, where they had demonstrated their abilities in product design far more than architects had. In retaliation, they began planning their own show for the following year. Another designer, 36-year-old Raymond Loewy (1893– 1986), opened his New York office in 1929. His first product client was Sigmund Gestetner, head of the British Gestetner Company, for which he redesigned a Model 66 duplicator (mimeograph) machine from a hodgepodge of shapes into a smooth single form after building a clay model. Gestetner liked it so well he paid Loewy \$2,000. It would be introduced to the market in 1933, and the same design would be used for 15 years afterward. Raymond Fernand Loewy was born in Paris, and as a 15-year-old in 1908, he saw the Brazilian inventor Alberto Santos Dumont (1873-1932) fly his handmade airplane about a hundred yards over a polo field in the Bois de Boulogne in Paris. Inspired, Loewy designed a rubber-band-powered scale-model airplane and named it the Monoplan Ayrel. It won the James Gordon Bennett Cup for model airplanes. Bennett was the American editor of the Paris edition of the New York Herald and the Paris Herald. Teenage boys offered to buy Loewy's plane in such numbers that he patented it and formed a company, the Ayrel Corporation, to produce quantities in rented space. It was advertised and merchandized all over France, and it sold so well he had an offer to purchase his company, and promptly sold it so he could continue his studies.³ He graduated from Paris University in 1910 and from 1914 to 1918 served in the French Army as a captain in the Engineering Corps during World War I, where at the front he decorated his dugout with flowered wallpaper, draperies, and tufted pillows. During four years of war, during which he was burned by mustard gas, he rose from private to captain and received the French Legion of Honor. In 1918 he graduated from the Ecole de Lanneau in Paris. Loewy immigrated to America in 1919 to join his brother Georges, a doctor in Manhattan, with only \$40 in his pocket and still wearing his captain's uniform (the only clothing he had). His sketching on the ship so impressed Sir Harry Gloster Armstrong, then British consul general in New York, that he gave Loewy a note of introduction to the publisher Condé Nast, where he got his first job. He also worked as a fashion illustrator for Vogue magazine, Harper's Bazaar magazine, and Wanamaker's, Saks Fifth Avenue, and Bonwit Teller department stores. He designed costumes for Florenz Ziegfeld before establishing his office. Later, when asked his first impressions upon arriving in America, Loewy replied, To put it briefly, I was amazed at the chasm between the excellent quality of much American production and its gross appearance, clumsiness, bulk, and noise. Could this be the leading nation in the world, the America of my dreams? I could not imagine how such brilliant manufacturers, scientists, and businessmen could put up with it for so long.4 As mentioned earlier, the acclaimed "Dean of Advertising," Ernest Elmo Calkins, had been a vocal proponent of more stylish and attractive products for several years. Now he put his money where his mouth was. In 1929, he appointed 40-year-old Egmont Arens (1889–1966) as art director for Calkins & Holden, where Arens initiated and headed an internal industrial design department. Arens was born in Cleveland, Ohio, and at a young age was sent by his family to New Mexico to recover from tuberculosis. From 1911 to 1914 he attended the University of New Mexico, and from 1915 to 1916, the University of Chicago. He began his career as sports editor of the Albuquerque Tribune-Citizen in 1916, and from 1917 to 1923 operated his own Washington Square Bookstore on West Eighth Street in New York, where he established the Flying Stag Press, printing magazines with an upstairs press. His interest in the arts led to editing and printing Playboy magazine (no, not that one) in 1919, the first American magazine devoted to modern art, named after Irish playwright John Millington Synge's 1907 play, The Playboy of the Western World. In 1922, while still publishing and running the bookstore, Arens was art editor of Vanity Fair, one of the leading style and fashion magazines in the country, and later served as managing editor of Creative Arts magazine. A prolific writer, Arens's interest in modern design led him into products, because by 1927 he was designing and seeing to the manufacture of a line of lamps and other consumer products, which had caught the eye of Calkins & Holden. At an American Management Association (AMA) annual meeting in 1929, an officer of the KitchenAid Manufacturing Company of Dayton, Ohio, E.B. French, reported that Egmont Arens's 1928 redesign of his company's KitchenAid mixer (possibly KitchenAid Model G) had cut its weight in half and improved its appearance. As a result, sales had increased 100 percent. Another attendee reported that while a simple radio costs only \$50, “in an artistic cabinet it brings \$150." A representative of the L.C. Smith typewriter company said that his company introduced color in 1926 after years of black only, and soon only 2 percent of sales were black. Business leaders were beginning to accept the reality of modern design as something they needed to deal with. Earlier in 1929, the AMA had conducted seminars for businessmen on whether “intelligent and profitable use [can] be made of the modernistic style of design." Seminar titles included “How the Retailer Merchandizes Present-Day Fashion, Style, and Art"; "How the Manufacturer Copes with Fashion, Style, and Art”; and "The Renaissance of Art in American Business." At the AMA annual meeting cited in the paragraph above, keynote address speaker E. Grosvenor Plowman, advisor on merchandising problems to the Associated Industries of Massachusetts, stated that the modernistic style was the “offspring” of the Jazz Age, and that “the modern style will remain as long as the machine in its present form is the characteristic of world production, [changing] its outward form from year to year, just as women's dress styles change," and that manufacturers would have to learn to balance faddism and permanence.⁶ Plowman clearly thought that modern design was "faddish" and that "permanence" (the way products always looked) was quite acceptable. The date of this particular AMA annual meeting was coincidentally on October 29, 1929, the exact day of the stock market crash that initiated the Great Depression. Investors had been buying stocks on margin, that is, on credit, for years. Brokerage firms would lend \$9 for every \$1 an investor deposited, which vastly overpriced the stock market. When the market fell, brokers called in these loans, which could not be paid back, so many investors were wiped out. RCA stock, at \$549 per share in 1929, dropped to almost \$0 by 1932. Even before the stock market crashed, many businesses were making more products than they could sell and were overstocked. But now, many Americans drastically reduced their spending on consumer goods and services. Manufacturing and sales were soon down by 50 percent, and unemployment would rise to 25 percent. Many people lost their jobs, and among these was Helen Dryden, the "highest-paid woman artist." She held her art director job at the Dura Company until the stock market crash of 1929, when she was let go and replaced by George W. Walker (1896–1993), who had also just lost his job at Graham Paige. Walker was born in Chicago and attended the Cleveland School of Art and the Otis Art Institute, Los Angeles, in 1916. In 1920 he began his career as an advertising illustrator in a Los Angeles department store. He returned to Cleveland as a freelance illustrator and established his own firm, working for department stores and Premier Press. He entered the automotive business in 1928 by working for the Peerless Auto Company, and then for the Baker-Raulang body company, both in Cleveland. Amos Northup (1889– 1937), then chief designer for the Murray Corporation of America in Detroit, visited Baker-Raulang and urged Walker to come to Detroit, where he interviewed and worked briefly for Harley Earl and John Tiaarda at General Motors, and then for Graham Paige, working with Amos Northup until the company failed in 1929 after the crash. Now he was with Dura, which was also an automotive parts supplier. Northup, an automotive designer born in Bellevue, Ohio, had attended Cleveland Polytechnic Institute, where he studied design, and began his career as a furniture and interior designer. In 1918, he joined Pierce Arrow as the company's truck designer, but soon he was designing interior colors and trim for passenger cars. In 1921 he left Pierce Arrow and opened his own private studio, doing interiors for Pierce Arrow, Leon Rubay, and the 1924 Wills Sainte Claire. In 1927, he was hired by the Murray Corporation, the second-largest independent body producer, and shared in the design of the 1928 Hupmobile. In 1928, he would leave Murray to join Willys-Overland in Toledo, Ohio. Norman Bel Geddes was now concerned with other enterprises. In 1929, Bel Geddes was designing products and buildings for the Toledo Scale Company, a commission enabled by his uncle, Frederick Geddes, a director of the company and a trustee of the Toledo Museum of Art, within which Bel Geddes would design a theater stage. But because of the stock market crash, his product and factory designs for Toledo never saw fruition. One of Bel Geddes's former apprentices, also a successful set designer, ventured into the new profession. In 1929, 25-year-old Henry Dreyfuss (1904–1972) opened his own office at 580 Fifth Avenue, New York, for stage and industrial design activities. He had just won a competition for the design of a “Phone of the Future” sponsored by Bell Laboratories and would begin work on it in 1930, although it would not be produced until 1937. Dreyfuss was born in Brooklyn, New York, to a family in the theatrical materials supply business. He completed studies as an apprentice to Norman Bel Geddes in 1924 and produced 250 stage sets for a number of theaters before 1928, when, as we know, Bel Geddes closed his career in the theater and entered the industrial design field. Dreyfuss was now on his own. ![](images/f12e4d0a057b1efa02583ef7a7900c66343a7c1d4d6de84a73648c7cd1ac46e6.jpg) Over the year before the 1929 stock market crash, several art/design schools had initiated programs to educate engineers in art and design, at the request of major manufacturing corporations. In Toledo, Ohio, the Owens Bottle Glass Company convinced the Toledo Museum School of Design to initiate courses in industrial design for its engineering employees in 1928. Henry Dreyfuss, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine. New York Times/ Redux). In Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, that same year, Westinghouse Electric requested an evening course at Carnegie Institute of Technology (CIT) “in the fine art of design as applied to electrical machinery” for engineers, which began in 1929 and was called Design and Appearance. Wilfred Readio, head of CIT's Industrial Arts program, Department of Painting and Decoration, which taught commercial illustration, costume design, textile design, interior decoration, stage design, and printing, headed the course.7 There were three sections in the course, and Donald Dohner, who taught design, and Alexander Kostellow, who taught drawing, taught the other two. They were both on the faculty under Readio. Westinghouse in 1930 hired Donald Roscoe Dohner (1892–1943), one of the CIT instructors, to become director of art in the engineering department at Westinghouse, a position in which he controlled the appearance of all products manufactured in the company's 25 plants. Over the next four years, with a staff of eight, most recently graduated students from CIT, Dohner would design many Westinghouse products from locomotives to ashtrays, including meters, control panels, and switchboards. He worked with toolmakers, die makers, shop foremen, and superintendents. He studied markets, materials, and manufacturing operations and made a point of working in close contact with engineers, not only on appearance and sales appeal, but on cost, ease of manufacture, convenience, and economy of maintenance. Dohner had grown up in Indiana and starting in about 1912 studied at the John Herron Art Institute, Indianapolis; the Academy of Fine Arts in Chicago; and the Chicago Art Institute. He devoted two years to work in advertising, one year in merchandising, and in 1916 he studied the sales influence of product design. In 1918 he went to Pittsburgh to study set design at the Carnegie Institute of Technology (CIT), but while waiting for classes to begin, he took a job as industrial arts instructor in the Pittsburgh public school system. After his graduation from CIT in set design, in about 1925 he began teaching at CIT as an associate professor of design. Now, at age 38, he became one of the first corporate heads of U.S. industrial design departments. Robert Lepper, a 1927 graduate of CIT's industrial arts program in painting, had just returned from Europe and was hired as an associate professor to teach Dohner's night course. More on him later. ![](images/cf0f379b7c6000ae3e02eb2e00685e627a1c31d204fdb96370bb957b1da2b8df.jpg) Another major manufacturer, the Owens-Illinois Glass Company, in 1930 hired Edwin W. Fuerst (1903–1988) as a designer and stylist of its Toledo design studio, which specialized in glass containers and proprietary bottles. Born in Ottawa, Ohio, Fuerst left high school during his junior year after the death of his father and worked at the Detroit Steel Range Company. He Donald R. Dohner, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine. Photograph by Bachrach). then worked at Willys-Overland Motors, Inc., for a short time and returned to Detroit Steel Range, where he learned tool and die designing while working as a night superintendent and paymaster. While there, in 1927, he started attending classes at the Toledo Museum School of Design, mentioned earlier, until 1932. But in 1929, at age 26, he established his own design office in Toledo. One of his first clients was Owens-Illinois, who hired him as designer and stylist of its design studio. Other manufacturers were beginning to see the light. In 1930, Gustav Jensen (1898–1954) was hired by the International Nickel Company to design a new sink using Monel metal (a copper-nickel alloy) to popularize this new material. He also designed a telephone for AT&T (as one of ten entries in an AT&T “Telephone of the Future” competition won by Henry Dreyfuss) ![](images/5ede3e76ce38352b9a92dcce8af705ac76b8797b2d5046699a03d433f6b8a8b2.jpg) ![](images/4fd6bfee66e2c67be79def43006bd731e5e0f77af34bfb9e852db3c25ba10df4.jpg) Radio by Jensen (Pencil Points magazine, 1937). and a water heater for L.O. Koven & Brothers. Jensen was born in Copenhagen, Denmark, where his father was a banker and lawyer, and his mother came from a long line of ministers. He studied philosophy at GustavJensen, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine. Photograph by Ruth Bernhard). the University of Copenhagen but wanted to become an opera singer. Nevertheless, he developed an interest in architecture, which led him to art and typography. He emigrated to the United States in 1918, settling in New York City at the age of 20. He soon had clients including Colophon Quarterly, Covici-Friede, United Drug Company, and DuPont, for whom he designed book jackets, bindings, and cosmetics. He had begun work in industrial design in 1928 and was soon designing letters, borders, perfume bottles, cosmetic boxes, telephones, radios, silverware, toasters, and, as noted above, kitchen sinks. He called himself a “designer to industry” and would be productive up to World War II. Forty-two-year-old famed sculptor Alfonso Iannelli (1898–1965) in 1930 turned to industrial design by beginning to work as a designer for Sunbeam Appliance Company. He designed logos and appliances with modernist shapes, including a Cubist style, chrome-plated faucet for the Mueller Mixing Valve Company. Born in Italy to a poor family, he apprenticed with a decorator before immigrating to America with his family in 1898. He studied at the Art Students League in New York and in 1900 moved to California to work in architecture, architectural decoration, and stained glass. From 1910 to 1915 he designed hundreds of posters and show cards for the Orpheum Theater Chain. In 1914 he came to Chicago to design sculptural ornamental work for Midway Gardens, a huge entertainment center on Lake Michigan, designed by Frank Lloyd Wright, but Wright claimed all of Iannelli's work as his own. Iannelli refused to ever work for Wright again. In 1915, he established Iannelli Studios and began teaching at the Art Institute of Chicago, where in 1922 he was honored with a one-man exhibition of his work, which included modernist lamps and furnishings. In 1930, Nathan George Horwitt (1898–1990) designed the Beta chair, using steel, chrome-plated tubing, for the Howell Manufacturing Company. Horwitt was born in Russia and as a child immigrated to the United States, where he attended the City College of New York and New York University. He later attended the Art Students League of New York and served in the U.S. Army during World War I. He was hired as an advertising copywriter and was eventually promoted to director of advertising. He established the firm of Design Engineers in Manhattan in the late 1920s, and after that he developed patents to be sold to manufacturers. ![](images/4569dc2342b50a86912786fd795496ac02a0feaffc904fd2a877206750a96391.jpg) Norman Bel Geddes was becoming interested in the future. He wrote an editorial in 1930 entitled “Ten Years from Now” for the Ladies' Home Journal, predicting 50 industrial innovations, including the following: Nathan George Horwitt, ca. 1960 (courtesy Movado Group). Aluminum will replace steel in railroad car construction ... synthetic materials will replace products of nature in structural materials .. synthetic materials will replace wool, cotton, and silk in textiles .. aircraft will attain a speed of six miles per minute [360 mph] ... a net of airlines will encircle the world ... [and most prophetically] artists will be thinking in terms of industrial prob. lems of their age.8 His vision of the future would within two years inspire him to predict and design fantastic futuristic trains, planes, and ships in a trendsetting book. Bel Geddes was actively promoting his work during 1931–32 with similar magazine articles and mounting dozens of exhibitions of his work across the country and abroad, including Toledo, New York, and Vienna. Designers were beginning to work together to promote their work. Members of the American Union of Decorative Artists and Craftsmen (AUDAC), formed in 1927 but ignored by museums and department stores in 1929, quickly reorganized and finally got a chance to display what they could do. They mounted their own exhibition in early 1930 at the Grand Central Galleries in New York to counter museum promotions of European designs. The exhibition featured five modern interiors filled with American products already on the market to demonstrate that human comfort, practicality, and utility alone were appropriate American design criteria, rather than the typical European artistic designs favored by museums. Well over 120 exhibitors were represented, including 27 industrial designers. The October 1932 issue of Popular Mechanics reviewed the exhibition and reported that the exhibition was "proof that art and the machine have formed a partnership” and that “the best manufacturing enterprises ... were seeking counsel from the small group of men who are equipped to design beauty into the products of factory machines." Among the many exhibits of utilitarian objects were Gustav Jensen's Monel metal sink, Walter Dorwin Teague's scale model of his Marmon car design, Jo Sinel's International ticket scale, and Russel Wright's cocktail shaker. Wright also designed an elegant set of sterling silver flatware with simple, geometric shapes that was totally modern and would much later be reproduced by the Museum of Modern Art. Wright's designs were so successful that he began a business, Russel Wright Incorporated, with his wife, Mary, designing and producing spun aluminum accessories for the home. Russel Wright (1904–1976) was born into a historic old American family in Lebanon, Ohio. His mother had direct lineage with two signers of the Declaration of Independence, and his father and grandfather were local judges. Wright trained in art under Frank Duveneck, American figure and portrait painter, at the Art Academy of Cincinnati while still in high school. In 1921 and 1924, he attended the Art Students League in New York and the Columbia University of Architecture. Despite his interest in art, he dutifully followed his family tradition by studying for a legal career at Princeton University. While there, he was a member of the Princeton Triangle Club and won several Tiffany & Company prizes for outstanding World War I memorial sculptures. When he left Princeton, he abandoned law as a career and immediately became a theatrical set designer for Norman Bel Geddes in New York, and later for other film and theater directors. In 1928, however, he had started his own design firm, making theatrical props and small decorative cast metal objects. In 1931, AUDAC would publish its Annual of American Design to promote its members and to record its exhibition of 1930 with 176 pages, 300 photos of products, and essays about design. Frank Lloyd Wright (1857–1959) wrote about the "Principles of Design"; historian Lewis Mumford (1895–1990) wrote about “Culture and Machine Art"; Norman Bel Geddes wrote about “Business Interiors"; Kem Weber described the “Modern Shop"; Paul Frankl wrote about the “Home of Yesterday, Today, and Tomorrow"; and Richard Bach, of the Metropolitan Museum of Art, wrote about the "Resurgence of Quality," among other contributors. Sections of the book included "The Shop," "The Home," “Textiles," "New Materials," "Industrial Design," "Architecture Halls, Walls, and Fittings,"“"Graphic Arts in Advertising," and “Commercial Photography." Bel Geddes praised the successful collaboration between art and industry. The publication, a 9/ × 12/ inch hardcover book, was a masterpiece of Art Deco design and probably the earliest documentation of American industrial design, as it entered the public scene. Soon there would be much more visibility for industrial designers, with the help of museums, business magazines, and national merchandizing organizations. From May 2 through July 1, 1931, the Brooklyn Museum held an exhibition of Modern Decoration and Design by members of the American Union of Decorative Artists and Craftsmen (AUDAC), the organization's first large general museum exhibition, which was billed by the museum as "about the first opportunity the public has had to see a representative indication of the trend and importance of modern design among American craftsmen." Well-known exhibitors included Frank Lloyd Wright, Rockwell Kent, Lee Simonson, Joseph Urban, Paul T. Frankl, Egmont Arens, Donald Deskey, Helen Dryden, Norman Bel Geddes, Nathan George Horwitt, Gustav Jensen, Walter Dorwin Teague, Walter von Nessen, and Russel Wright, among others. Kem Weber, of Hollywood, California, designed a suite of rooms for the investor of moderate means."10 By 1931, several additional major organizations were initiating departments of industrial design. One was Montgomery Ward in Chicago, the mail-order giant, which hired 31-yearold Anne Swainson (1900–1955), a Swedish émigré and fashion coordinator, as head of its new Bureau of Design, which was described as “a service to buyers in evaluating the quality and appearance of products that Montgomery Ward ordered from thousands of outside suppliers." Swainson was educated in applied arts at Columbia University and had been working as a “fashion coordinator" at Chase Copper & Brass. The Bureau concept was her invention." She and her staff redesigned Ward's catalog, replacing old-fashioned woodcuts with photography, and insisting on the authority to approve or reject all projects. She was the first woman executive at Montgomery Ward and staffed her department with arts graduates from the Armour Institute ofTechnology (later to become the Illinois Institute of Technology, IIT) and the Art Institute of Chicago. Among them was George A. Beck (1908–1977), a recent graduate of the Chicago Academy of Fine Arts. Over the years, the Bureau would employ dozens of designers who would later become prominent in the field. With her staff, Swainson assisted in merchandising, product, or packaging design for many of the companies that produced soft or hard goods to be listed in the catalogs, as many often had no internal design capability. Products repackaged or designed by the Bureau of Design were adorned with the “MW” (for Montgomery Ward) logo. Another major manufacturing firm, the American Radiator & Standard Sanitary Corporation, the Kohler of its time, which produced bathroom and kitchen fixtures in porcelain enamel, as well as heating equipment, engaged a 34-year-old designer from one of the art industries, glassware. George Sakier (1897–1988) began in 1931 as a consultant and later became head of the company's Bureau of Design Development. ![](images/38fdd08971347b61162405378fff6a91ca430744c282bee3c4caa89372e9cfcd.jpg) Sakier was a man with multidisciplinary talents. Born in the United States, he studied at the Engineering School of Columbia University and at Pratt Institute in Brooklyn. At age 19, in 1916, he composed the text Machine Design and Descriptive Geometry. George Sakier, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine). During World War I, he worked as a camouflage technician, and after the war he continued to work as an artist, combining technology with classicism. In the 1920s, he worked in art direction for Vogue, Harper's Bazaar, and Modes and Manners. As a contributor to European art periodicals, he stimulated the rescue of a Mayan collection of artifacts from the cellar of the Palais du Trocadéro, a palace built in 1867 for the World's Fair in Paris, proving to France that America had an ancient art. Sakier's fine art paintings were shown in the Julien Levy ultramodern gallery in New York, established in 1931, which also displayed the work of Pablo Picasso, Max Ernst, Marcel Duchamp, and Salvadore Dali. From 1929 until 1979, Sakier worked as primary consultant for the Fostoria Glass Company of Moundsville, West Virginia, and designed the company's Art Deco designs, including black Art Deco vases No. 2404 and No. 2428 in 1930. Other companies were engaging design consultants to ensure that their products were of the latest modern design. The Toledo Scale Company in Toledo, Ohio, in December 1931 engaged a firm to design new public scales in various colors using a new plastic material, Plaskon, to complement different store interiors. The new design firm formed that year was the partnership of Harold Van Doren and John Gordon Rideout, forming the firm Van Doren and Rideout. Toledo Scale was their first client, and their first assignment was for a line of public health “person weigher” scales in colorful, Art Deco, skyscraper styles. Harold Livingston Van Doren (1895–1957) was born in Chicago, finished high school in New Jersey, and graduated from Williams College in 1917. After serving in the Army during World War I, he studied at the Art Students League in New York from 1920 to 1921, and went ![](images/c3ae1e20dbceecda1a77c115f6ce912ba08589a72db08cf1c60f0fe31e60e06b.jpg) Harold Van Doren, ca. 1934 (February 1934 Fortune magazine. Photograph by Souren). to Paris from 1922 to 1924 with a fellowship in art history. He worked as a lecturer at the Louvre in Paris, as an artist for the Chicago Tribune's Paris edition, as an actor in a Jean Renoir film, and as a translator of books on French artists Cézanne and Renoir. He would have had an opportunity to attend the 1925 Art Deco exhibition in Paris. In 1927, he became assistant director of the Minneapolis Institute of Arts while serving as an art director for Minneapolis newspapers and writing articles for the Saturday Evening Post and Encyclopedia Britannica. In 1930, his college classmate H.D. Bennett recruited him to Toledo as a color consultant for Bennett's new enterprise in the development of synthetic materials. Van Doren developed commercial applications for Plaskon, a plastic molding compound, and formed his design partnership with John Gordon Rideout (1898–1951), who was born in St. Paul, Minnesota. Rideout entered the University of Washington as an architecture student, but World War I interrupted his study and he served in the Navy in 1917. In the 1920s he worked as a graphic designer in Chicago and was one of the founders of the Chicago Society of Typographic Arts. In 1931 he came to Toledo to partner with Van Doren in industrial design. With the Depression deepening and more manufacturers seeking modern design to boost sales, more and more design- ![](images/713f5d3c90e24f041237bdc2749ef208621e86b84199ef5e3fed0fe153261324.jpg) John M. Little, ca. 1949 (courtesy Linda Braming). ers were getting into this new profession of industrial design. John McLeod Little (1906–1996) opened an industrial design firm, J.M. Little and Associates, in Minneapolis in 1931. Born in Minneapolis, he studied at the Chicago Academy of Fine Arts, the Art League of Minneapolis, and the Minneapolis School of Art from 1927 to 1929, after which he worked for the Batten, Barton, Durstine & ![](images/9ad9bd810cdc47111026e034aeff883fab0c3bc85cf1d8c36c0b6ef8483fec65.jpg) John Gordon Rideout, 1937, on his wedding day (courtesy Alyce Gordon Rideout). Osborn (BBDO) advertising agency. While managing his office in the 1930s, he accepted professorships in the field of design at the University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, and later at the University of California at Berkeley. Museums were still promoting modern design. The Twelfth Exhibition of Contemporary American Industrial Art was held at the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York, October 12 to November 22, 1931. Industrial designers among the exhibitors of furniture included Norman Bel Geddes, Donald Deskey, Paul T. Frankl, Walter von Nessen, Gilbert Rohde, Joseph Sinel, and Walter Dorwin Teague; exhibitors of metalwork and lighting fixtures included Donald Deskey, Henry Dreyfuss, Paul T. Frankl, and Walter von Nessen; and an exhibitor of silverware was Peter Müller-Munk.12 The public was increasingly aware of modern design through popular motion pictures, which almost inevitably were filmed in sets that were completely modern in Art Deco décor, costumes, furnishings, and accessories. While most people suffered economically because of the Depression and lived quite frugally, they seemed to love escaping to theaters to see their favorite stars in modern, luxurious and extravagant settings. With all the bad economic news described in the Depression, it is easy to assume that many did not have enough money to spend on consumer goods. But the most prominent advertiser of the time disputed that. In 1932, Elmo Calkins of the leading advertising agency, Calkins & Holden, wrote an introduction to a book, What Consumer Engineering Really Is (an unfortunate title for what would have been better called industrial design), written by Egmont Arens and Roy Shelton of Calkins' industrial styling division. Analyzing the Depression, Calkins wrote, Many stopped buying while still able financially to continue, and many are still able but restrained by fear and misplaced thrift. The sudden cessation of buying slowed up the entire industrial machine. Retail storekeepers curtailed orders to factories. Factories cut down production, reduced wages, laid off men, still further reducing the number of customers for goods. In a comparatively short time, with all the resources of the country still intact, we had Depression. There were no longer enough buyers for the large quantities of goods we had learned to make and distribute so abundantly.ⁿ3 Not only did Calkins correctly analyze the problem, but he had a theory, “obsoletism," for solving it: Obsoletism is another device for stimulating consumption. The element of style is a consideration in buying many things. Clothes go out of style and are replaced long before they are worn out. That principle extends to other products—motorcars, bathrooms, radios, foods, refrigerators, furniture. People are persuaded to abandon the old and buy the new to be up-to-date, to have the right and correct thing. Does there seem to be a sad waste in the process? Not at all. Wearing things out does not increase prosperity, but buying things does. Thrift in the industrial society in which we now live consists of keeping all the factories busy. Any plan that increases consumption of goods is justifiable if we believe that prosperity is a desirable thing. If we do not, we can turn back the page to earlier and more primitive times when people got along with little and made everything last as long as possible. We have built up a complicated industrial machine and we must go on with it, or throw everything in reverse and go backward.14 Calkins expressed what all industrial designers sensed instinctively but many manufacturers and businessmen of the time did not. Although “planned obsolescence” would acquire a negative connotation 25 years later (Hidden Persuaders, by Vance Packard, 1957), nevertheless it was precisely what was needed to turn around the Depression, and is still a basic source of our material prosperity and economic health. Calkins could have added that by providing a "previously owned” market for goods, many lower economic classes were able to own products at far less cost than new ones, thus sharing in an ever-expanding standard of living. The Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) had been founded in 1929 to promulgate and become an arbiter of modern art. Its first exhibit that year featured the “Big Four" of Postimpressionism: Cézanne, Gauguin, Seurat, and Van Gogh. In February and March, 1932, MoMA held its first architectural exhibition, The International Exhibition of Modern Architecture, and published a book, The International Style: Architecture Since 1922. It was MoMA's intention to establish architecture as a form of modern art to rival painting, and as the intellectual origin of international modern design. Organizers of the exhibition were Alfred H. Barr, Jr. (1902- 1981), the founder and head of the museum; Henry Russell Hitchcock (1903–1987), an architectural historian; and the wealthy connoisseur/collector/architect Philip Johnson (1906- 2005). Hitchcock and Johnson were fascinated with European modernists such as Bauhaus architects Walter Gropius and Mies van der Rohe, as well as architects Le Corbusier and J.J.P. Oud, all of whom Hitchcock had called the “New Pioneers” in his 1929 book, Modern Architecture. Other architects represented included George Howe, Richard Neutra, Raymond Hood, and Rudolph Schindler. This new style featured spare, rectangular, steel and glass structures. The term “International Style" became the formal name of modern architecture and would dominate the architectural scene for the next 30 years. Interestingly, Frank Lloyd Wright (1867–1959), America's greatest architect, was invited to participate but declined because he had misgivings about this new style, which he detested because it was counter to his own “organic" style of architecture. In fact, Wright had inspired many of the lauded European architects of the so-called International Style, including Gropius, Theo van Doesburg, Le Cobusier, and Richard Neutra, with his Wasmuth Portfolio published in Europe in 1910. Now, in 1932, Wright was regarded by many U.S. architects as an old-school, irascible, has-been. Philip Johnson had in fact disparagingly referred to Wright as “America's greatest 19th-century architect."15 The architect that Wright had inspired in Germany, Walter Gropius, was now, however, having difficult problems with the increasing power of Adolf Hitler.


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\04_Chapter_04.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `75622d788d79e681e6736fa40e014b6ed2dd3cd74393d73d07f21b853eed4311`
- characters: 44965

# Streamlining and the Chicago Fair The German Bauhaus, much admired by MoMA, was in serious trouble in Dessau, Germany. Faced with political and financial pressure, Gropius and Moholy-Nagy had resigned from the Bauhaus in 1928 and went to England, while architect Hannes Meyer, a committed Marxist who attempted to link the school to a vision of social and political transformation, assumed leadership. Some students were communists, others Nazis, both escalating political tensions. Architect Ludwig Mies van der Rohe (1886–1969) had become director of the Bauhaus in 1930, replacing Meyer, who had been forced by Nazis to resign at the same time that communist students were expelled. In the month before the MoMA exhibition, the Dessau city council, pressured by the powerful National Socialist (Nazi) German Workers Party under Adolph Hitler, who was not yet in full power, dissolved the Bauhaus in that city, due to a “Jewish-Marxist art manifestation," a travesty that may have inspired MoMA to elevate the legacy of the Bauhaus to near martyrdom. As a last-ditch effort to save the school, Mies van der Rohe, the last director of the Bauhaus, would move the school to Berlin, but within a year, Hitler would close the school in April 1933, and its faculty and students would scatter around the world, many coming to the United States later in the decade. There were other similar schools in Germany that were much larger in scope and number of students than the Bauhaus, such as the Industrial Art School in Berlin, with distinguished graduates such as Bruno Paul, Adolph Meyer, Kem Weber, and Mies van der Rohe himself, but such schools were not shut down by Hitler and thus were essentially forgotten in the United States. In July 1932, Belle Kogan (1902–2000) opened her own design office, Belle Kogan Associates, at 185 Madison Avenue in New York City with a retainer from the Quaker Silver Company. The company had hired her in 1929 and sponsored her training in glass and silver manufacturing. Kogan was born in Ilyashevka, Russia, and had emigrated to the United States in 1906 with her parents, who settled in Allentown, Pennsylvania. She was the first of eight children, and she showed interest in art at an early age. Her Bethlehem High School art teacher encouraged her to study mechanical drawing, where she was the only girl in the class. After graduation, she chose to attend the Pratt Institute in Brooklyn while teaching her high school class in mechanical drawing. But in 1920, she was forced to leave during her first semester at Pratt to manage her father's jewelry store. During this period, she attended the Art Students League of New York as time permitted. After Quaker hired her, the company sent her to train as a silver designer at the Rhode Island School of Design, and in 1930 at the German Art School in Pforzheim, Germany. The first female industrial designer was now in business for herself. Other industrial designers were already making names for themselves. Both Raymond Loewy and Henry Dreyfuss appeared on major magazine covers. Dreyfuss was interviewed by Popular Mechanics magazine and pictured in the October issue working on his Toperator washing machine. In it, 28-year-old Dreyfuss described the process followed by industrial designers as more than simply adding beauty, but requiring research and incorporating practical considerations of cost, function, and convenience. Gilbert Rohde had designed furniture for the Heywood Wakefield Company, including a side chair in 1931, 250,000 of which would be sold over the next eight years. He had established a relationship with D.J. DePree, president of the Herman Miller Furniture Company, in 1929. Rohde convinced DePree that the company should manufacture modern furniture. In 1932, DePree appointed him as director of design to shift the company's orientation from traditional to modern furniture, and instituted the policy of putting Rohde's name on each product. Rohde's designs of 1933 and 1934, including the clock he designed in 1933 for the Herman Miller Clock Company, would be equally strikingly modern, spare, and simple as many later designs of the 1950s. A Design for Machine exhibit held in Philadelphia in 1932 featured furniture by Gilbert Rohde and designs by Russel Wright in a breakfast room that included lamps, tables, flatware, and accessories in aluminum, a material that had been used previously only in cookware. Since 1930, Wright and his wife had been designing and making decorative aluminum pieces in attractive, modern styles, some of which were cooking utensils attractive enough to be carried directly from the stove to the serving table. Wright had also designed an early table radio model along with his aluminum housewares. More manufacturers were seeking designers. Jo Sinel in 1932 designed an Accousticon hearing aid for Dictaphone, a Radio-Victrola for RCA, Toastmaster tray accessories, and a Folmer-Graflex camera. Joseph B. Platt (1895–1968) in 1932 was engaged by the Parker Pen Company to design its Vacumatic fountain pen and its famous arrow-shaped ![](images/1ce8b67d84542c856feb02ecfc172bd1d0b1ad80f9bedfa785e3a8adc0c1cd4f.jpg) Parker Pen clip by Platt, 1932 (U.S. Patent Office, D88,821). pocket clip, which became a Parker trademark on all its pens to this day. Platt in 1919 had married June Evans (1898–1977), the daughter of Rudolph Evans, the sculptor who would create the 1,000-pound bronze statue of Thomas Jefferson that would be added to the Jefferson Memorial in Washington in 1947. In the 1920s, Platt and his new wife had relocated to the French commune of Senlis, an expatriate American community north of Paris, where Platt worked as a correspondent, editor, and illustrator for Vanity Fair, Vogue, and House & Garden magazines. He illustrated magazine covers from about 1923 to 1925, and probably attended the Paris Art Deco Exposition. He and his wife hoped to become community leaders at Senlis, but this did not work out. Returning to the United States in about 1929, the Platts lived in New York City and in Little Compton, Rhode Island, a beach resort. Their apartment in New York was in the same building as that of Raymond Loewy and his new wife, Jean Thompson, and the couples became good friends. Loewy was probably responsible for getting Platt the job at Parker Pen. Platt's wife, June, was also, according to Loewy, “an incomparable and creative cook," and starting in 1936 with June Platt's Party Cookbook, she used Loewy's name as an endorsement on the book jacket. She would write a number of popular cookbooks well into the 1950s, including the June Platt Cook Book in 1958. During the Depression, Platt and his wife collaborated in painting murals, probably WPA projects, one of which wrapped the dining room of the Country Club of Detroit in Grosse Pointe Farms, Michigan. In 1932, another major manufacturer, the B.F. Goodrich Company in Akron, Ohio, engaged H. Creston Doner (1902–1991), who had just opened his design office in Akron, Ohio, to design the company's rubber products. Doner was born in Nevada, Ohio, and was raised on farms in Nebraska and Pennsylvania. After high school he installed radios and worked on oil wells in Oklahoma.
