# Industrial Design Unleashed By 1933, eight years after the Paris Art Deco Exposition of 1925, which had sounded a wake-up call, warning that America was far behind Europe in modern design and therefore in international economic competition, there were perhaps three dozen industrial designers who were working in the so-called artless industries, a drop in the bucket compared to the 50,000 needed in the post–World War I consumer markets, as recommended in 1918 by Walter Sargent (1868–1927), then director of the Department of Fine and Industrial Art at the University of Chicago. Of course, World War I had dramatically suppressed the European advance in industrialization, and the Depression had stopped America's economy in its tracks. At the bottom of the Depression in 1933, industry was stalled. Many manufacturers still resisted investment in new tooling for new designs because sales had declined dramatically, and they were in the red, struggling to stay in business. Many were bewildered by a situation foreign to their experience—a situation in which the functional but homely merits of their products were not enough to sell them. Those who sought qualified designers to design ņew products found that the few industrial designers that were available were too busy to take on new clients. But the vast majority of manufacturers, businessmen, and engineers were simply unaware of modern design or industrial design. They were not among the elite sophisticates who frequented art museums and international art expositions, or who read art magazines. They were practical people, concerned about the nuts and bolts of manufacturing, not art. Many of them were about to learn about industrial design and its leading practitioners for the first time, through an influential article that reached the entire business world. The success of the Chicago Fair in 1933 was the first glimmer of hope for the future experienced by the public in three years. Now industry needed a morale boost, and it came in February 1934 when Fortune, the premier magazine of business, published a nine-page feature article about industrial design, which would have a profound effect on the new field, as well as on manufacturing and the economy itself. In fact, it was the first time the term “industrial design” was used in the popular press, rather than the traditional, more common term of “industrial art” for the practice of art in industry. The article was titled “Both Fish and Fowl," intended to suggest that industrial designers were both artists and businessmen, a connection that most people, including businessmen, were unlikely to make. Artists, many people thought, lived alone in garrets, struggling to eke out a living, and selling their paintings on the street. They were rarely, if ever, thought of as businessmen. A cartoon accompanied the article on the title page, which showed a company president addressing his board of directors with the statement, "Gentlemen—I am convinced that our next new biscuit must be styled by Norman Bel Geddes." ## 5. Industrial Design Unleashed The Fortune article was written anonymously, but is now known to have been authored by architect George Nelson (1908–1986), 26 at the time, and the winner of the 1932 Rome Prize in architecture. Nelson was then the assistant editor of Architectural Forum magazine, and would within a decade become a nationally known industrial designer. Nelson was born in Hartford, Connecticut, and graduated from Hartford Public High School in 1924, and from Yale in 1928 with a degree in architecture. In 1929 he was hired as a teaching assistant while obtaining his second bachelor's degree in fine arts at Yale in 1931. In Rome, he traveled throughout Europe and met many modernist pioneers, whom he interviewed for articles in Pencil Points magazine. Through these articles, he described the work of Walter Gropius, Mies van der Rohe, Le Corbusier, and Gio Ponti. In 1933 He returned to America to devote himself to writing. This Fortune article was probably his first for a major nonarchitectural magazine. ![](images/dabf213951bded9ddafffe76ff039857b9fc0e3c2382901d60e74457d02117f1.jpg) George Nelson, ca. 1950s (courtesy Industrial Designers Society of America). Nelson began the article with a quotation from Oliver M.W. Sprague (1873-1953), who had just in December 1933 resigned as special assistant to the secretary of the treasury in Washington in order to return to Harvard Business School and resume his teaching there after a leave of absence of four years with the government. Now he was free to express his opinions publicly, and rather than criticizing the administration, Sprague placed the blame of the deepening Depression squarely on manufacturers: Failure of industries to adapt policies designed to open up additional demands for industrial products is, in my judgment, the chief cause of the persistence of the Depression.¹ In other words, manufacturers had failed to attract new consumers through the design of new and different products, precisely the service that industrial design offered. Nelson stated the essential case for industrial design: Whenever two products are equal in point of utility and price, the one that looks more attractive to the purchaser will be bought first. When the demand is sufficient to take only one of two products, the maker of the attractive product stays in business, the maker of the other goes bankrupt.² After this do-or-die challenge to his business audience, Nelson briefly provided historical context by describing how the art industries traditionally and effectively used design, but that the engineer-run artless industries of the 20th century were unprepared and unstaffed to do so when style became important to their largely functional products. He then described in detail how industrial designers worked, and what benefits they could bring to business. The main thrust of the article focused on "a few leading industrial designers" and what they had done in the last few years by citing their previous experience, their clients, their fees, their typical achievements, and photo-portraits of ten of them. Those included were many who have already been described in this book. Quotes that follow are from the Fortune article. Norman Bel Geddes, now a tousled, curly-haired 41-year-old, was described by Nelson as a "bomb-thrower" and the "P.T. Barnum of industrial design," but three pages are devoted to his background in stage design, his business practice, and his book, Horizons, including an entire sidebar page, "Story of a Stove," that was devoted to his stunningly modern and simple, but practical, stove concepts for the Standard Gas Equipment Corporation (SGE). The president of SGE, Frank Roberts, had walked into Bel Geddes's office and requested a new stove design within two weeks and offered to pay \$1,500. Bel Geddes explained that he did not just draw pretty pictures, that study to produce a better stove would take a year, and required \$50,000 as an advance of royalties. Shocked, Roberts conferred with his directors, but he decided to take the risk Bel Geddes put an engineer in the SGE plant, studied competitors' methods, and interviewed hundreds of housewives and store dealers. His report and recommendations numbered 300 pages. Photos in the article showed how Bel Geddes reduced the number of stove components currently stocked by SGE from hundreds to just 16 through standardization, which he demonstrated like a showman with colored block models. When the stove appeared on the market in January 1933, the first one all white, Robert's decision was vindicated: “Housewives wanted the Geddes stove. Utilities that had never heard about the line before welcomed it. Sales rose, doubled, held their gains." In the same year as this article, Bel Geddes designed an Electrolux air-cooled gas refrigerator, two years before Loewy's Coldspot for Sears, as well as an Electrolux vacuum cleaner; a refrigerator for Servel, Inc.; a prefabricated service station for Socony-Vacuum Oil Company; metal and plastic beds for the Rome Manufacturing Company; cigarette cases, boxes, cocktail shakers, candlesticks, and ashtrays for the Revere Copper and Brass Company; and a Plymouth car design for the Chrysler Company. He also wrote an article entitled “Streamlining" for Atlantic Monthly magazine. All the while, he continued to design and produce theater sets and to revise theater architecture.³ In the article, Nelson praised designers, not merely for attractive and modern styles, but also for their contributions to manufacturing cost reduction and user convenience. Donald Dohner at Westinghouse found a way to save \$40,000 on tooling costs by making a few changes to his model. He changed a metal vacuum cleaner to plastic, making it lighter, quieter, and more powerful, putting Westinghouse into the vacuum cleaner business. Finding that fat men operated most locomotives, he widened passages and spaced levers and wheels for fat hands. Dohner is shown as a serious-looking, slim, dark-haired 4l-year-old with a “Hitler"- style mustache. John Vassos, shown as a dark-haired, handsome 41-year-old, noted for his radios for RCA, his turnstiles for the Perey Manufacturing Company, and a Coca-Cola drink dispenser, also invented a hook for a wall telephone, which the company found patentable. Henry Dreyfuss, the youngest at age 30, a smiling young man with glasses, placed all the controls in one place, on the top, in his design of the Toperator washing machine for Sears. The article featured detailed biographies and operating styles of key designers. Lurelle Guild, a balding 36-year-old, was described to be as “prolific in design as he was in writing. He redesigns about a thousand products a year, the majority of which are art-industry products, and makes an average of four finished drawings for each product." Guild claimed that he “has never designed a product that was a commercial failure, has never lost a client." Designs by Guild cited in the article included a refrigerator for the Norge Corporation, cooking utensils for the Wear-Ever Aluminum Company, and designs for Montgomery Ward for everything from stoves to roller skates. His coffeepot for Wear-Ever had a lower production cost but increased sales by 40 percent. His annual retainer fee was \$25,000, his daily fees were \$100 to \$200, or alternatively, he offered flat fees of \$300 to \$25,000, plus royalties. Walter Dorwin Teague, now 51 and shown with a mustache and wearing a dapper fedora, was described as "suave, able, and successful” and “is the generally acknowledged doyen of the profession” with an “unbroken record of commercial successes." His designs cited included cameras for Eastman Kodak, with whom he had a six-year retainer. In 1934 his famous Art Deco Kodak Baby Brownie camera was introduced, priced at only \$1, and four million of them would be sold. He also had designed a furnace for National Radiator; a line of Taylor Instruments; a line of Pyrex ovenware; a mimeograph machine for the A.B. Dick Company; a dramatic Art Deco Bluebird radio with a blue, circular mirror face for Sparton; and an oil heater for American Gas Machine that increased sales by 400 percent. His retainer fees were \$12,000 to \$24,000, and his flat fees were \$500 to \$5,000. Donald Dohner, whose department at Westinghouse reportedly cost \$75,000 per year, was described as “among the dynamos of East Pittsburgh," and his design for a water cooler "captured 40 percent of the national market in a few months." His electric range "outsold previous models seven to one," a 700 percent increase, with lower production costs. His dieselelectric locomotive, intended merely for an exhibit, “was actually sold right off the exhibit floor." (Author's note: Dohner's diesel-electric design was destined to become Pennsylvania Railroad's famous GG-1 locomotive late in 1934. More on this later.) Gustav Jensen, shown as a blondish, serious-looking 36-year-old, "generally regarded as the top man from the purely artistic point of view," was recognized for his design of his Monel metal sink for International Nickel Company, a telephone for AT&T, and a water heater for the L.O. Koven & Brothers Company. His retainer ees were cited as \$500 to \$20,000. A brief biography of George Sakier, shown as a scowling, dark-haired 37-year-old, described him as “probably the purest type of industrial designer among those here described," and mentioned his work for American Radiator and Standard Sanitary Corporation, designing bathtubs, washbasins, vacuum equipment, and bathroom units stamped from metal, which "are the first prefabricated house units actually in production. The entire bathroom with the three major fixtures and the walls and floor can be assembled as a single unit, or the parts can be used separately. One hundred and thirty-three of them are now being put into a Washington, D.C. apartment house." His income was estimated at \$15,000 to \$25,000, plus retainers, from his design work. Also mentioned was his glassware work for Fostoria, with the result that "American open-stock glass for the first time took precedence over European, and finally he won the distinction of having his own designs for glass pirated in Europe." Raymond Loewy, pictured as a dark-haired, mustached 41-year-old, was described as "the least publicized and one of the largest earners," with retainer fees from \$10,000 to \$60,000, plus royalties. His work cited included a globe-shaped radio for Colonial which registered a sales increase of 700 percent; his Hupp automotive designs; kitchen sink and bathroom units for Sears, Roebuck; and his duplicator (copy machine) for Sigmund Gestetner. Loewy was referred to as "he who discovered that all Americans like the color green and the smell of lilac." Henry Dreyfuss was recognized for having already been written about in the New Yorker, American, and Saturday Evening Post magazines for his work in stage design, and in fact he was still designing one show on Broadway every season. His product designs cited included the Toperator wash machine for Sears, Roebuck; his Protectograph check protector for the Todd Company (introduced in 1935); and alarm clocks for Western Clock Company, but he was also designing interiors for Cities Service corporate aircraft and an insulated thermos jug made by the American Company while working on a “telephone of the future" for Bell Laboratories. His flat fees were \$1,000 to \$25,000, or an hourly rate of \$50. John Vassos was mentioned as a well-known writer of psychoanalysis, but also for his designs of a Coca-Cola dispenser; his turnstiles for Perey Manufacturing Company, which increased sales by 25 percent; and his radios for RCA. He generally charged \$12,000 for retainer fees, or flat fees from \$1,000 to \$7,000. Harold Van Doren was described as "handsome," and his design cited was his scale for Toledo Scale, which permitted lower manufacturing costs and multiplied sales by 900 percent. His consultation fees were \$100 per day, or flat fees from \$500 to \$5,000. Joseph Platt's Vacuumatic fountain pen' for Parker increased sales by 100 percent, with lower factory costs. Jo Sinel's Accoustican hearing aid for Dictograph (1932) was cited for having resulted in a reduced price, with a net profit increase of 50 percent. Sinel had also just designed a typewriter for Remington, a Juicy Fruit vending machine, two lanterns for Dietz, and a Toastmaster Jr., none of which were mentioned in the article. In fact, in another 1934 issue, Fortune gave a fullpage color spread to Sinel's packaging and trademark designs, for which he was also well known. Donald Deskey and Russel Wright were both mentioned as newcomers to the field. Deskey had just been retained by the Libby Glass Company in Toledo, Ohio, and was not only designing a champagne glass for them, but advising them on their entire line of products. Wright had recently designed elegant sterling silver flatware that the Museum of Modern Art would much later honor by commissioning the production of replicas. ## Nelson concluded the article, Most manufacturers agree that the industrial designer has stimulated all those he has met in the company's organization, given them new ideas, new energy, a new attitude. Designers will not cure our industrial ills, for our ills lie deeper than design. But the success of industrial designers is symptomatic of a changing attitude on the part of the manufacturers, a growing recognition of the old American ideal of progress: cheaper and better goods for all.⁴ Nelson would establish a New York architectural design office in 1936 with William Hamby, which would continue until after the war, and Nelson would become a prolific and dynamic leader in postwar furniture design and establish his own office in New York in 1947. The article had an immediate impact, not only on the small community of industrial designers, who benefited enormously from the national publicity and recognition, but on the large segment of the business community that had little or no previous knowledge of industrial design and how it could increase sales. Suddenly they made the connection between their primary problem of diminishing sales and the obvious solution of new designs to generate new demand. The traditional manufacturing image of industrial art as simply the addition of decorative surface embellishment, had been transformed into industrial design, the reshaping and modernizing of the entire product. In a sense, this was the reestablishment of the artist as a multidisciplinary creative individual in the modern world, as emphasized in Nelson's title, "Both Fish and Fowl," not unlike the 19th-century craftsmen who were involved with the materials, costs, forms, engineering and production of artifacts, as well as the appearance. Industrial designers not only became the 20th-century craftsmen, controlling the form of mass-produced products, but could command fees beyond any craftsman's imagination. Predictably, the demand for industrial designers increased overnight. The attraction for designers is not difficult to understand. In 1934, the average annual salary for corporate executives was \$5,000, and the average laborer's pay was 50 cents per hour. One could buy a three-bedroom house from Montgomery Ward for \$1,000, a men's dress shirt for \$1, and an icebox refrigerator for \$11.95. The average annual retainer fees of \$50,000 and average hourly rates of \$50 charged by industrial designers were incredible. In today's dollars, that would be \$1 million annually and \$1,000 per hour. But to manufacturers, higher fees for sales increases ranging from 25 to 900 percent, with an average of 350 percent, were worth it. Manufacturers could, and did, pay anything for that kind of boost to sales. These fees were enough to attract some of the most talented people in other fields. Some automotive designers on Harley Earl's payroll at General Motors abandoned the field to enter the more lucrative world of independent industrial design consultants. Among these were Carl W. Sundberg (1910–1982) and Montgomery Ferar (1910–1982). Sundberg was born in Calumet, Michigan, graduated from Northern High School, and attended Wicker Art School in Detroit. He began his career in 1927 designing custom car bodies under Ray Dietrich at ![](images/3b10df58370c211545acaf84402cfe62771d6113e2243b4d4d79c9f033f47913.jpg) Carl W. Sundberg, ca. 1930s (courtesy Sundberg-Ferar, www.sundbergferar.com). ![](images/144dcd9fde2ac04dccef64c2f8f493c9438a6fa2336d0c8e61aede738117dc00.jpg) Montgomery Ferar, ca. 1930s (courtesy Sundberg-Ferar, www.sundbergferar.com). Dietrich Body Company, and then worked in plastics design at Kurz-Kasch, Inc. He then joined Earl's Art and Colour Section at GM. Ferar was born in Boston and received a bachelor's degree in architecture at MIT in 1932, and took a year of study in Europe with a fellowship in 1933. He worked for several architectural firms in Boston before moving to Detroit to work for the Detroit Planning Commission, and he joined GM's Art and Colour Section, where he met Sundberg. In 1934 they both left GM to form the industrial design firm of Sundberg-Ferar, Inc., in Detroit, beginning a lifelong partnership. Another automotive designer at GM, John Richard “Jack" Morgan (1903–1986), left GM to become chief product designer at Sears, Roebuck's first design department in Chicago in 1934, which was formed as part of Department 817, the Merchandise Testing and Development Laboratory. Morgan was born Juan Ricardo Morgan in Guatemala City, Guatemala, and moved with his family in 1913 to Canada. At age 11 he won first prize for a watercolor at the Canadian National Exhibition. He became a student of renowned landscape painter Thomas John “Tom" Thompson (1877–1917). As a painter, Morgan traveled abroad to Brussels, Berlin, Copenhagen, Oslo, Stockholm, Paris and London, and eventually became an accomplished artist with a studio in Wheeling, Illinois. He had two one-man shows in Chicago and exhibited in Palm Beach, Connecticut, and Pennsylvania. But Morgan's love of automobiles inspired him to attend the Detroit Technical Institute, and in 1927 he joined the C.G. Spring and Bumper Company. In 1928, he was hired by Harley Earl's infant Art and Colour Section at GM, which by then numbered 50. Working in the Pontiac Studio under Frank Hershey (1907–1997), Morgan designed the first auto bumper to be integrated into the body. At Sears in 1934, Morgan designed the Sears Waterwitch outboard motor and would head Sears Department 817 until 1943, designing vacuum cleaners, kitchen cabinet consoles, and much of Sears' appliance merchandise, including the first "turtle neck" washing-machine tub. One of Morgan's key early designers was Clare Hodgman (191l–1992), born in Buchanon, Michigan, who had started his career in General Motors' Oldsmobile studio with Harley Earl in the early 1930s and had been previously working for Loewy. Another designer, Brooks Stevens (1911–1995), like John Morgan, was also working on outboard motors, needed for a popular new outdoor recreational activity, power boating. His current client was Evinrude, and he had just opened his own office in Milwaukee in 1934. Twenty-three-year-old Clifford Brooks Stevens was born in Milwaukee and studied architecture at Cornell University from 1929 to 1933. He won a contest for designing a new trademark for his father's firm, Cutler-Hammer, and would now continue to design equipment and displays for the company as a consultant designer. His first product design for another firm was a glass peanut butter jar for Holsum Products. ![](images/1f4521b7f3292eb35ce9afc5e4e346726cb1180271c625b71684500f2a30a5ad.jpg) Brooks Stevens, ca. 1936 (Brooks Stevens Archive, Milwaukee Art Museum, Gift of the Brooks Stevens Family and the Milwaukee Institute of Art and Design). The February 1934 Fortune article spurred industrial designers to toot their own horn. They organized an Art and Industry Exhibition at Rockefeller Center, sponsored by the National Alliance for Art and Industry, that featured 1,000 products by 100 designers, to illustrate “what designers are doing ... to help the engineer sell his mechanical devices." The exhibit included Raymond Loewy's iconic chrome-plated, teardrop, highly streamlined pencil sharpener (which was never produced); Henry Dreyfuss's check printer, a bathroom scale by Harold Van Doren; furniture by Gilbert Rohde; and a Wurlitzer radio by Russel Wright. By this time, the popularity of Art Deco and French Art Moderne was fading, and streamlining was in the ascendancy. Egmont Arens reported “an amazing response and welling up of national enthusiasm for 'streamlining’ which had captured the American imagination to mean modern, efficient, well-organized, sweet, clean, and beautiful." In a more abstract manner, the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) mounted a Machine Art Exhibition curated by architect Philip Johnson (1906–2005) from March 6 to April 30, 1934, celebrating the “abstract beauty” of shapes made by machines, and a poster on April 30, 1934, celebrating the abstract beauty of shapes made by machines. The exhibition catalog began with an ancient Greek quotation by Plato: By beauty of shapes I do not mean, as most people would suppose, the beauty of living igures or of pictures, but to make my point clear, I mean straight lines and circles, and shapes, plane or solid, made from them by lathe, ruler, and square. These are not, like other things, beautiful relatively, but always and absolutely.⁵ In the exhibit catalog, MoMA director Alfred H. Barr, Jr. (1902–1981), followed up on Plato's description of the beauty of geometry by stating, “Machines are, visually speaking, practical applications of geometry."6 The catalog continued The exhibition contains machines, machine parts, scientific instruments, and objects useful in ordinary life. There are no purely ornamental objects; the useful objects were, however, chosen for their aesthetic quality. Some will claim that usefulness is more important than beauty, or that usefulness makes an object beautiful. This exhibition has been assembled from the point of view that though usefulness is an essential, appearance has at least as great a value. The objects displayed were divided according to use in six categories: Industrial units: Machines and machine parts, insulators, cable sections, propeller blades [aircraft and ship], etc. [springs, ball bearings, insulators, car pistons, and a Sears Waterwitch outboard motor designed by John R. Morgan]. Household and ofice equipment: Sink, furnace, bathroom cabinets [an Arco Panel lavatory in which a prefabricated mirror, cabinet, and lighting unit formed a floor-to-ceiling cabinet backing for a washbasin, by George Sakier], dishwasher, carpet sweeper, mirrors, fans, vacuums, and business machines [including a National cash register designed by Walter Dorwin Teague]. Kitchenware: [Toaster, pots and pans, coffee urns, bowls, ladles, and the like]. House furnishings and accessories: Objects used in daily life: tableware, [porcelain flatware, tumblers], vases and bowls, smoking accessories, lighting fixtures, and furniture [including the Beta chair designed by Nathan George Horwitt, and billiard balls]. Scientific instruments: Precision, optical, drafting and surveying instruments [microscopes, slide rules]. Laboratory glass and porcelain: Beakers, hydrometer jars, Petri dishes, and boiling flasks. The listings of 400 items included the name of the item, the manufacturer, the designer, and the price. The manufacturers were treated as artists, not mere producers. Mounted on the walls throughout the exhibit, in clear black lettering, were the names of U.S. companies: Aluminum Company of America, U.S. Steel Corporation, American Radiator Company, Bingham Stamping and Tool, American Sheet and Tin Plate Company, etc. In this advertising-like environment, visitors recognized nearly all of the items and could handle and test them. The New Yorker wrote, “The place itself looks, more than anything else, like a very elaborate hardware store." It was a genuine shopping experience, as visitors purchased items such as chairs, billiard balls, and plumbing fixtures, demonstrating that in America, art and everyday objects fused into a single experience. Over 31,000 people visited the exhibit.8 MoMA's purpose, of course, was to establish machine-made products as a legitimate form of modern art, in the same way it had promoted that status for architecture in its 1932 exhibition, and to obsolete the Arts and Crafts movement, as Philip Johnson said, "to demonstrate ... that the decorative arts were no longer in existence, but that art could still be made without the handcraft approach—so it was an anti-handcraft show."9 In fact, the Arts and Crafts movement had already expired. The famous Wiener Werkstätte (Viennese Workshops), founded in 1903, had just been closed, not by the Nazis, but because the high prices for its products had caused it to fail in its social cause of ensuring that the life of everyone was enhanced by everyday objects designed by artists. Now industrial designers were actually implementing that idealistic objective in America. MoMA had another less obvious agenda, and that was to co-opt industrial design, this new art, under the leadership wing of architects, as had been the case in Europe. MoMA hated streamlining, which was harshly criticized by MoMA director Alfred Barr: "Their designs are aimed at sales appeal of the most superficial kind."10 MoMA resented that advertising artists, set designers, writers, automotive and furniture designers were dominating industrial design and being heralded in the press and business world. MoMA wanted to take over the new profession for architects. This goal remained for the rest of the decade, when MoMA would launch a full-scale attack to dominate industrial design, which would succeed only in modifying the general style from streamlining to "organic" design. But the term "streamlining" still lives in our language, to describe the simplification and speeding up of things, as in phrases such as "we streamlined our office procedures." The MoMA Machine Art Exhibition also established MoMA's collection protocol for the future, as MoMA acquired 100 of the manufactured objects in the exhibition into its permanent collection, a practice it would continue for decades, just as traditional museums acquired collections of paintings or artifacts. The exhibit boosted the sale of American-made goods, strengthened the growing alliance between artists and manufacturers, and broke down the wall between art and commercial products, just as Duchamp had done in 1917 with his urinal as art. When MoMA says it is art, it is art. Over the two months of the exhibit, more than 31,200 people attended, and the cultural elite and critics embraced it. Its effect on the popularity of modern design and on the acceptance of industrial designers rivaled the Fortune magazine article. Meanwhile, in Detroit, George Walker had been working since 1929 for Dura, an automotive parts supplier, when in 1934 he opened his own industrial design office in Detroit and would soon design an upright vacuum cleaner for Apex, which would be marketed under the Montgomery Ward (MW) brand. But Walker would soon become inyolved with the automotive industry far beyond his imagination, as we will see later. Car companies had been following the lead of General Motors and establishing internal design departments. Ford in 1933 began to update the appearance of its cars yearly, and to do this, Edsel Ford (1893–1943), Henry's son, established a styling department under the leadership of Eugene T. “Bob" Gregorie (1908–2004). Gregorie was born in New York City and raised in Cedarhurst, Long Island. In 1927, with no formal education, he started as a nautical draftsman at the Elco Boat Works in Bayonne, New Jersey, then moved to the more prestigious vacht designers Cox & Stevens in New York in 1928. In 1929, he followed his first love of automobiles by beginning at Brewster & Company, maker of custom bodies for Rolls Royce. Later that year he joined Harley Earl's Art and Colour Group at GM, but the stock market crash cost him his job. After a time unemployed and staying with his family in South Carolina, he was offered a job as body draftsman at the Lincoln Motor Company in Detroit in 1931. He jumped at the chance. In 1928, following the lead of GM, Chrysler had established an internal Art and Color Department of five, headed by Herbert V. Henderson, but the department was subordinated to Chrysler's powerful engineering staff, and designers were relegated to cosmetic details such as grilles and hood ornaments. Chrysler's genuinely new innovative engineering design, under way since 1927, was by leading engineers Carl Breer, Fred Zeder, and Owen Skelton. Breer had designed the successful first Chrysler in 1924 and claimed it was his idea in 1927 to “follow nature" by designing a scientifically engineered, streamlined car. It was radically innovative in engineering and esthetics. Wind tunnel experiments led to an ideal streamlined form. To achieve the tapered aerodynamic tail of a teardrop, the engineers moved the engine up over the front axle and placed the rear seat within the wheelbase rather than over the rear axle. This more evenly distributed the weight between the front and rear wheels, making a more comfortable ride and better handling. Rather than a single flat plane, the windshield was comprised of two panes meeting in a V and sloping to the rear. The body was the first unitary construction, with the frame and body shell integrated into a lightweight but rigid structure. After the basic body form had been determined aerodynamically, stylists under Henderson were allowed to add cosmetic Art Deco details, such as parallel, decorative chrome elements on the grille, bumpers, and hood vents. The grille looked like a chrome waterfall splashing over the huge hood. Norman Bel Geddes, the country's foremost industrial designer, was engaged as a consultant in September 1933, and he reduced the number and widened the vertical chrome bars on the waterfall grille, raked the windshield back, and added side vents for cooling. The modernistic interior had rubber mats instead of carpets, and the seats were mounted in a frame of steel tubing. Introduced to the public in 1934, the car was hailed as the first functionally streamlined production car, the Airflow, used for both the Chrysler and DeSoto new models. Bel Geddes happily promoted the design to the public. But to many, the bulbous sloping, front end that blended into the fenders looked clumsy, front heavy, and crudely shaped. It looked a bit like Dr. Porsch's 1932 prototype Kleinauto on steroids, devoid of grace and beauty. The engineers had achieved an innovative structural design, but potential customers stayed away in droves because the design, particularly the waterfall grille, was too radical for customers accustomed to seeing essentially vertical front noses on cars. Chrysler would sell 18,000 units the first year, but only sales of Chrysler's conventionally designed models saved the company from financial ruin. The disaster convinced Walter Chrysler that engineering innovation alone was not enough, and he hired Raymond Dietrich (1894–1982) to become head of the Art and Color Department, and at the same time elevated the responsibility and power of the department. Dietrich was born in the Bronx, and at age 12, his father sponsored his apprenticeship as an engraver with Manhattan's American Bank Note Company. He earned money as a pitcher for numerous semipro baseball teams to finance his education in the arts. In 1913 he joined coach builder Brewster & Company, which sponsored him in study at the Andrew F. Johnson Technical School in Manhattan to learn advanced drafting and body design. Graduating in 1917, he first went to Chevrolet but within a year rejoined Brewster. As a “master craftsman," he finished designs in a closed room to protect secret techniques. In 1920, he and Thomas Hibbard (1898–1982) left Brewster to start their own firm in Manhattan, LeBaron Carrossiers. They initiated the process of designing a whole car in detail on paper to achieve a harmonious appearance, a technique that later would become standard automotive as well as industrial design practice. In 1924, the firm merged with the Bridgeport Body Company, forming LeBaron, Inc., but in 1925 Dietrich sold his stake and joined the Murray Corporation of America, the company that built most of Lincoln's and Ford's production bodies. Murray and Dietrich shared ownership in a subsidiary manufacturing plant called Dietrich, Inc. Dietrich assisted Amos Northup in his design of the 1931 Reo Royale Eight, the predecessor of the groundbreaking 1932 Graham Blue Streak, mentioned earlier, but Dietrich was forced out of Murray in 1931. At Chrysler, Dietrich was given the job of redesigning the objectionable hood of the illfated Airflow, which he did by extending the hood and adding a more conventional-looking, vertical grille. He also styled a conventional companion for the Airflow, called the Airstream, which would be produced in 1935–1936 for Chrysler and DeSoto, and marketed beside the Airflow, meeting the public need while hoping to sell enough Airflows to offset its development. The Airstream would outsell the Airflow by five to one the first year, and nine to one in 1936. The market failure of the Airflow would go down in automotive history as the result of styling too advanced for popular acceptance, and the failure of engineers to grasp the subtleties of appearance design. The entire industry concluded that even meticulous and innovative engineering could not sell an ugly car. The Airflow failure would become a powerful argument for stylists, who possessed the elusive skill of designing for public emotions and tastes, rather than just for practical and functional purposes like engineers. Despite the disaster of the Airflow, and despite the scientific fact that streamlining of cars had little effect on operation except at high speeds, streamlining would remain a popular style for the remainder of the decade, because the style was imprinted on consumers' minds as an icon of modernity and progress, which they hoped would take them out of the continuing Depression. Streamlining was front and center on May 26, 1934, as the Century of Progress Exposition reopened in Chicago for its second year. Headlining the opening was the national demonstration of several brand new, streamlined trains. On May 26, the diesel-powered Burlington Zephyr, known as the “Silver Streak," set a speed record for travel between Denver, Colorado, and Chicago, Illinois, by making a “dawn to dusk" dash over the 1,015-mile distance in 13 hours and 5 minutes (normal trains took 25 hours) at an average speed of 77 mph, and arrived at the Century of Progress Exposition on opening day to appear on the Wings of the Century stage for display. The Zephyr was built by the E.G. Budd Manufacturing Company, Philadelphia, for the Chicago, Burlington and Quincy Railroad (CB&Q), commonly known as the Burlington, to promote and advertise passenger rail service. Christening was on April 18. The Zephyr consisted of three cars: the power car, baggage-coach combine, and the coachobservation car. It was made of bright stainless steel, réquiring no painting or maintenance, and was fastened together using a new technique of electric spot-weling developed by Budd. The entire train weighed the same as a single conventional train car and reached ll2 mph during its record run. A 600 hp, eight-cylinder, Winton engine powered a generator, and the electricity generated was fed to electric traction motors connected to the axles in the front wheel truck. There were four trucks, one between each car, and one at each end. 'The nose design was a slanted half cylinder, often referred to as a “bathtub shrouding” or “shovel nose," with forward-facing windows to provide full visibility for the operator. ![](images/a4e50584ca4c65be94dcc03e87cee1c6514b96e7c5a6b5cf8fa72f62650e7b62.jpg) ![](images/6d2e1cea6899fe2801a28eeff51275563486ad0b86881630aefd60fd8782bc6b.jpg) ![](images/af451f52f8858e4987ff44b930234e2477dd3203ba72bcd631d2dba8e13adfd0.jpg) The exterior streamline design was by aeronautical engineer Albert Gardner Dean, in collaboration with Philadelphia architects John Harbeson (1888–1986) and Paul Philippe Cret (1876–1945). After display at the exposition, the train was taken on a 31-state, 222-city publicity tour. More than two million people saw the train before it entered regular service in November between Kansas City, Missouri, and Lincoln, Nebraska. The very name, Zephyr, would soon be applied to products to capitalize on its popularity as a term of speed and modernity, such as Kem Weber's streamlined Zephyr digital clock design of 1934, and the Lincoln Zephyr of 1936. The Burlington Zephyr was a hard act to follow, but there was another streamliner that tried to do so. The gasoline-powered M-10000 (its first design patent number was D100,000, followed by U.S. patents D100,001 and D100,002), produced by Union Pacific Railroad, visited the Chicago exposition after a tour of 68 cities that totaled 13,000 miles, everywhere attracting press attention and crowds that approximated one million. It was the first internal combustion, lightweight, streamlined, express passenger train in the United States, delivered to the railroad on February 12, 1934. Named on the design patent were Eugene Adams, Martin P. Bloomberg, William H. Mussey, and William B. Stout, all engineers at Pullman-Standard, the body developer. There were three cars, the power and baggage car up front, and two passenger cars, totaling 204 feet in length and weighing 77 tons. All were built of Duralumin, a hardened aluminum alloy, and the bodies slanted inward at the bottom. The entire train was supported on just four wheeled trucks, located between cars, and at front and rear. The 600 hp engine, built by Winton, a subsidiary of General Motors, powered a generator of electricity that fed electric traction motors. The engine was housed in the front power car and featured a turret cab with windshields and a bulbous nose with two large air intakes for cooling. The body was painted in Armour Yellow with a Leaf Brown roof and undersides. Overall, the M-10000 design very closely resembled Norman Bel Geddes's Locomotive Number 1, his futuristic, never-produced design in his book, Horizons, in 1932, which also had three cars and a bulbous nose. M-10000, nicknamed the “Tin Worm," would enter regular service as the City of Salina, for the Kansas City-Salina route it served. The Chicago Fair closed on Halloween, October 31, 1934, with a total two-year attendance record of over 48 million. Newspaper accounts described the last night as resembling New Year's Eve celebrations, with merrymaking running wild. For the first time in American history, an international fair had paid for itself, paying off the \$10 million debt from a bond issued on October 28, 1929, the day before the stock market crashed. But it was the modern trains exhibited there that would inspire the railroad industry to follow the automotive lead in modern design and draw industrial designers into the business.


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\06_Chapter_06.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `7c36dadec9deedc166bdc0e5aa5ec20fa8d094c826bfb709ce44b79efd1140b1`

# CHAPTER 6 # Recovery, Plastics, and Education The two pioneering train designs connected to the Chicago exposition had a significant and immediate effect on the railroad industry. In November 1934, the Pennsylvania Railroad (PRR) hired Raymond Loewy to refine the Westinghouse design of the GG-1 design for mass production. Loewy had designed a trashcan for Pennsylvania Station in New York in 1932, and at the time had shown the PRR president a futuristic sketch of a streamlined locomotive. The GG-1 was a class of electric locomotives powered by alternating current (AC) overhead wires. Twelve 385 hp traction motors drove the GG-1's 57-inch-diameter driving wheels on six axles. The body, over 76 feet long, was built in two halves joined with a ball-and-socket joint to allow negotiation of sharp curves, and each half had a raised control cab, each facing opposite directions, so it could be driven in forward or reverse. In 1933, PRR had decided to replace its current P5a electric locomotives with ones that were more powerful and more streamlined. PRR asked General Electric and Westinghouse to design prototypes that were capable of 100 mph, a streamlined body shell, and a central control cab. Both companies submitted prototypes in August 1934, and both worked with PRR's Baldwin Locomotive Works as the design progressed. Features from both prototypes were refined and combined to produce the final design. The Westinghouse prototypes were based on six plaster models made by Donald Dohner (mentioned earlier), Westinghouse's director of art in its engineering division, in 1933 when the project began. One of these models, shown in photographs, closely resembles the final design of what became the GG-1 prototype, which essentially was completed by August 1934, three months before Loewy was called in. By all traditions of attribution, Dohner should have been credited with the styling of the GG-1. Loewy was called in for consultation on October 25, 1934, by PRR vice president and heir apparent Martin W. Clement to “enhance the GG-1's esthetics." A fee of \$5,000 was agreed upon, but he had only about a week before the production order was to be placed, so his contributions on the existing prototype were limited to the color scheme, which became Brunswick Green, with five gold pinstripes running along the sides and curving down on the front and rear toward the ground. Loewy also claimed he had recommended a smooth welded body instead of the riveted one used in the prototype, but there is some evidence that the rivets were the result of rushing the completion of the prototype and that they were never intended for use on the production models. One hundred thirty-nine production models would be built between 1934 and 1943, and many remained in service until the 1970s. In any event, Loewy was retained for \$20,000 annually as the primary design consultant of PRR for the rest of the decade. Until his death, he publicly claimed the design of the GG-1 as one of his most important achievements and was exclusively credited with its design in all literature pertaining to the GG-1. Loewy was its ultimate promoter, not Dohner, who was essentially a modest man, who would, within a month after he designed the models, leave Westinghouse to begin his career in industrial design education, for which he is better remembered.¹ ![](images/03bb3e1459a6bfd3841e0d4cf409d7fbd778dd552b8ac4219864c35851f25c98.jpg) Pennsylvania Railroad GG-1 (Classic Trains, Summer 2009. Photograph by Karl Zimmermann). The main reason Donald Dohner never received credit was that after February 1934 he was no longer employed by Westinghouse. In fact he was fired as a result of the Fortune article on industrial design that month. The article, described in detail earlier, revealed the fact that his department at Westinghouse was costing the company \$75,000 per year (nearly \$1.3 million in 2013 dollars), a fact apparently not known by Westinghouse management, which was understandably shocked, and in the middle of a Depression took executive action to cut expenses. ![](images/e6c27aea490ba71a9ca55475f464b90cad80098da45e5f0835f1d91c1a18e8f1.jpg) Alexander Kostellow, ca. 1950s (Industrial Design magazine, October 1954). Ironically, this unfortunate turn of events resulted in a new career for Dohner, and a milestone for the industrial design profession. As you may recall, before joining Westinghouse in 1930, Dohner had taught design as an instructor at the Carnegie Institute of Technology (CIT) starting in 1929. Upon hearing of Dohner's loss of job, his old colleagues at CIT were overjoyed at the opportunity of welcoming back someone who was now a nationally known industrial designer, recently featured in Fortune magazine. Painting and Design Department painting instructor Alexander Kostellow (1896–1954) reportedly shouted to department head Wilfred Readio, "Donald's on the loose! Let's get him! It's [industrial design] the wave of the future! Painting is dead! Start an option [degree] in industrial design!"2 Alexander Jusserand Kostellow was born in Iran (then Persia). He studied in Paris and the University of Berlin and came to the United States in 1916, where he studied painting at the National Academy, the Art Students League of New York, and the Kansas City Art Institute, where he met and married Rowena Reed. Both came to Pittsburgh in 1929 to teach at CIT, he in painting, and she in sculpture. Carnegie was not the first school with interest in industrial design. As mentioned earlier, there were a number of art schools already teaching it in various programs for engineers, and Pratt Institute in Brooklyn had engaged Donald Dohner as a “consultant and lecturer in industrial design." Students, who previously would have studied architecture, were now attracted to industrial design as an alternative, and indeed many did see it as "the wave of the future," as declared by Kostellow. The Chicago Art Institute offered industrial design courses in 1932, the same year the University of Cincinnati initiated an “Art and Industry" option in its School of Applied Arts, and in California, Kem Weber initiated a program in industrial design at the Art Center School in Los Angeles. He would teach there until 1943.3 In 1933 the Detroit Society of Arts and Crafts had made national news by recognizing the automobile as an “art form," and at the Cleveland School (now Institute) of Art (CIA), Viktor Schreckengost (1906–2008) had initiated a pioneer program of industrial design in 1933, which would become well known for automotive design. ![](images/c7c5c6e105bca5512a5bac36bbd962f41f845ab681fda9e16adbcb8afb70c96e.jpg) Schreckengost was born in Sebring, Ohio, to a potter father and had studied design and ceramics at CIA, graduating in 1929. After studying at the Kunstegewerbeschule (Arts and Crafts School) in Austria, he joined the faculty at CIA and worked part time at the Cowan Pottery Studio. There he created his famous Viktor Schreckengost, ca. 1939 (courtesy David Nowacek and Gene Schreckengost). 28-inch-diameter Art Deco “Jazz" punch bowl that had been commissioned anonymously by Eleanor Roosevelt, who later ordered more for the White House and Hyde Park. Schreckengost soon became a consultant to the White Motor Company in Cleveland, and would later create an innovative truck design with the cab over the engine to provide more storage space in the back. ![](images/c457f7c8797cd3bd754987062ca2b465bab69ee92c268cb508266a2ec644ee09.jpg) Robert Lepper, ca. 1940s. At Carnegie in 1933, optional classes in industrial design, based on Dohner's 1930 syllabus, had been offered to students and were in progress as Dohner rejoined the faculty in 1934 and initiated a new degreed program in industrial design at CIT, the first school to do so. Assisting Dohner was Art and Design Department head, Wilfred Radio, and faculty members Kostellow, Russell Hyde, Fred Clayter, and Robert Lepper (1906– 1991). Lepper had been hired to fill in for Dohner's position when he left for Westinghouse in 1930. Lepper was born in Aspinwall, a suburb of Pittsburgh, and graduated with a degree in painting from CIT in 1927. He spent a year or so in Europe soaking up modern art and joined the faculty at CIT in 1930, where he painted "machine art," such as his 1932 Crankshaft, a realistic image of a Lincoln 12-cylinder crankshaft with pistons floating in the air. Because of his interest in mechanical things, he was more or less drafted into the new industrial design degree program in 1934. So in the fall semester of 1934, third-year students were offered an option for a degree in industrial design, along with other third-year options of illustration, interior decoration, or art education. The first class included three women and two males, all to become the first degreed graduates of industrial design in the world. Dohner took them to manufacturing plants, such as Westinghouse, Fostoria Glass, and ALCOA (Aluminum Company of America), to show them how things were made, and then instructed them to design new products based on what they had seen. Elsewhere, modern design continued to be publicized. From November 5, 1934, to January 6, 1935, the Metropolitan Museum of Art mounted an exhibition, Contemporary American Industrial Art, by 20 architectural and industrial designers that featured entire-room arrangements by architects except for a full-scale replica of an industrial design office and studio designed by Raymond Loewy. Architects and museums related all industrial design to architecture because they believed that architects should dominate the new feld, as was the case in Europe. Accordingly, many of the rooms were traditionally credited to name architects, who selected all the decorative furnishings to keep industrial design “in its proper place” under the supervision of architects. However, this time, a number of industrial designers were invited to participate in the event, including Viktor Schreckengost (chinaware), Donald Deskey (an entire dining room), Gilbert Rohde (Herman Miller clock and furniture), Gustav Jensen (rugs, wall treatments), Peter Müller-Munk (silverware), Walter von Nessen (display case and metalware), Lurelle Guild (silverware), Russel Wright (metalware, floor lamps), Alfons Bach (furniture) and Walter Dorwin Teague (on the cooperating committee). Still, the products shown were all from traditiónal art industries, studiously ignoring the many larger artless industry products by industrial designers, such as the elegant gas range Teague had recently designed for the Floyd-Wells Company in Royersford, Pennsylvania, that rivaled Bel Geddes's SGE range in simplicity, whiteness, and modernity. But attempts by architects to supervise and control industrial design were too late. The horse of industrial design was out of the architectural barn. Industrial designers already owned the field of both mechanical and decorative, modern mass-produced consumer products. Streamlined trains were still in the national news at the end of the year. On December 27, 1934, the New York Central Railroad (NYC) exhibited its new steam locomotive at Grand Central Station, with a streamlined shell painted in gunmetal gray and called the Commodore Vanderbilt. Named to honor the original Commodore Vanderbilt locomotive of 1870, it was the first to utilize streamlining by adding a streamlined shell over a steam locomotive, NYC's J-1E 4–6–4 “Hudson” No. 5344. An undergraduate student at Cleveland's Case School of Applied Science, Norman F. Zapf (1911–1974), designed the shell for his 1934 senior thesis, "The Streamlining of a Locomotive." Zapf tested scale models in a wind tunnel and concluded that at typical passenger speeds of 75 mph, streamlining reduced drag by 30 percent and required 350 less horsepower than a nonstreamlined locomotive. Nearly all his suggestions were incorporated into the final Commodore Vanderbilt. The nose form was similar to the "bathtub” design of the Burlington Zephyr. Carl Kantola, an in-house New York Central employee, worked on adapting the design to the required mechanical modifications. A number of sources cite Henry Dreyfuss as being a design consultant for the Commodore Vanderbilt, but his contributions, if any, are unknown. After being exhibited, the Commodore Vanderbilt departed on an exhibition tour of major cities on the NYC system, which was a smashing success, was highly photographed, and was surrounded by crowds wherever it went. After the tour, it was put in service hauling the 20th Century Limited between New York and Chicago, the grandest, most elegant passenger train in America. For the rest of the 1930s, the Commodore Vanderbilt was the most popular Lionel O-gauge model train. Although 1934 was one of the worst years of the ten-year Great Depression, it was the most exciting time for the new profession of industrial design, which became a household word as its leading practitioners became known nationally. Formal professional education in industrial design had begun. Manufacturers scrambled to catch up with the wave of modern design swirling around them by seeking new designs and new designers, hoping to extract themselves from the litany of declining sales reports. New design departments sprouted up in dozens of corporations. National spirits were rising due to the optimism generated by the Chicago Fair and the futuristic, streamlined trains crisscrossing the country. The future seemed to be the light at the end of the tunnel. The fledgling practitioners of industrial design, now that they had established legitimacy in the world of design and in the public arena, were looking to legally protect their innovative, modern, American designs from the wholesale copying that had previously been common practice for European designs. That had been the primary concern of the American Union of Decorative Artists (AUDAC) when it was formed in 1927. Now, in 1933, to address AUDAC's accusations of design piracy, representatives and designers in the furniture industry joined together to found the National Furniture Designers' Council (NFDC) in order to draw up a code for enjoining piracy for the National Recovery Administration (NRA). The purpose of the Roosevelt administration's establishment of the NRA in 1933 was to eliminate “cutthroat competition" by bringing industry, labor, and government together to create “fair practices" and to set prices. Industries were invited to submit codes of fair competition, and the furniture industry did so. But in 1935, the U.S. Supreme Court found that the NRA law was unconstitutional, ruling that it infringed on the separation of powers under the constitution. The NFDC disintegrated, but the seed for a furniture industry design organization had been planted, and in July 1935, Lawrence H. Whiting, director of the American Furniture Mart, called a special meeting of furniture designers, including those from the now defunct NFDC, and engaged Richard Bach, of the Metropolitan Museum, and Alfred Auerbach (1881–1944), design and merchandising consultant as well as editor of Retailing magazine, to serve as speakers for the meeting. The proceedings of the meeting are unknown, but the result, within a year, would be the forming of a furniture design organization, which will be described later. That year, Al Lambach, mentioned earlier, returned to Chicago to work for Merton Gershun at the Chicago Furniture Mart, and also for the Tell City Furniture Company in Tell City, Indiana, where he designed radio and phonograph cabinets for Zenith, Clinton, Traveler, and Emerson. Because home radios of the day were traditionally enclosed in furniture for placement in living rooms, a number of furniture designers in the 1920s and early 1930s were actively designing in this field. Furniture, as one of the traditional art industries, was still in the good graces of museums, such as Bach's Metropolitan Museum, and of course the architects as well, since many famous European architects, and equally famous American architects such as Frank Lloyd Wright, had been designing furniture for generations. A number of pioneer industrial designers had designed furniture too, including Norman Bel Geddes (Simmons Company, 1928); Paul Frankl (Skyscraper furniture, 1925); Gilbert Rohde (for Herman Miller Furniture Company, 1932–1935); John Vassos (RCA radio cabinets and his portable phonograph of 1935); Nathan Horwitt (Beta Chair for the Howell Company, Geneva, Illinois, 1930); Kem Weber (armchair by Lloyd Manufacturing Company of Menominee, Michigan, 1934); Donald Deskey (since 1926); and Russel Wright (Haywood-Wakefield, 1934). But most of those above had designed in the modern styles, not the traditional historic styles of many designers in the furniture industry. Throughout the 1930s, Wright and Walter Von Nessen had designed scores of modern metal accessories for the Chase Brass & Copper Company. In 1935, Wright had renamed his firm from Russel Wright Incorporated to Russel Wright Accessories, and designed a natural maple line of furniture called Modern Living, made by Conant Ball Company for Macy's, which was widely promoted. The popular line would later be called American Modern, produced in bleached wood“blond” maple, and would make Wright famous. He opened a showroom for his business in New York, with the help of businessman Irving Richards, under the name Russel Wright, Inc. In 1934, one of the pioneer furniture designers, Paul Frankl, relocated to California and became an active interior designer, working in a style that would be called “California Modern" at his gallery on Rodeo Drive in Beverly Hills, where celebrities such as Fred Astaire, Katherine Hepburn, Walter Houston, and Alfred Hitchcock became his clients. Other designers were working for celebrities. Around this time, Margaret Bourke-White (1904–1971), the famous Fortune photographer, approached John Vassos to design her studio in the Chrysler Building in New York, as well as the interior of her apartment in Tudor City. He did, and they remained close personal friends until her death. Furniture and furnishings were still the mainstream of the traditional art industries except that now modern design was replacing some of the traditional styles of historical periods. But the new mechanical, mass-produced products of the artless industries remained in artistic limbo as a category yet to be accepted by museums as legitimate art. Museums, including the Museum of Modern Art, and architects still had serious reservations about other mass-produced, mechanical products in which industrial designers were exclusively excelling, such as stoves, refrigerators, sinks, toilets, radios, office machines, etc. Elitists balked at calling these items furniture let alone works of art. To the elite artistic world, these products were commercially embarrassing, far exceeding the profitability of furniture or the decorative arts, and far more financially rewarding to the designers than any architect could expect. Accordingly, many regarded this arena of design as pure commercialism, the enemy of fine art, and were reluctant to embrace such mechanical products on an equal footing with furniture. Nevertheless, evidence of the influence of industrial design was everywhere in the consumer marketplace. Sears, Roebuck & Company had hired Raymond Loewy in 1932 to redesign its Coldspot refrigerator after Henry Dreyfuss had declined the project, due to his similar refrigerator project for General Electric. It was an unwritten code of ethics in early industrial design (and still is) that you didn't accept projects for products that were competitive with your own designs for another client. Upon accepting the job, Loewy described the current Coldspot as an “ill-proportioned vertical shoebox ‘decorated' with a maze of moldings, panels, etc. perched on spindly legs high off the ground."4 Loewy's design was actually done by Clare E. Hodgman, of Loewy's staff, who after the project was completed, was hired by Sears. The design was developed carefully with clay appearance models and came out in 1935—a sleek, white, vertical box with three, vertical, parallel ribs running from top to bottom in the center of its front, accenting the rectangular chrome Coldspot name escutcheon at eye level. Loewy had been inspired by his design of the interior shelf pattern from the radiator of the Hupmobile, on which he was working at the time. Sears paid Loewy \$2,500 for the design, but he had spent nearly three times that in expenses. But sales of the Coldspot in 1935 would double to \$1,388,000, and in five years would increase by fivefold. A highly appreciative Sears paid Loewy \$25,000 for three future annual model changes for the Coldspot, each of which was more aggressively modern than its predecessor. Loewy's reputation was made. He celebrated by relocating his office to a penthouse studio at Fifth Avenue and 47th Street and opening his first office in London, England, the first American designer to go international. Recall that his first design in 1929 was for an English client, Gestetner. He now had 12 clients and was doing very well. Sears hired Robert E. Bourke (1916–1996) in 1935. He was born in Chicago, went to Morgan Park High School in the southwest side, and as a kid was very interested in model airplanes. At age l1, he began to sketch model automobiles. He attended the Chicago Art Institute, cleaning floors and latrines to pay for tuition. He took courses in architecture and engineering at Armour Institute during the Depression and began working at Sears under Clarence Karstadt. His first assignment was to design a manure spreader by the Oliver Farm & Equipment Company. Jack Morgan headed the department. Just like his fellow employee, Clare Hodgman, Bourke would later work for Raymond Loewy. In May 1935, the French oceanliner Normandie, at the time the largest and fastest passenger ship afloat, completed her maiden voyage from Le Havre to New York in four days, three hours, and 14 minutes, winning the traditional Blue Riband (an unofficial accolade given to passenger liners for the highest average speed). The Normandie's luxurious interiors were in the latest Art Deco and Streamline Moderne styles, and the Normandie was quickly heralded as a ship for the rich and famous. ![](images/cd47ca04421bf85589589c7f9b471766263a33ae5f83618909343ed34e484187.jpg) Peter Müller-Munk, ca. 1950s. She was also the inspiration for an elegant, chrome-plated, brass pitcher designed by Peter Müller-Munk (1907–1967), the New York silversmith mentioned earlier. The Normandie Pitcher, shaped like the prow of its namesake, was made by the Revere Copper and Brass Company in Rome, New York, and would be produced until 1941. Müller-Munk was born in Germany, studied as a silversmith at the University of Berlin, and immigrated to the United States in 1926. He worked at Tiffany's in New York as a metalworker from 1926 to 1928, exhibiting at the 1928 Macy's exposition. In 1929, he established his own silver studio and exhibited at the 1929 and 1930 Metropolitan Museum shows. Now Müller-Munk was jumping into the nascent industrial design education field. At Carnegie Tech, in Pittsburgh, upon finishing the first year of the degree program he initiated, Donald Dohner was contacted by his former employer in the Pittsburgh public schools, James Boudreau, who had become dean of Pratt Institute's Art School in 1928. Boudreau wanted Dohner to organize and lead an industrial design program at Pratt, similar to the one he had just started at Carnegie. Having also worked with Boudreau as a lecturer and consultant at Pratt in 1932, Dohner accepted, and Müller-Munk was called in to replace him as head of the program at Carnegie. In the fall, Müller-Munk and Alexander Kostellow would continue Dohner's curriculum and practice of taking students to tour local manufacturing facilities and having them design products based on the production methods they had seen. As a matter of fact, it is this unique accommodation of the client manufacturer's production and economic limitations that distinguished industrial designers from their predecessors, the pure artists and industrial artists. Pioneer industrial designers were developing the unique philosophy of industrial design as a multidisciplinary process that was not only art, but a balance of economics, engineering, materials selection, and human convenience considerations. Their other distinguishing characteristic was their commitment to modern design, rather than the imitation of traditional period design styles, still clung to by many industrial artists, and the most modern style, now known as streamlining, was at the forefront of transportation design. On May 29, 1935, the first of a series of streamlined Hiawatha trains, the Twin Cities Hiawatha, began service from Chicago, through Milwaukee, to the twin cities of Minneapolis and St. Paul in morning and afternoon runs. Operated by the Milwaukee Railroad to compete with the Burlington Twin Cities Zephyr and North Western Railway's Twin Cities 400, the Class A 4–4–2 steam locomotive, built by the American Locomotive Company (ALCO), was designed by ALCO design consultant Otto Kuhler (1884–1977) with a nose form similar to the Burlington Zephyr, but without the front cab windows, the cab being in the rear as with all traditional steam locomotives. The nose was graced with horizontal stainless steel wings with a red engine number at its center. Kuhler also designed the famous Hiawatha “beaver tail" observation car at the rear, and all the interiors down to the napkins and draperies. Kuhler was born near Essen, Germany. At age 19 he was commissioned to illustrate a catalog of steam locomotives. His auto body design for a Mercedes chassis won a gold medal in 1913. He served in the German army in World War I, after which he enrolled in the Academy of Art in Dusseldorf to learn etching. He was employed as a stylist with N.A.G. in Berlin and designed sets for the silent film The Tunnel, then became associate editor of Der Motor magazine. He styled car bodies for Snutzel Pére & Fils in Brussels and a 1914 roadster for Fiat in Italy. Kuhler immigrated to the United States in 1923 and worked as a commercial artist in Pittsburgh. After becoming a citizen in 1928, he opened a studio in Manhattan, promoting the streamlining of railroad equipment and working for the J.G. Brill Company styling trains and trolley cars, including a smooth front-end trolley called the Brill Bullet in 1931. In 1932, the American Locomotive Company (ALCO) commissioned him as a design consultant. In April 1935, Business Week magazine announced that “Depression is a forgotten word in the automobile industry, which is forging ahead in production, retail sales, and expansion of productive capacity in a manner reminiscent of the twenties." At the Ford Motor Company in Detroit, the young styling department established in 1933 under Eugene T. “Bob" Gregorie had been struggling under the dominance of engineers, as the one at Chrysler had been. Ford stylists were subjugated to body engineers, who resented the stylist's role in design, and to Charlie Sorensen, who placed speed of production over beauty and style. Both were under the powerful Henry Ford, who staunchly resisted modernization and change of any type. But like Earl, Gregorie had a corporate patron who understood the growing importance of automotive appearance: Henry's son, Edsel (1893–1943), who was a sophisticated patron of the arts, and who had an excellent eye for design. Edsel was determined to win control of styling for Gregorie's department and assisted Gregorie in battles with engineers and production supervisors. Unlike GM, which had a complex bureaucracy, Ford had only Henry to deal with, and only Edsel to counter him. So Gregorie needed only to convince Edsel of his designs. Edsel had seen the styling disaster of the 1934 Chrysler Airflow by engineers, so when it was time to design the new, low-cost 1936 Lincoln, Edsel made sure Bob Gregorie was in full charge. The early concept for the car was the Briggs Dream Car, designed by John Tjaarda at Briggs for Ford at the 1933 Chicago Century of Progress Exposition, which was a rear-engine car not unlike a sleeker version of Ferdinand Porsche's Kleinauto then under development in Germany, with a front sloping hood that now reminded people of the discredited Chrysler Airflow. Edsel was also concerned and thought the design was "too Teutonic," knowing the German origins of the Kleinauto. Tjaarda was reluctant to change the design, so Edsel called Gregorie in to work with Tjaarda to resolve the dilemma. Gregorie decided to change the front end by relocating the motor to the front and enclosing it in a hood that was gracefully shaped like the inverted prow of a ship with a V-shaped grille. Gregorie's experience in boat design had contributed to an inspiring styling innovation. The fenders and teardrop rear end were retained, but with the ![](images/d8626a91c4660f0586a8ebb13741cd437f68e1680aca45b9f7ae995ec248e4bc.jpg) Lincoln Zephyr, 1936. new prowlike front end, the front fenders seemed to suggest water being sprayed up and to the sides from the prow. The final design would be introduced as the 1936 Lincoln Zephyr, capitalizing on the name of the popular streamlined Burlington Zephyr train. It would be hailed by many as the first commercially successful streamlined car, and its unique front-end design would be imitated by dozens of competitors over the next five years. It was indeed a watershed of automotive design and in fact remains a classic design today. Another transportation marvel was introduced in 1935, the Scarab, engineered by aviation engineer William Bushnell Stout (1880-1956), with the exterior designed by John Tjaarda. Unlike any car of the time, the Scarab was what many call the first minivan, with a large interior compartment with a flexible seating system that could accommodate up to ten passengers. Stout envisioned it as a mobile office. To create the space, he did away with the normal chassis and drive shaft by using a rear engine and a unitized body structure, permitting a low flat-floor interior. The body design was without fenders, presenting a fully streamlined aluminum aircraft-type shell with built-in headlights. Although the Scarab was offered for sale at \$5,000 (a Chrysler Airflow then sold for \$1,345) and up to a hundred a year were to be manufactured, only nine were actually made. The major star at the November 1935 New York Auto Show was the Cord 810 Winchester Sedan, designed by Gordon M. Buehrig (1904-1990) and made by the Auburn-Cord-Duesenberg Automobile Company in Connersville, Indiana. Cord was the brand name, after the owner of all three brands, Erret Lobban Cord, whose philosophy was to build innovative, different cars. The Cord 810 was indeed innovative and different. It featured front-wheel drive, a rear-hinged hood, retractable hidden headlights, absence of a running board, no visible grille, and was offered with a supercharger. It was highly streamlined, with a rounded, boxlike hood featuring a grille of horizontal louvers that wrapped around the lower half of the box, which some dubbed the “cofin." The crowds at the New York show were so dense that visitors had to stand on the bumpers of nearby cars to get a look. Many orders were taken at the show, but the cars would not be ready for delivery until April 1936, due to problems of production. Over 1,000 were sold the first year. In 1936, Buehrig would also design the 1937 Cord 812, which in 1951 would be displayed by the Museum of Modern Art as a work of art. But by 1937, Errett Cord's empire would collapse and all production would cease, another casualty of the Depression. In 1936, Buehrig would go to the Edward G. Budd Company in Philadelphia as chief designer of prototypes. ![](images/dd1f8a2a7bfe9e3f1af785fc0a35adc5bd8908ae4ae4d142cd8918aded19e469.jpg) 1936 Cord by Buehrig. Buehrig was born in Mason City, Illinois, and attended Bradley Polytechnic in Peoria before becoming an apprentice at the Gotfredson Body Company, Detroit, in 1924. He worked for Dietrich, Inc., in 1926, then at Packard, and in 1927 was one of the first employees hired by Harley Earl at the newly established Art and Colour Section at GM. In 1928 he worked for the Stutz Company in Indianapolis, and in 1929 became chief body designer for Auburn-Cord-Duesenberg, where he designed the Duesenberg Model J line of luxury cars. In 1933 he returned to GM, and in 1934 he returned to Auburn-Cord-Duesenberg. Harold Van Doren and his partner, John Gordon Rideout, had developed a growing industrial design business in Toledo, Ohio. In 1933, they had designed a bright green, plastic, skyscraper-style table radio for Air King Products, a large Magnalite (cast aluminum with varying thicknesses, allowing for maximum heat conductivity) teakettle for Wagner Manufacturing Company (designed by Rideout), and plastic stemware for the National Silver Company. In 1934 they designed a Magnalite aluminum covered casserole for Wagner, and a trendsetting sheet-metal gasoline pump for the Wayne Pump Company. In 1935, they designed a lightweight, modern Sentinel Duplex retail scale for Toledo Scale, using the largest plastic molding of the time, and developed an entire line of streamlined children's bicycles, tricycles, scooters, and wagons for the American National Company, including the Skippy-Racer Scooter (1933), the Skippy Sno-Plane Sled and the Skippy Stream Line Racer Wagon (1934), and the Streamline Velocipede and the Pioneer Streamlined Tot Bike (1935). Now even kids could enjoy streamlined transportation! However, in 1935 Rideout left Van Doren to open his own office in Cleveland, Ohio, John Gordon Rideout and Staff, and hired Arthur N. BecVar (1911–2003), who had graduated from Notre Dame University in 1933 with a BFA, received an engineering certificate from Purdue University, and an MFA at the Institute of International Education, Charles University, Prague, Czechoslovakia, in 1933–1934. With Rideout gone, Van Doren renamed his Toledo firm Harold Van Doren & Associates. The reason for Rideout's move was not business, nor problems with Van Doren, but the more romantic result of his relationship with Alice Chalifoux (1908–2008), a concert harpist with the Cleveland Symphony Orchestra and head of the harp department at the Cleveland Institute of Music, whom he married in 1937. ![](images/95d2a908037e07b1bd08611d01b8e75bfe5ffbc2093e7f77f9881afdfddb77ba.jpg) Arthur N. BecVar, ca. 1970s (courtesy Industrial Designers Society of America). The 1930s saw the development of all kinds of plastics, new synthetic materials with integral coloring that could reduce weight as well as replace many metal parts, thus avoiding the costs of metal machining, finishing, and painting. Before the 1930s, the only plastic used in products was Bakelite, a thermosetting phenol formaldehyde resin developed in 1907 by Belgian-born chemist Leo Baekeland in New York. But it was only available in black, dark brown, or dark maroon, not particularly attractive consumer colors. The new plastics of the 1930s offered a full palette of colors. Typical was the urea formaldehyde called Plaskon used by Van Doren and Rideout in the white Toledo scales, mentioned above. Polystyrene and acrylic, the latter called by trade names Plexiglas or Lucite, had been developed in 1930. Polyethylene was developed in 1933, nylon in 1934 by DuPont, polyester in 1936, and melamine formaldehyde, called Melamine, and Dacron, both in 1938. Plastics transformed the look and feel of consumer products. Not only that, but plastics revolutionized the manufacturing process. New injection molding machines melted a stream of small pellets of raw plastic into semiliquid form and forced it into complex steel molds. It quickly cooled back into solid form, the finished part was ejected, and the machine would begin the next cycle. Hundreds of parts could be produced within hours. Industrial designer Belle Kogan, who at the time was designing clocks in plastics for the Warren Telecron Company, wrote articles in 1935 plastics magazines urging manufacturers to use plastics, which she described as a material with tremendous possibilities ... which no manufacturer, if he be alert and watchful of his competition, can afford to overlook. Radios, clocks, dishes, jewelry—all being developed today—have an enormous significance. Kogan also pointedly advised manufacturers to study the “feminine viewpoint," citing the fact that thirty million women—all potential customers-—constituting practically the entire buying structure of the nation—comprise a force, which cannot, or should not, be disregarded. The tastes of the American woman, her reaction to color and form, are of vital importance to the manufacturer.⁶ In another article, Kogan also advised designers to do their homework: It is the designer's job, therefore, to know materials, to know their qualities, their basic structures, their resistance to wear, and their cost—as well as to know markets and trends—in order to intelligently design for the manufacturer.?
