Her articles were typical of others being written by industrial designers in trade magazines—stressing practical, technical, and cost-reduction results of design rather than simply artistic beauty. Kogan had an office in New York, producing houseware designs in silver, aluminum, ceramics, glass, wood and cloth for Quaker Silver Company and many other clients. In 1936 she would design a modern double serving dish in silver for Reed & Barton. Regular commercial air transportation was just beginning, inspired by Charles Lindberg's solo flight across the Atlantic in 1927. The China Clipper, a Martin M-130 four-engine flying boat built for Pan American Airways, initiated mail service on November 22, 1935, from San Francisco to Manila. Its interiors, intended for long Pacific trips (59 hours, 48 minute fying time, with overnight stops at Honolulu, Midway, Wake Island and Guam), with sofa-type seating that converted into sleeping berths, lavatories, bar, kitchen, and pantry, were designed by Norman Bel Geddes in 1934. The twin-engine Douglas DC-3 airliner initiated commercial domestic flights shortly thereafter, on December 17, 1935, after refinement with development models DC-1 (1933) and DC-2 (1934). The DC-3 carried 21 passengers, and a transcontinental flight took about 15 hours, with only three refueling stops. People were astounded at the potential for future air travel. Kem Weber was probably inspired to design his famous Airline chair in 1934 by earlier versions of the DC-3. Weber had labored for months to design a chair that could be sold disassembled and readily put together by the consumer, and the Airline chair was the result. Weighing 17 pounds and packed flat in a box, it was to sell for \$24.95. The chair design failed ![](images/de935f11658edc2710525d8c13e57f58accddc0c6d05fbf1cf54925b95ba451d.jpg) Airline chair, 1934, by Kem Weber. to find a manufacturer, and so craftsmen, rather than machines, built 300 for the Walt Disney Studios in California. Weber is also noted for being the main architect of the Walt Disney Studios complex in Burbank, California. Mickey Mouse had been created in 1928. At the same time that industrial design began to flourish, the Roosevelt administration's New Deal was pouring funds into new relief programs to help the unemployed, to provide social entitlement programs for the elderly, and to allow unions to organize. Industrial design, which was now driving industry, would become a potential political target of the newly empowered, organized American labor movement. 

---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\07_Chapter_07.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `2c59c1255d9e59fc73dcfad74e14fe57fa82a6c329d54bf18d397cace6967d9e`
- characters: 44456

## Labor Design and Design Organization With all the publicity and attention focused on the inevitable march of modern technology and design, as well as on the high unemployment levels, strikes and labor disputes of the time, it was perhaps inevitable that industrial design would become involved with government and the intensive labor activism of the Depression era. In April 1935, the Works Progress Administration (WPA) was created by an act of Congress upon the order of President Franklin Roosevelt as part of the Second New Deal. At the time, there were 20 million persons on relief. In order to employ the unemployed, the act provided \$4.9 billion in funds for the construction of roads, bridges, and local public buildings, but it also employed musicians, artists, actors and writers in public works. A number of artist/designers, including some mentioned in this book, took advantage of WPA grants to paint murals in post offices, universities, and other public buildings. In July, the Wagner National Labor Relations Act validated union authority. By August, the Social Security Act provided retirement benefits, the first federal assistance to the elderly. In September, the Hoover Dam (named after Herbert Hoover, president when it was begun in 1931) was dedicated, creating Lake Mead, the world's largest reservoir at the time, with President Roosevelt presiding. The Roosevelt administration quickly renamed it Boulder Dam, but the confusion between names continued. In 1947, the name would be changed back to Hoover Dam. On December 2, 1935, in New York, a frigid and blustery day, several hundred men and women lined up outside the 35th Street offices of the WPA for a chance to enroll in the Design Laboratory, a pioneering school sponsored by the Federal Art Project (FAP), one of the WPA's cultural projects. It was the first institution to offer free, comprehensive education in modernist design. Its brochure claimed it had been “created to supply a hitherto unfulfilled and pressing need in America" for people with skills to devise, package, and promote consumer products that were styled to sell, thereby stimulating economic recovery. Clearly, this noble purpose was inspired by the incredible success of industrial designers in doing precisely that over the previous eight years. By emphasizing “coordination in the study ofesthetics, industrial products, machine fabrication, and merchandizing," the school would "train designers, not specialized craftsmen, by correlating thorough instruction in the general principles of design and the fine arts with shop practice." Two hundred twenty people enrolled that day, more that the school's administration anticipated, and there were still hundreds more on a waiting list The school was the result of political action by the Depression-era avant-garde, the unprecedented public arts bureaucracy of the New Deal, the militant industrial unionism of the Congress of Industrial Organizations (CIO), which had just been formed within the American Federation of Labor (AFL), the United Auto Workers (UAW), and the radical socialist politics of the Popular Front (a broad coalition of different political groupings, often made up of centrists and leftists). The collaboration was an example of how modernism was viewed by some as a means to control cultural production by political action and thus prevent its dominance by business interests and capitalism. The idea of democratizing American culture was attractive to many elites of the time, who resented the new lucrative art form of industrial design being controlled by big business and, by the way, a handful of industrial design celebrities getting rich in the process. The school organizers recruited well-known industrial designers such as Raymond Loewy, Walter Dorwin Teague, Donald Deskey, George Sakier, and Russel Wright to lend legitimacy to an advisory board by the use of their names, and lured furniture designer Gilbert Rohde to serve as director. It is likely that these men were attracted by the idealistic intention of training young designers, as stated in the brochure, and as desperately needed in the field, rather than by the hidden leftist ideology behind it. This ideology was obvious in major study texts required by the curriculum, including The Theory of the Leisure Class, by Thorstein Veblen, published in 1899, which claimed that reform of industrial art had to be through the technology of machines, and highly influential sociologist/historian Lewis Mumford's book, Technics and Civilization (1934). Mumford considered streamlining as mere expressive and “outmoded decoration” and insisted that functionalist esthetics (such as the geometric abstract forms at the Bauhaus) was more appropriate for society, a view shared and promulgated by the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA), which venerated Bauhaus architects, and which would enthusiastically take up the cause of functional esthetics. The recent closure of the leftist Bauhaus by the Nazis as “a Marxist manifestation” generated much political sympathy throughout the liberal and progressive art community, which actively sought to bring exiled Bauhaus refugees to the United States to lead the modern movement they had initiated in Europe. Support of the Bauhaus was seen by some as a gesture of patriotic defiance against Nazism and war. Others saw communism as a similar deterrent to the evils of fascism, since Hitler's government was fervently anti-communist, and communists were fighting incessantly against Hitler and fascism. Despite internal political and philosophical differences of faculty members, the school implemented a provisional three-year design curriculum in August 1936, which promised to “present the ... standards of taste and style ... in the so-called International Style"” (the term invented and promoted by MoMA starting in 1932 and based on the Bauhaus). At the same time, an essay by the Federal Art Project (FAP) historian published in The Magazine of Art in October 1936 denigrated the “handful of super-men who are revolutionizing everything from hairpins to locomotives at a fabulous price," an unmistakable reference to the leading industrial designers. Many students and faculty of the Design Laboratory joined militant organizations of cultural workers, including those where communists, socialists, or Trotskyites (those supporting the political and economic theories of communism advocated by Leon Trotsky and his followers, usually including the principle of worldwide revolution) predominated, and instigated strikes and demonstrations demanding autonomy of the school, calling for a “United Front against War and Fascism," all of which put them at odds with FAP administrators. In December, several students and faculty were arrested for public protests and demonstrations.' Despite the labor unrest and strikes, 1936 was dawning with signs of economic recovery. The main economic indicators had regained the levels of the late 1920s, except for unemployment, still high at 11 percent, though this was much lower than the 25 percent of 1933. Industrial production had recovered significantly, and many businesses were getting back to normal, thus increasing design activity. In 1936, Montgomery Ward volume rose from 25 percent to 82 percent over Sears.2 By then, Anne Swainson was supervising 30 designers at Montgomery Ward's Bureau of Design, many of whom went on to become prominent industrial designers. Among the first was Dave Chapman, who had served as head of the Bureau's product design department. Chapman opened his own office in Chicago in 1936, which would become the largest industrial design firm in Chicago. Chapman had married his wife, Eileen, in about 1935. Šhe was the daughter of the chief legal counsel for Montgomery Ward at the time.3 Famous celebrity industrial designers were being called upon to serve in positions of civic responsibility. In New York City, mayor Fiorello La Guardia had appointed Norman Bel Geddes to the Municipal Arts Committee of New York in 1935,4 and Walter Dorwin Teague was appointed as one of the eight directors on the New York World Fair's Board of Design in 1936, as planning began for the 1939 event. In 1935, Bel Geddes designed and produced Dead End in New York. He toured the city in police cars for atmosphere and recorded river and street sounds for four days for use in the play's background. He had hired Robert Gruen (1913–1999) that year as a set designer, and Gruen would open his own glassware design office in New York in 1940. Also in 1935, Egmont Arens established his own design firm in New York, promoting what he called "industrial humaneering," emphasizing simplicity and function. John M. Little of Minneapolis joined Van Doren & Associates in Toledo in 1936. Little knew Van Doren from Minneapolis, where Van Doren had been assistant director of the Minneapolis Institute of Arts (MIA) until 1930, and Little had opened an industrial design office there in 1931. In 1937 Donald Dailey (1914-1997) would join Van Doren's firm as design director. Dailey was also from Minneapolis and had attended art classes at the MIA. He had also studied marketing and engineering at the University of Toledo (1934–1936) and design at the Toledo Museum School of Design (1936–1937). Henry Dreyfuss had been retained in 1934 by the Hoover Company to design vacuum cleaners. His annual retainer fee was \$25,000 (\$435,000 in 2013 dollars), and his first design, the Hoover Model 150 upright vacuum cleaner, debuted in 1936, beginning Dreyfuss's association with Hoover that would last for 20 years. That year, he also designed a typewriter for Royal, accessories for United Airlines DC-3s, and his first streamlined Mercury K-5 Pacific train design for the New York Central Railroad, marketed as the “Train of Tomorrow." The first Mercury in the series operated daily between Cleveland and Detroit starting in 1936. ![](images/1491905a963ab8aebabf59e88e0aab28cb0ad69931e25aed6c01b5f22e93bd84.jpg) Hoover Model 150, 1936, by Henry Dreyfuss. For the locomotive, Dreyfuss covered the exterior pipes, whistles, and fittings with a smooth, “bathtub” cowl, with cutaway sides to display the driving wheels and rods, which were dramatized at night by concealed spotlights under the cowling on either side. Dreyfuss also designed the interiors on the train, including the coaches, kitchen, dining car, smoking lounge, parlor car, and observation car, all with arrangements designed to avoid the traditional “linear" look of railroad coaches and to have the atmosphere of a private club. For example, the dining car was in three sections, two with conventional seating, but the middle section was made up of tables for two seated side-by-side, facing the center aisle. In the rounded-end observation car, Dreyfuss lowered the windowsills to maximize the view through four-foot-high windows. Instead of having seating arranged around the walls, facing in, he placed the seating in the center, facing out toward the windows. Later versions of the Mercury included the Chicago-Detroit Mercury and the Chicago Cincinnati Mercury. The first Cleveland-Detroit Mercury would last run in 1959. Dreyfuss was not the only train designer. In February 1936, Raymond Loewy, with employee Carl Otto (b. ca. 1910–ca. 1986 ), hired in 1935, designed the Pennsylvania Railroad's K-4s streamlined shroud for steam locomotive No. 3768, hiding most of the mechanical details. Otto was born Carl Louis Otto in Michigan and was trained as an engineer at Michigan State University. He began his career as a draftsman with Duesenberg in Auburn, Indiana, and then went to New York to work for Norman Bel Geddes. Loewy did not follow the “bathtub” streamlining shrouds of previous trains, but decided to retain the unique character of the traditional, cylindrical boilers of steam locomotives. He basically designed a bullet nose for a cylindrical boiler and placed it upon a streamlined platform covering the driver wheels that suggested the top half of a bullet. He would refine the design into the S-1 in 1939. Loewy had also acquired the Studebaker account in 1935 and was retained for the design of exteriors of the 1937 models, at the same time as Helen Dryden, former art director of the Dura Company in Ohio, who had been retained at \$100,000 per year for Studebaker car interior designs since 1934, worked on the interior. At the time, she was acclaimed as “the highest-paid woman artist." Loewy's exterior design, completed in 1936, featured a narrow, vertical grille with horizontal louvers, the top three of which extended rearward along the side of the hood. Headlights were teardrop shaped. Dryden was publicly credited in Studebaker ads as “the stylist” for the 1937 Studebaker, but she was dropped as a consultant that same year. Loewy would continue to design Studebaker cars until the company went out of business in 1961. In 1935, Walter Dorwin Teague pioneered in the branding of gasoline service stations when he designed his first for Texaco, which included not just the logotype, but a functional, rain-protected architecture and layout that would become a standard benchmark for all service stations over the next two decades. In 1936, he also designed the Kodak Bantam Special camera in classic Art Deco style for his longtime client. That same year, Brooks Stevens designed a custom Zephyr "Land Yacht" for William Woods Plankinton, consisting of a streamlined truck cab and 17-foot hitchable trailer with an electric icebox, full kitchen with electric stove, bathroom with shower, living room, hunting and fishing equipment, and sleeping quarters for eight. The entire length of the rig was 45 feet, displaying a most modern and exotic mobile home for its well-to-do owner. Norman Bel Geddes in 1936 was busy designing exhibits for the 1939 World's Fair, a truck for the Autocar Company, and the first all-metal soda dispenser for the Walter Kidde Corporation, as well as exhibiting his designs at the Museum of Science and Industry in Chicago and designing stage sets for The Eternal Road in New York.6 In June 1936, Carnegie Tech graduated the first five students ever to receive a college degree in industrial design. When degrees were awarded alphabetically at graduation, the first to receive a degree was Maud (Bowers) Rice (1913–1998), a Philippine national. Donald Dohner was now at Pratt Institute in Brooklyn, starting a three-year program in industrial design, with the first graduates due in 1939. Peter Müller-Munk now headed the Art and Design Department at Carnegie. Alexander Kostellow, of the Carnegie industrial design faculty, and his wife, Rowena Reed, would depart to join Dohner at Pratt in 1938. Kostellow would soon become a prominent and influential faculty member in the Pratt program.7 In 1936, the Homer Laughlin China Company introduced a line of mass-produced ceramic tableware for the home called Fiestaware, designed by Frederick Hurten Rhead (1880– 1942). The brightly colored, mix-and-match, modern line was highly successful in the market and is still a classic design valued highly by collectors. Rhead had emigrated from the United Kingdom in 1902 and worked for several ceramics factories, including Roseville, where he was director from 1904 to 1908; Jervis Pottery on Long Island; and from 1917 to 1927, he directed research at American Encaustic Tiling Company in Zanesville, Ohio. From 1927 until his death, he would remain as art director for Homer Laughlin. In July 1936, the Spanish Civil War began, and in November, Franklin Roosevelt was elected by a landslide to his second term as president of the United States. Unemployment was still at 14 percent. Roosevelt would initiate payroll taxes to pay for Social Security, and with the threat of unrest in Europe, he would establish the Neutrality Act intended to avoid any involvement in taking sides in disputes between nations. In Chicago, Lawrence H. Whiting, director of the American Furniture Mart, invited a group of furniture designers to a luncheon at the Furniture Club to hear speakers Donald Deskey, Gilbert Rohde, Kem Weber, and Leo Jiranek, all mentioned earlier. Possibly influenced by the Wagner Act of 1935, which established federal rights of workers to organize unions, to engage in collective bargaining, and to take part in strikes, attendees at the 1936 meeting at the Furniture Club agreed to form a new organization: the Designers' Institute of the American Furniture Mart (DIAFM).8 This appeared to be intended as a union of furniture designers, since a main requirement of membership restricted member design activities to furniture design, a restriction that was not appreciated by many industrial designers, who were designing everything from locomotives to wash machines. As we noted earlier in the formation of the Design Laboratory, this was a time of intense labor unrest. From September 1936 to May 1937, half a million workers were involved in sit-down strikes nationally. A strike against Republic Steel resulted in four deaths and 84 injuries. By the spring of 1937, the WPA designated the Design Laboratory as one of the many programs to be discontinued, as the school struggled with layoffs, violence, and confusion. A radical, leftist, professional trade union, the Federation of Architects, Engineers, Chemists and Technicians (FAECT) came to the rescue as the new sponsor and to provide direction of the school. In the summer session, the curriculum was revamped along the lines of the Bauhaus Foundations Course and was turned into a four-year BA and BFA program comparable to standard universities. Enrollment increased to 400, and administrators hoped it would become a "labor university." Other private educational institutions of design were being established or initiated at the same time. The University of Illinois at Champaign-Urbana established a BFA program of industrial design in 1937. Cooper Union in New York offered a four-year certificate in plastic design in 1937, with instructors Paul Feeley and Lila Ulrich, a Bauhaus graduate. The California Graduate School of Design in Pasadena organized a two-year graduate program in industrial design with advice from Richard F. Bach of the Metropolitan Museum of Art, Royal Bailey Farnum of the Rhode Island School of Design, Peter Müller-Munk of the Carnegie Institute ofTechnology, and architect George Howe. It was the only graduate program of industrial design in the country. Graduate engineers were taught the principles of product engineering and industrial design. Architect Hunt Lewis was named initial director of the school, and he invited as faculty members Walter P. Baermann (1903–1972), a 1932 émigré from Munich who had served as educational director of the Springfield (Massachusetts) Art Museum; Karl With (1891–1980), a German art historian and educator who had fled the Nazi regime in 1933; and Kem Weber, who also taught at the Art Center School of Design since 1932, and who had recently designed his famous 1934 Airline chair. Baermann was soon appointed director, and he remained in that position until 1941, when the school was merged into the graduate engineering curriculum of the California Institute of Technology (CalTech) and renamed the California School of Design.9 At the Cranbrook Academy of Art near Detroit, Charles Eames (1907–1978) joined the faculty in 1937 and headed the department of experimental design to explore new materials such as plastics and plywood for furniture. Eames was born in St. Louis and studied architecture at Washington University from 1924 to 1926. He worked with an architectural firm in St. Louis from 1925 to 1927, and then as a partner in the firm Gray & Eames from 1930 to 1937. At Cranbrook, Eero Saarinen (1910–1961), the son of Cranbrook's founder, Finnish architect Eliel Saarinen (1873–1950), had graduated from Yale in architecture in 1934 and joined his father in 1935. In 1937, Eero began working with Eames, along with Italian émigré Harry Bertoia (1915–1978). With the expansion of industrial design education and the intense publicity generated by the new profession, men and women were entering the field in scores. Counting the automotive, railroad, airline, appliance, and furniture industries, the population of industrial designers was probably in the hundreds by this time. The purpose of this book is to focus on those pioneers who were active in the profession before 1935, so while important designers from this point on will be mentioned, their biographies and careers will be minimal, and the text will focus on those earliest dozens of machine designers that are of specific interest in this book. German refugees from the Bauhaus were flocking to America. Bauhaus master Josef Albers (1888–1976) and his wife, Anni (1899–1994), had arrived in 1933 to become faculty members at Black Mountain College in North Carolina. Walter Gropius, former director of the Bauhaus, who had opened an office in London after his resignation from the Bauhaus in 1928, arrived in the United States in 1937 to become a professor in the graduate program at the Harvard School of Architecture, along with his Bauhaus student, Marcel Breuer (1902– 1981). Làszló Moholy-Nagy, also of the Bauhaus but in exile in London at the time, became, upon the recommendation of Gropius, director of the School of Design at the Association of Arts and Industries in Chicago, which he opened in 1937 and promptly renamed the New Bauhaus: American School of Design. Hin Bredendieck (1904-1995), a former Bauhaus student; Georgy Kepes (1906–2001), a Hungarian designer who had worked for Moholy-Nagy in his Berlin studio; and Alexander Archipenko (1887-1964), a Russian artist and sculptor who had worked in Berlin and immigrated to the United States in 1929, all joined Moholy-Nagy at the school. The school attracted only 20 students and would close in 1938 under financial difficulties, but it would be reopened in 1939 as the School of Design, under the patronage of Walter Paepcke, president of the Container Corporation of America. Many industrial designers were enjoying a busy year in 1937. Detroit industrial design consultant George Walker began designing Nash automobiles, a client he would work for until 1945. Brooks Stevens designed a Model B tractor for Allis-Chalmers, which was lighter and cheaper than its key competitor, Ford's Farmall, and a streamlined sales van for the Western Printing and Lithograph Company based on his 1936 Zephyr land yacht design. Henry Dreyfuss designed a whitewall Double Eagle automotive tire for Goodyear. Bell Labs introduced the “Phone of the Future” that Dreyfuss had been working on since 1930— a black phenolic plastic 300 tabletop phone that combined the transmitter and receiver into a single combined handset resting in a horizontal cradle. It replaced the “candlestick” model that had been introduced in 1916, and which, in 1919, had a rotary dial added. The classic 300 would be produced until 1950. Otto Kuhler, working with the Baltimore & Ohio (B&O) Railroad as art director of the BO magazine, designed its logo and its 1937 streamlined, bullet-nosed, steam locomotives, known as the “Kuhler type," for B&O's Royal Blue train on the Washington to New York run. The locomotive, a 4–6–2 “Pacific" Class, was painted light gray and royal blue. Time magazine reported it as “swashbuckling” and as “symbolizing the new era in railroading." The design was virtually copied by Raymond Loewy for his 1939 S-1 locomotive. Unfortunately, the Royal Blue line only ran as far as Jersey City, and passengers had to be transported to Manhattan by White Motor Company buses, which Kuhler also designed. Despite all the streamlined shrouds designed for steam locomotives, the days of steam engines were numbered. Diesel power would replace it. In May 1937, as opening ceremonies for San Francisco's Golden Gate Bridge transpired, the flagship of the Acheson, Topeka, & Santa Fe Railway, the Super Chief, began regular service between Chicago and Los Angeles. Departing Los Angeles at 7:30 a.m., it reached Chicago in 36 hours and 49 minutes, setting a record by averaging 60 mph, often reaching 100 mph. Called the “Train of the Stars" because of the many celebrities from Hollywood it carried, it had been under development since 1935, when General Motors' Electro-Motive Corporation (EMC) delivered two diesel-electric units, not much more than boxes on wheels. Just before regular service was initiated, the heavyweight standard cars were replaced with lightweight stainless-steel cars built by the Budd Company in Philadelphia, including a dome-topped observation deck in one. The streamlined engine was distinguished by its raised, forward-facing operating cab, with split windows similar to an automobile or airliner. This was not unusual, considering that the General Motors Art and Colour Section department under Harley Earl executed the styling. A dramatic “warbonnet" color scheme of a red nose and cab with a bright yellow cartouche with Santa Fe logo was styled by Leland Knickerbocker from the GM Art and Colour Section. Philadelphia architects Paul Cret and his partner, John Harbeson, designed the Budd Company car interiors. The Super Chief would operate until Amtrak would take over the nation's passenger service in May 1971. Raymond Loewy in 1937 considered himself an expert in locomotive design, and at the height of his involvement with the Pennsylvania Railroad, he published a 7.5 × 10 inch book with 125 illustrations, The New Vision Locomotive, showing the aesthetics of locomotive evolution, and including photos of his K-4 design as it was evolving into the S-1. That year, Loewy also designed a less dramatic cream separator for International, and a Purma Special camera for Purma Cameras, Ltd., an English company. Sept. 9, 1941 H. L. HAMILTON ET AL LOCONOTIVE BODY Filed July 18, 1941 Des. 129,410 ![](images/a0c1f5e64dd6590b68fcc4899679680946e1acd735e6866d5bad2b6f670b0fec.jpg) Santa Fe Super Chief, 1937 (Patent No. D129,410, U.S. Patent Office). Peter Müller-Munk, who had headed the Carnegie Tech degree program in industrial design since 1935, now also began to practice professionally in the open market by designing an electric, two-speed kitchen Blendor (with an o) under the Waring name in 1937. The liquidblending device, invented by Frederick J. Osius, was named after its financial backer, prominent bandleader Fred Waring (1900–1984), and was introduced at the National Restaurant Show in 1937 for bartender use, but it would soon become a popular home appliance. Müller-Munk's design, called the Miracle Mixer, was classic Art Deco, the base of the blender a cylindrical waterfall of chrome. By 1938, Müller-Munk would organize his independent design firm, Peter Müller-Munk Associates (PMMA), in Pittsburgh, hiring many of his own industrial design students and graduates from the Carnegie Tech program. Another classic appliance design was introduced in 1937, the KitchenAid Model K stand mixer, designed by Egmont Arens for the Hobart Manufacturing Company. Arens had designed previous mixers and coffee grinders for Hobart, but this design of the Model K, except for minor cosmetic changes, would remain relatively unchanged to the present day, a success story exceeding 75 years. Another classic designed that year was the Model XXX (30) tank vacuum cleaner designed in a mod- ![](images/b5f8f360141ce182cb05ca5bd2896ab31ac219becd31a9f25982d4637d87eb01.jpg) Electrolux Model 30, 1937, by Lurelle Guild (Patent No. D106,662, U.S. Patent Office). ernistic style by Lurelle Guild for Electrolux. Millions would be sold between 1937 and 1954, the longest run of any vacuum cleaner design of the era, at a retail price of \$69.95. The long-term success of many product designs of the 1930s seems to conflict with the concept described in 1932 by Elmo Calkins of “planned obsolescence," which predicted that annual design changes spur consumer spending. But the longevity of certain product designs can be explained by several reasons. First, while many consumers are eager to obtain newer versions of a product, there are also some who are not and who will keep and use a favored product until it expires mechanically. Second, many new, industrial-designed products, for the first time in the 1930s, acquired a shape, style, and configuration that seemed "right" for its assigned task, establishing what is called a “typeform," that is, a form that seemed to many the ideal for a particular type, or category, of product. Other manufacturing competitors, noting the popular success of a particular brand, would imitate the design, therefore increasing public familiarity and acceptance of the standard typeform until some substantially different, more popular, configuration replaced it as the new typeform for that particular product. The KitchenAid mixer survives because it is regarded as the ideal typeform for this particular category of products. The power of historically successful designs is illustrated by the automotive industry, which has had dozens of different, successive typeforms over the years, but would remain nostalgic about car designs of the 1930s, which produced the first designs that were truly modern. Detroit in the late 1990s and early 2000s would develop a number of so-called retro designs, intending to recall that period, and which were intended to replicate the original typeforms. They included the (new) Volkswagen Beetle (1994), the Plymouth Prowler (1994), Chrysler's PT (Personal Transportation) Cruiser (1999), Buick's 2001 Blackhawk, Ford's 2002 Forty-Nine, and Chevrolet's HHR (Heritage High Roof) in 2006, all in 1930s styles. But in the late 1930s, the problem was too many cars, and not enough roads. Since half of all households now owned an automobile, the U.S. road system was being challenged by an increasing number of cars on narrow, crowded roads. It was an obvious problem addressed by Norman Bel Geddes, on assignment for the J. Walter Thompson advertising agency in 1937. Based on his experience with his scale-model racetrack of the 1920s, and his war game of the early 1930s, he designed a model City of Tomorrow with expressways featured in a national advertising campaign for the Shell Oil Company. The model would be expanded to become the Futurama exhibit for General Motors at the 1939 New York World's Fair, which in turn would inspire the future federal interstate highway system. Jumping rom highway systems to cocktail parties, Bel Geddes also designed a Skyscraper cocktail shaker and a Manhattan serving tray in 1937. The automobile industry was transformed during the Depression. Car production fell from 5.6 million in 1929 to 1.4 million in 1932. Dozens of auto manufacturing brands in 1929 were reduced to 10 in 1935, which included the Big Three of Ford, Chrysler, and General Motors. GM, with five new models since 1930, led the pack and in 1936 was unionized. In 1937, GM's Art and Colour Section would change its name to the Styling Division. Harley Earl had a staff of 200 designers, cranking out annual model changes on the fleet of GM cars. Earl supervised five studios, one for each division (Cadillac, Buick, Oldsmobile, Pontiac, and Chevrolet), as well as 12 special studios. Yet he wanted more designers, and so GM and its Fisher Body Division initiated a talent search by sponsoring and promoting a national, 1:12 scale model (one inch equals one foot) “Dream Car” design competition that awarded college scholarships to the best original student designs for a four-door sedan (later various different body styles were added). It was called the Fisher Body Craftsman's Guild, which would continue for 30 more years, encouraging a new generation of automotive designers, many of whom would end up in Detroit, and not a few in Earl's studios. Actually, the contest was a major addition to an earlier Fisher Body Craftsman's Guild contest that was initiated in 1930, not to innovate designs, but to accurately replicate Fisher plans for a miniature scale model (1:15 to 1:18, where one inch equaled 1.25 to 1.6 feet) of a 19th-century horse-drawn Napoleonic coach, the trademark adopted by Fisher in 1923, and which appeared on all production Fisher bodies made for GM. There were two competitive categories: the Junior Division (ages 12 through 15) and the Senior Division (ages 16 through 19). Winners won prize money for a scholarship to a college of their choice: \$5,000 for each of the top two winners in each category. That was more than double what a man typically earned in a year! The Napoleonic coach contest would continue along with the new model car competition until 1940, so applicants could choose either one to enter, but the Napoleonic contest ended at that time. Students would no longer be interested in historical craftsmanship, but in futuristic cars. In 1935, Amos Northup, automotive designer for the Murray Corporation of America, the independent Detroit auto body manufacturer, had patented his famous “shark-nose” hood design, which generally resembled its namesake. The design would be copied by most manufacturers in the late 1930s, including the 1938 Graham, the 1937 Willys “windswept” design, the 1936 Lincoln Zephyr, and the 1940 Studebaker, Chrysler, Plymouth, and Ford. But in 1937, Amos Northup died at age 48 as a result of slipping on ice and fatally cracking his skull. His designs were a powerful influence as cars in the 1930s were transformed from boxes to the sleek forms that are familiar to us today. Most GM production designs during the 1930s were not futuristic, dramatic or innovative, but followed the relatively conservative annual advance of styling. This changed in 1938, when Earl developed a more radical prototype Buick called only the Y-Job. Like the similarly sounding name iPhone by Apple in 2007 that predicted the future appearance of all handheld computer devices, the Y-Job predicted the shape and style of most postwar cars. Why the name "Y-Job"? Most experimental cars were tagged with an "X," sometimes with a name or number. Just to be different, Earl decided to use the letter “Y," the next letter in the alphabet. The Y-Job was designed by Oldsmobile studio head George Snyder under Earl's direct supervision, with highly innovative styling features of hidden headlights, suitcase fenders, low horizontal grille, flush push-button door handles, electric windows, a poweroperated electric convertible top, and a rounded fuselage body without traditional running boards. Most unusual was the Y-Job's low, long silhouette with a 58-inch (less than five feet) height at the top of the split, angled windshield, A dozen closely-spaced, parallel and horizontal chrome strips (speed lines) adorned the lower half of both front and rear fenders, the chrome bumpers wrapped around the body corners, and a chrome “bombsight" hood ornament, which would become a Buick trademark, was the icing on the cake. The Y-Job was never shown to the public until after the war, but Earl used it as his personal transportation and it attracted a lot of public attention that way. Some of its features would appear in the 1938 Cadillac 60 Special, designed by Bill Mitchell (1912–1988), who had been hired by Earl in 1935. But after the war, the 1950 Buick would look almost like a clone of the 1938 Y-Job. In 1938, Earl became a vice president of GM, and he would rule GM styling for the next 20 years. In 2001, Buick would recreate the Y-Job with modern advancements called the Buick Blackhawk, drawing extensively from the Y-Job and the 1939 Buick. It was entirely handmade, cost \$475,000, and would be featured in the 2003 ilm Bad Boys II. On June 15, 1938, Dreyfuss saw the introduction of his design oflocomotive shrouds for the New York Central Railroad's 20th Century Limited, which was a refinement of his Mercury K-5 design of the previous year. The shrouds hid the assembly of boiler valves, pipes, and operating parts of ten new J-3, 4–6–4 Hudson-type steam locomotives. The shrouds, similar to Loewy's K-4 for the Pennsylvania Railroad, featured bullet-nosed boiler shapes, but with a center fin that resembled ancient warrior helmets. Dreyfuss also designed the cars using blue, gray, and silver, and like the K-5, they were designed with asymmetric furnishings to avoid the tunnel look of most train interiors. Probably the most famous American train, the 20th Century Limited was an express train that operated from 1902 to 1967 between Grand Central Station in New York and LaSalle Street Station in Chicago. The new train by Dreyfuss made the trip in 16 hours. That same year, Dreyfuss also designed Model A and Model B tractors for John Deere, a new client he had acquired in 1937. Raymond Loewy was having a banner year in 1938, the year he became an American citizen. He opened a branch office in South Bend, Indiana, to service his major client, Studebaker. To head the group, he appointed Virgil Exner (1909–1973), formerly with Harley Earl at General Motors, who had joined Loewy in 1937. Exner, born in Ann Arbor, Michigan, studied art at Notre Dame but had to drop out in 1928 due to lack of funds. He took a job as an advertising artist at an agency handling the Studebaker account. He was hired by Earl in 1933, where he became styling chief for Pontiac and designed Pontiac's famous “Silver Streak" hood ornament. ![](images/a57674d97c327a6acd73fd389c7f7d2c8310e9b802c3f29a30a7635828452133.jpg) Pennsylvania Railroad's 20th Century Limited, by Henry Dreyfuss. Loewy also hired Clare Hodgman, who had started his career in the early 1930s with General Motors in the Oldsmobile studio, and who had worked for Loewy on the Sears Coldspot refrigerator project in 1932–1935. The first task of the group was to begin work on the 1939 Studebaker Champion, a low-cost model. They also designed a new Studebaker “Big S” logo and emblem. Other Loewy designs that year included a Metro series of light- and medium-duty trucks and vans for the International Harvester Company. ![](images/fb3eb1bbd87756b5c78c7421a45fc85bcefd76c1226718b35bcb68b919b6eb94.jpg) Virgil Exner, ca. 1953. Loewy had established a personal office in London in 1935, but now in 1938, he staffed it to function on a continuous basis, with Carl Otto (d. 1986) as head, along with Claire E. Hodgman. The new expanded office designed a logo for the British Petroleum (BP) Company, while the New York office designed the main hall for the ocean liner Panama. Hodgman worked on the Hillman Minx and the Rootes Sunbeam Talbot, and also designed conceptual racecars for the British Autocar magazine of March 1938. One of Otto's accounts was Standard Auto (Triumph). In 1938, Brooks Stevens designed the first commercially successful steam iron for the Edmilton Company in Milwaukee, the Steam-O-Matic, which was introduced at Macy's in New York and Marshall Field's in Chicago. Viktor Schreckengost, head of the Cleveland School (now Institute) of Art, began creating stage and costume designs for Cleveland theater productions and continued to produce award-winning ceramic sculptures for Cleveland Museum of Art shows. Kem Weber was designing a line of tubular steel chairs for the Lloyd Manufacturing Company in Menominee, Michigan. In Chicago, Ann Swainson, head of Montgomery Ward's Bureau of Design, designed a plastic radio cabinet, and her staff designed special china called Cascade, which was exhibited at the large-scale exhibition on modern design at the Metropolitan Museum of Art, and which became one of Montgomery Ward's most popular patterns. Swainson had traveled in 1937 to her native Sweden, visiting factories, department stores, and art schools, and was impressed with the Swedish government's part-time training of industrial designers in schools and factories. Elsewhere in the United States, other corporations were hiring and engaging industrial designers as part of their merchandizing strategy, seeking increased sales and modern style. In 1938, a major corporation was now expanding its industrial design activity into all visual aspects of corporate identity. Libbey-Owens-Ford (LOF) was formed in 1931 by the merger of Libbey-Owens Sheet Glass Company and another Toledo company, Edward Ford Plate Glass Company, which pioneered in automotive safety glass. In 1937, H. Creston Doner had been invited by LOF to leave his post with Goodrich in Akron, Ohio, to come to Toledo to direct the department of design there starting in 1938, a position he would hold until 1967. He supervised the architectural and interior design operations, as well as promotional campaigns for color and lighting effects; the design of LOF warehouses, showrooms, offices, and retail stores; and the design of a new corporate image for truck fleets, building signs, business forms, and advertising materials. Meanwhile, in 1935, Libbey Glass Manufacturing Company in Toledo had been acquired by Owens-Illinois Glass Company and became a subsidiary. In 1938, Edwin W. Fuerst was appointed to head the design department at Libbey Glass, while his brother, Dwight Fuerst headed the design area for bottling and packaging at Owens-Illinois. Edwin designed Greenbrier pattern stemware for Libbey. ![](images/cf40672fc07c38c29f183e5afbf9a10e24a8af9411cf60d600d088e109f78e43.jpg) Philco refrigerator, 1938, by Harold Van Doren (Patent No. D131,103 U.S. Patent Office). Elsewhere in Toledo, Harold Van Doren & Associates were also quite busy. Van Doren himself had worked as a color consultant to Libbey-Owens-Ford's Vitrolite division in 1937. But in 1938, Van Doren's office was now designing refrigerators for Philco, a Philadelphia company, and personal weighing scales and the Guardian Duplex Scale for the Toledo Scale Company. Another industrial designer, Walter Dorwin Teague, also had clients in Toledo in 1938. He had been called in as a consultant to the Owens-Illinois Glass Company, which had acquired the Libbey Glass Company. Teague was to work with two divisions: bottling and packaging, and structural. He designed an Insulis glass construction block in a Circon pattern for the Owens-Illinois Glass Company's structural division, which then asked him to design the division's offices in the Chrysler Building in New York. Additional design organizations began to be formed. In Chicago in July 1938, the Designer's Institute of the American Furniture Mart (DIAFM), founded in 1936 under the auspices of Lawrence H. Whiting, director of the American Furniture Mart, met for the first time since its founding, “at the instigation of Whiting, Alfred Auerbach, and Richard F. Bach of the Metropolitan Museum of Art."1º The principal speaker this time was John Vassos, then head of design at the Radio Corporation of America (RCA) in New York. In his address, Vassos urged that the organization divorce itself from the patronage and support of the Furniture Mart, because it restricted member work to furniture design. In fact, most members were furniture designers, and Vassos himself designed radios, which were often enclosed in wooden furniture. Vassos suggested the organization extend its practices beyond that of furniture design into consumer products, packaging, automotive design or any number of additional design opportunities then available. The group, comprised of 45 cofounding members, agreed with him. Among them were certainly Alfons Bach, Leo Jiranek, Al Lambach, Ernie Swarts, Belle Kogan, and probably, although not confirmed, Lionel C. Algoren (1896–1984), Kem Weber, Donald Deskey, and Gilbert Rohde. They changed the name of the organization on the spot to the American Designers' Institute (ADI) to indicate that it was a national, not just a Chicago organization. Even though Vassos was not currently a member of the group before his presentation, the group elected him the first president of ADI, which would become incorporated in the state of Illinois in 1940. Belle Kogan was elected secretary and treasurer, and she set up meetings, often with speakers, keeping the minutes and other records. Her only help came from her own office staff. ADI also established a chapter in New York." ![](images/f8d1046e18c40a4d7d40c9783f38c449f50f48af48cb97785f507ee1b26065d4.jpg) ADI logo, 1938. That same year, 1938, another industrial design organization was formed, also in Chicago, which would separate itself to escape from what it saw as furniture designer domination.


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\08_Chapter_08.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `bc518fff6a8160c9d5d26207841e2d2bd03a9b17466fd6682aa944aab2cc6011`

# War Drums and the New York Fair According to sculptor/designer Alfonso Iannelli (mentioned earlier), a group of Chicago designers, including himself, Dave Chapman, "Chick" Waltman, H. Creston Doner, and Jean Reinecke, formed the Chicago Society of Industrial Designers (CSID), which limited its members only to the design of products for mass production, a much more attractive and permissive option to many industrial designers, who were in the process of trying to define their scope and marketability to industry in the broadest manner possible. Prior to and during this period, noted sculptor Iannelli had established Iannelli Studios in Chicago, and in addition to his sculpture projects, he designed consumer products, such as mixers, hair dryers, knife sharpeners, and hair clippers, for the John Oster Manufacturing Company, Sunbeam Electric Company, Wahl Eversharp Company, Mueller Products, and Fairbanks Morse Stoker.1 During 1938, the international situation was growing worse, almost by the day. In March, Germany annexed Austria to Hitler's Third Reich by means of the Anschluss (Connection), with little international resistance. In May, Hitler awarded “cultural prizes" to Fritz Todt for the Autobahn (Auto Way), the first "super highway," of which 3,000 kilometers had already been completed; to Ernest Heinkel for his He 1ll bomber design; to Wilhelm Messerschmitt for his Me 109 fighter plane design; and to Dr. Ferdinand Porsche for his design of the new Type 38 Kraft durch Freude (Strength through Joy) or KdF Volkswagen (People's Car). Porsche had initiated the design in 1932 as the Kleinauto (Small Car), and Hitler subsidized its development when he came to power in 1933. Thirty sample cars were built in 1937 for promotional purposes, orders were taken, and Hitler introduced a sávings scheme where the public could collect stamps that would eventually pay for the car. ![](images/25691095526cb7668cbdd31342e28b500924bc6436907a63bb361236bd859628.jpg) Volkswagen Type 38, 1938. So on May 26, 1938, witnessed by 70,000 people and 150 reporters, Hitler laid the cornerstone of the Volkswagen factory in a small village in Lower Saxony, named the car as described as above, and renamed the village KdF Stadt (KdF City). The first production was to start in September 1939, the same month that World War II would officially start. Then on July 29, 1938, Japan invaded Russia, and on September 29, neutral European nations agreed to German occupation of part of Czechoslovakia, called the Munich Agreement, which British Foreign Secretary Neville Chamberlain called “Peace in Our Time." Only a few Volkswagens were produced before World War II, and those were exclusively for Nazi dignitaries. These dramatic events in Germany demonstrated to many that Hitler threatened world peace and that war was inevitable. To others, however, they demonstrated the incredible technological, organizational, and production capabilities of Germany. This initiated the reputation, still acclaimed today, of“German engineering." Perhaps Germans were “the Master Race," as Hitler claimed, and would lead the world out of the Depression with public works. Most people just wanted America to stay out of yet another European war, because the memories of World War I were still vivid. The U.S. economy had suffered a brief recession in early 1938, but with war already building in Europe, the economy began to recover, and by autumn, it was booming from orders from European countries for arms and war equipment. Into this politically charged international atmosphere stepped the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) with a highly publicized retrospective exhibition, Bauhaus 1919–1928, which ran from December 7, 1938, to January 30, 1939. As we know, numerous faculty and students of the Bauhaus had already immigrated to the United States after Hitler closed the school in 1933. The latest to arrive, in 1938, was Ludwig Mies van der Rohe, the last director of the Bauhaus, to teach design and to design new buildings in the new, MoMA-approved International Style for the Armour (later, Illinois) Institute of Technology in Chicago. Herbert Bayer (1900–1985), a former Bauhaus print shop master who had resigned with Gropius and Moholy-Nagy in 1928, created Nazi publicity campaigns in Germany until he came to the United States in 1938 to work with Walter and Ise Gropius in designing the MoMA exhibition. He had managed to spirit many Bauhaus artworks out of Germany. Bayer would stay here after the exhibition as a consultant to major U.S. corporations, including the Great Ideas of Western Man advertising series for the Container Corporation of America starting in 1946. The Bauhaus exhibit featured the sculptures, housewares, furniture, and architectural renderings of such Bauhaus luminaries as Gropius, Mies, and Marcel Breuer. The show and catalog, edited by Bayer with Ise and Walter Gropius, downplayed the school's Expressionist beginnings and excluded the political turmoil periods under Hannes Meyer and Mies. Mies reportedly was asked to take part but declined. The names of Bauhaus artists still living in Germany were suppressed for fear of reprisals against them, and Alfred Barr, MoMA director, stopped well short of condemning Hitler in his preface to the catalog; equally concerned that the show would be viewed as anti-German and that the Bauhaus would be seen as a “Jewish-Communist cabal," he proposed to include a statement specifying the small number of faculty who were Jewish. Gropius apparently dissuaded him from doing this, but had to be prevailed on in return to omit an assertion that the school under the directorships of himself and Mies "had always been non-political in character." MoMA desired to demonstrate that the Bauhaus had already been reestablished in the United States. The "New Bauhaus: American School of Design," established in Chicago in 1937 by Bauhaus master Làszló Moholy-Nagy, would have been a perfect example to demonstrate this, and perhaps had even been planned by MoMA the year before. But the school had closed in the spring of 1938 and would not reopen until the fall of 1939, although this was not known at the time. It was no longer a good example for MoMA. Students from the Design Laboratory School, based on Bauhaus principles, now urged MoMA to exhibit their work. By the summer of 1938, the left-leaning Federation of Architects, Engineers, Chemists, and Technicians (FAECT) had terminated its sponsorship of the school due to Depression cutbacks, and Gilbert Rohde had resigned as director. By changing its name to the Laboratory School of Industrial Design, it obtained a charter from the New York Board of Regents that permitted it finally to confer degrees, but most of its students and faculty continued their participation in Popular Front activities. The school had struggled along on revenue from tuitions and by December was rewarded by its token inclusion of student work, tucked away in a corner of MoMA's Bauhaus exhibition, as MoMA's example of how the innovations of the Bauhaus were “gradually being introduced in the U.S."² MoMA's Bauhaus exhibit elicited many negative reviews from critics, who found it manipulative, chaotic and gadgety. Lewis Mumford's positive column in the New Yorker was a rare exception.³ The exhibition fit perfectly with MoMA's political agenda, initiated six years earlier at its International Exhibition of 1932: establishing the primacy of European architecture in the modern movement. But it also provided additional ammunition for MoMA's campaign to establish the Bauhaus as the original and "proper" source of the new profession of industrial design. If this was established, American industrial design could be described as a poor or misguided imitation of European innovation and could be “corrected" by MoMA to achieve maturity. This political objective stemmed from MoMA's basic assumptions (some would say biases) that guided its policies. First, like virtually all museums in the United States for decades before, MoMA at that time believed that Europe was, and should be, the center and global leader of all artistic endeavors, particularly in modern art, architecture, and design. MoMA had touted the Bauhaus exhibition as modern design based on the “best of Europe," implying that America should imitate Europe. In fact, in the previous March, MoMA had exhibited the architecture and furniture designs of Finnish architect Alvar Aalto (1898–1976) to reinforce European superiority in these fields. As a result of this Eurocentric template, European architects were regarded by MoMA as more significant than American architect Frank Lloyd Wright, even though Wright had inspired many of these same European architects nearly 30 years before and recently had designed a simple, modern Usonian ranch house community that would become a model for most U.S. postwar suburban developments. With similar logic, European Bauhaus designers were regarded by MoMA as more significant than American industrial designers, despite the fact that Americans were the first to actually design mass-produced products for mass consumption in a highly industrialized environment. Second, MoMA, at that time, believed that architects had been, and should be, the leaders not only in architecture, but in all artifacts within a home, including furniture and accessories. Historically, this had been true for many famous architects, both in Europe and in the United States. This was certainly true at the Bauhaus as well, and since MoMA was under the control of architects, there was an assumption that because of this history, architects should rightfully be leading the new profession ofindustrial design. Many architects were particularly upset not only because industrial design in the United States over the previous ten years had been led, not by architects, but by advertising artists, stage designers, display designers, commercial artists, writers, and art critics, to name a few, but even worse, the leaders, such as Loewy, Dreyfuss, and Teague, had become national celebrities and commanded incredible fees far higher than architects could hope for. Lastly, at that time, MoMA, like many art museums, defined fine art as pure, aesthetic, individual expression, based on philosophy, theory, or rationality, and without any intent of commercial gain, mass production, or popular appeal. In fact, it was generally believed that art was good only if it defied popular tastes, traditions, and commercial interests. The Bauhaus fit this idealistic template combining artistic, counterculture, avant-garde, academic experimentation, historical context, and intellectual theory. American designers did not fit this template. Their crassly commercial designs used popular “gimmicks” such as streamlining or skyscrapers, were acclaimed by the public through mass purchasing, and made lots of money for rich industrialists. What was there for a sophisticated, elite art museum to like? MoMA's curator of architecture and industrial art, John McAndrew, praised the Bauhaus industrial designs as timeless, and “free of both modernistic and streamlined aberrations," but lambasted the designs of designers like Loewy, Dreyfuss or Bel Geddes as “not properly modern," for “imitating superficially the forms of modern art, and for “restyling almost everything so that what we owned might look old fashioned as soon as possible" (the accusation of “artificial obsolescence" surfacing) These assumptions dominated MoMA policies for years, and put them at loggerheads with many practicing industrial designers and leaders of the profession, who believed that it was American industrial design practitioners of the Machine Age in America who defined the practical implementation, process definition, education, practice, and commercial success of industrial design in mass production and marketing. Industrial design in America was much more than aesthetic purity or fine art, as MoMA narrowly defined Bauhaus contributions. It was the complex and iterative process of design: the advance planning of a product to include a reasonable balance of functionality, engineering and manufacturing feasibility, user emotional wants and ergonomic needs, manufacturing costs, appropriate retail prices, suitable materials, and also an artistic and attractive visual presentation to persuade consumers to buy. Fine artists did not do this. MoMA's claim that the Bauhaus “invented” modern industrial design was a specious one. From Christopher Dresser (1834–1904) in the 1870s to the Bauhaus in the 1920s, there were dozens of designers and Arts and Crafts workshops that produced individual designs that could be called modern' in the sense of modern simplicity and style, but they were all produced by hand or in limited quantities. The Bauhaus itself, as mentioned earlier, was patterned after medieval craft guild workshops. Its so-called “invention"” was based on an academic theory of Purism described by Reyner Banham, a respected international authority on architecture and design: [Corbusier stated in Esprit Nouveau in the early 1920s that] "Purism is evidence of the Law of Mechanical Selection” ... [which was] clearly intended to share the status of Darwin's Law of Natural Selection ... [This law of Purism] ... “establishes that objects tend toward a type that is determined by .. the ideal of maximum utility and the demands of economical production, which conforms to the laws of nature." Therefore they concluded that "objects of maximum utility and lowest price have simple geometric shapes” .. such as cylinders, spheres and cones. To most architects this would appear watertight, but to most production engineers it would appear too abstract to be useful, and demonstrably false in its outcome.4 In other words, Purism was seen as an absolute and irrefutable law of nature. Bauhaus designs were of bottles, jugs, pots and pans, glasses and pipes, handmade forms which approximate these simple geometric shapes. In fact, students were criticized when they strayed from such forms. There were no designs of complex mechanical devices having multiple parts or functions. The American machine designers of the 1930s were the first to create truly industrial designs, in collaboration with engineers, for industrial mass production and consumption, including electromechanical products at low prices for middle-class consumers and mechanical monsters such as locomotives. No other group of designers had ever accomplished this before. The terms “industrial” and “machine” were appropriate and unique adjectives for this group. What did they think of Bauhaus design? Harold Van Doren, in his book Industrial Design in 1940, would write: The Bauhaus approach is a philosophy of life, as well as a method of design. It lacks, however, the realistic qualities that we Americans, rightly or wrongly, demand ... but it will be difficult, I believe, to acclimatize the esoteric ideas of the Bauhaus in the factual atmosphere of American industry.⁵ The American styles criticized by MoMA in the 1930s were popularly referred to as "streamlining,” “Art Deco," “futuristic," “Art Moderne, “modernistic,""machine design," or "contemporary." Most used modern imagery that appealed to popular taste and public expectations of the times. What they were called was much less important than what they accomplished. They were new, exciting, and different from traditional designs, and together represented a uniquely American style that changed the look of the country's industrial products. Because of this, for the first time, world industrial design leadership resided in the United States, not in Europe. This had been achieved, not by following any formal design standards, academic theories, or museum promotions, but by individual industrial designers getting down and dirty into industrial environments and determining what consumers actually wanted and needed. This was pure capitalism at work, restoring the American economy. Everyone was happy, except perhaps for MoMA and engineers. Engineers were not all that happy with industrial designers intruding on their traditional turf in manufacturing firms. Because for decades they were the highest authority in most companies on how to design and produce practical mechanical products at the lowest possible costs, they were not pleased with the demands of industrial designers to change traditional production processes and materials or to add cosmetic costs for purely nonfunctional purposes. Many designers, including major ones like Dreyfuss and Loewy, had to fiercely battle in-house engineering cultures for their proposed design changes. The day after the Bauhaus exhibition closed at the MoMA, on January 31, 1939, the Pennsylvania Railroad (PRR) completed the streamlined shell designed by Raymond Loewy for its S-1 class steam locomotive, the longest and heaviest rigid-frame reciprocating steam locomotive ever built, and the only one with a 6–4–4–6 wheel arrangement. The engine and tender was 140 feet long, almost half the length of a football field, and too big for many curves on PRR lines. It was intended to replace PRR's aging K-4s locomotives, designed in 1914. Loewy had designed a streamlined shroud for the K-4s in 1936, and his design of the S-1 shroud was a refinement of that design, with its bullet-nosed cylindrical boiler with headlight at the tip, resting on a half-bullet "cowcatcher" base. The overall design was very similar to Otto Kuhler's 1937 streamlined, bullet-nosed, steam locomotive for the B&O Royal Blue line. The photos ofLoewy standing by, or on, the S-1's base are among his most famous publicity poses. In a more human-scale result, Loewy's group in South Bend, Indiana, had just completed the design of the 1939 Studebaker Champion. The car was actually designed by Clare Hodgman, on Loewy's staff, who would also work on the 1940 line of Studebakers for Loewy. As the Bauhaus exhibition ended in January 1939, America's designers were as busy as ever. Raymond Loewy designed the Farmall tractor for the International Harvester Company, one of his famous “before-andafter" transformations of an ironwheeled contraption, looking a century old, into a clean and modern piece of farm machinery. This led to the design of a distinctive new sales center building for International Harvester, of which 1,125 would be built, and also to the design of the Tractractor caterpillar tractor. By this time, the Barnes & Reinecke design office in Chicago had a staff that included David Painter (1913–2003), vice president in charge of design; James Teague; Fred Priess; and George Mendenhall. Reinecke had also served in 1937 as a part-time instructor at the New Bauhaus under Moholy-Nagy. Barnes & Reinecke's new client was the McGraw-Edison Electric Company, which had introduced the first “pop-up" toaster in 1926. Now Reinecke designed its Toastmaster model 1B9 for 1939 in a glittering, ![](images/7de600dd0730ee004098a42b7320b6f3c4f3d9f6ad612fe84a85bea38062b8f6.jpg) Hamilton Beach Drinkmaster, 1939, by Barnes & Reinecke (Patent No. D118,225, U.S. Patent Office). chrome-plated steel, streamlined box. It would increase sales dramatically, be widely imitated, and serve as the typeform for toasters well into the 1960s, by which time seven million Toastmasters would have been sold. In 1980, the company would change its name to Toastmaster. Barnes & Reinecke also patented an updated version of Hamilton Beach's classic soda foun- ![](images/fb6948df35a8d8ceafff8bbdc6e561b43b1b934660321d0734d281e39d87e499.jpg) American Modern sugar bowl and creamer, 1939, by Russel and Mary Wright. tain, Drinkmaster. There was an infinite variety of clients; for example, Robert Davol Budlong was designing accordions for the Scandalli Dallape Cellini Company. Russel and Mary Wright were making headlines in 1939 with the introduction of their highly successful line of ceramic dinnerware called American Modern, made by Steubenville Pottery in East Liverpool, Ohio. Wright designed the line in 1937, but production had been delayed due to the 1938 recession, during which Wright attended New York University School of Architecture. The name American Modern came from Wright's successful line of furniture of the same name still being produced, which was designed in 1935 under its original name of Modern Living. In 1938 Wright had established a “casual living" concept line of accessories. The new ceramic dinnerware forms were soft and organic, and glazes were in rich, earthy colors. American Modern dinnerware became the perfect gift for young brides. With Wright's name on the bottom of each piece, he quickly became a design celebrity, as 80 million pieces were sold. In 1941, American Modern would receive the American Designers' Institute award for the best ceramic design of the year. Gilbert Rohde, who had resigned as director of the Design Laboratory in 1940, while continuing as director of design for the Herman Miller Furniture Company, now taught an industrial design program with Donald Deskey at the New York University School of Architecture, which they would continue until 1943. In 1941 Rohde designed a dramatic, transparent, circular coffee table for Herman Miller using plate glass for the top, resting on acrylic legs with brass tips. He would soon convince the company to discontinue production of all its period reproductions because of his belief that imitation of traditional design was insincere aesthetically. This was the first furniture company to do so. In Toledo, Van Doren & Associates (VDA) designed a Speedway scale for Toledo Scale and a Maytag Model E Master Washer, the first to use white rather than the speckled gray and green finish common at the time. VDA had worked with engineer Thomas R. Smith to design the sleek, unified form that concealed the motor and hoses, increased capacity by 50 percent, and featured an insulated tub that kept water hot longer. After the war, demand would increase, and Model E's appearance went essentially unchanged for 44 years, becoming one of the most successful household appliances of the century. Since 1936, in addition to their normal projects, many designers were deep in preparation for the New York World's Fair, scheduled to open on April 30, 1939. As the approaching fair dominated the news, Vogue magazine asked the leading industrial designers (Teague, Loewy, and Dreyfuss) to design fashions to be worn in the future. Vogue published the resulting designs on February 1 in an article entitled “Dress of the Future." All the top designers would be represented at the fair. Planning had begun four years earlier. In 1935, at the height of the Depression, a group of New York City retired policemen decided to create an international exposition to lift the city and the country out of the Depression. They formed the New York World's Fair Corporation (NYWFC), elected former chief of police Grover Whalen as president, and invited a number of political and business leaders to plan, build, and organize the fair and its exhibits, with countries around the world taking part in the biggest international event since World War I. Walter Dorwin Teague was one of eight directors on the fair's Board of Design, and was not only designing four exhibits, one building, a pavilion, and a diorama for the fair, but had a full plate of consulting work. One of Teague's projects in 1939 was designing glassware for Libbey Glass in Toledo, the design specifically to be used in the State Dining Room at the New York World's Fair. Teague designed a series of proposals, including a footed tumbler with a fluted, rectangular-shaped stem, inspired by the Ionic Greek columns on the front of the fair's Federal Building, and stemware with a rounded, fluted stem and cylindrical bowl. The first became the State Dining Room design, which had the etched crest of a spread-winged American eagle surrounded with thirteen stars. One hundred and twenty place settings were produced, with ten different styles of glasses and finger bowls. This same pattern without the etching would become a stock Embassy pattern in the Modern American line of 1940, developed with Owens-Illinois' design department head since 1935, Edwin W. Fuerst. The second design, with the rounded fluted stem, would become the Monticello pattern in the same line. That same year, Teague designed an internal grinder for the Heald Machine Tool Company, a tank vacuum cleaner for Montgomery Ward, a 620 Special for Kodak, an executive desk lamp for the Polaroid Company (designed by Frank Del Guidice), ![](images/9b199d9afa318a98c1591bd84cf17bdb84f1b15900eff330a4886f7ffd35090e.jpg) 1939 World's Fair brochure (courtesy Victoria Matranga). and a grand piano for Steinway & Sons. On April 30, 1939, the World's Fair in New York, optimistically titled The World of Tomorrow, held its grand opening attended by 206,000 people, with President Franklin Roosevelt giving the opening address. His speech was not only broadcast on radio networks but was also televised. NBC used the event to initiate regularly scheduled television broadcasts in New York City. An estimated 1,000 people viewed the Roosevelt telecast from about 200 television sets scattered throughout the New York area. In the Radio Corporation of America (RCA) building, its theme and concept designed by John Vassos, RCA president David Sarnoff (1891–1971) in April also introduced the first consumer television sets, which were designed by Vassos and wrapped in a clear Plexiglas enclosure also designed by Vassos. Sarnoff's speech was titled “Birth of an Industry.” He predicted that television would one day become an important entertainment and information medium. His crystal ball was 100 percent correct. Picture tubes in 1939 ranged in size from 5 inches to 12 inches, and the largest tube faced upward on top of a wooden cabinet with a raised, tilted mirror in back of the tube to view the picture horizontally. The fair's official guidebook praised the captains of the Machine Age: The true poets of the twentieth century are the designers, architects, and the engineers who glimpse some inner vision, create some beautiful figment of the imagination, and then translate it into valid actuality for the world to enjoy.⁶ The theme of the fair was "Building the World of Tomorrow." "Builders of the World of Tomorrow” was emblazoned on all fair workers' uniforms. Technology was the magic ingredient that made it all possible, and fairgoers were confident that technology would take them into the future. To people in 1939, technology meant ordinary stuff, like refrigerators, automobiles, trains, airplanes and radios; it meant building hydroelectric dams to produce electricity for rural areas; it meant Pyrex and Lucite, rayon and nylon; it meant new ways to build bridges and skyscrapers; it was about elevators and escalators; it was about speed, travel, labor saving and comfort. Within the lifetime of people in 1939, technology like this had transformed everyday life for the better. Within a decade, technology had changed the shape of everything in the American home, including the home itself. These things are now so common and traditional that we tend to take them for granted as if they always existed, but they did not. They were created in the 1930s by technology, controlled by the “true poets” of the 20th century: the engineers, the scientists, the architects, and the designers. Compare 1939's technology to today's impersonal technology of space stations, supercolliders, and genomes, which are often quite remote to our personal, everyday lives. The trademark symbols of the fair were a 610-foot-tall Trylon spire, which was connected by what was then the world's longest escalator to a 180-foot-diameter sphere called the Perisphere, both gleaming white and dominating the site at Flushing Meadows. The Perisphere housed a diorama called Democracity, which depicted a model future utopian city and suburbs of 2039 on the banks of a river. The city radiated outward in widening concentric circles around a single towering skyscraper at its center. A great hydroelectric plant on the riverbank powered the city, and the surrounding, rolling countryside was full of parks and towns.7 Designed by Henry Dreyfuss and Julian Everett, the diorama was viewed from above on a moving sidewalk, as if seen from an airplane at 7,000 feet, while a multi-image slide presentation was projected on the interior surface of the sphere, serving as a movie screen as large as an acre. In the diorama, miniature cars entered and left the model city via high-speed roads that connected to beautiful green towns clustered around factories where they worked. As night fell, the domed sky grew dark and stars came out, like a real night sky, and lights came on in the model city. Then a thousand-voice chorus was heard, and images of a dozen different groups of people (farmers, miners, engineers, etc.) were projected on the dome. The images repeatedly dissolved and were replaced by larger images, as if people were converging on Democracity. When they had arrived, shimmering light rose from the horizon to engulf the entire dome, then faded and the show was over. After the presentation, visitors exited the Perisphere on a 950-foot-long Helicline, an elevated spiral ramp that partially encircled the sphere threequarters of the way around on the outside.⁸ The message for the future was clear. People would no longer have to live in a city. They would live in the countryside or garden apartments, around the city. Factory workers would live in green towns just like executives and white-collar workers. Democracity was a vision of modern suburbs and planned communities. Henry Dreyfuss Associates (HDA) also designed the fair's administration building and the AT&T exhibit, which featured "Pedro the Vodar," an early voice synthesizer robot. The fair was divided into differently themed “zones," such as the Transportation Zone, the Communication and Business Systems Zone, the Food Zone, the Government Zone, the Community Interests Zone, the Production and Distribution Zone, etc. Different wall colors and colored lighting distinguished zones from each other. The Transportation Zone included the General Motors (GM) pavilion that featured a 36,000-square-foot Futurama exhibit designed by Norman Bel Geddes in the Highways and Horizons building. It was a diorama of a section of the United States, comprised of miniature highways, towns, waterways, 500,000 individually designed homes, 50,000 miniature vehicles, and a million miniature trees. Incredible details, such as “working” waterfalls, low clouds fashioned with chemical vapors clinging to mountainsides, clotheslines, and cow patties that many visitors never noticed, created a spectacular overall effect. Bel Geddes had perfected the diorama from his 1937 City of Tomorrow exhibit for Shell Oil, and from his earlier models of horse racing tracks and war games in the 1920s and early 1930s, described earlier. ![](images/1b7cec9b62312f7b48ded23cbc7316ffda15a2eb45a58a27bc52c2b3000e414f.jpg) World's Fair map. Futurama was by far the most popular exhibit of the fair, with long, winding waiting lines in front of the enormous, high, narrow cleft flanked by a huge silver “G” and "M" on the modernistic, silver, GM pavilion shaped like a long, low triangle. Upon entering the bright red cleft, visitors descended a ramp to a darkened room with a large map of the United States on the far wall, and a narrator gave a talk about the country's overburdened road system, then announced: “General Motors invites you on a tour of future America. The moving chairs below the map will transport you into 1960." Each chair had its own piped-in sound track with music and narration. The train of chairs was more than 1,500 feet long. The chairs moved smoothly as they floated on a rising and falling track that was 23 feet off the ground at its highest point, and each chair's sound system delivered the narration in perfect sync with the passing scenery. How was this done without computers or audiotapes? At the time, there were only two ways to record and play sound: vinyl phonograph records played on a turntable, or sound recorded on movie film. At Futurama, this feat was accomplished by something called a Polyrhetor, developed by Westinghouse: a 20-ton, 12-foot-high cylinder holding 22 bands of movie film that recorded and played only sound, each individually triggered by the moving cars on a specific segment of chair track.
