Recognition by ID magazine was a highly coveted honor valued by designers, clients, and employers. Stowe Myers opened his own design office, Stowe Myers Design, in Evanston, Illinois, in 1954. He would receive awards for his design of buildings and exhibits at the U.S. exhibit for the 1959 International Trade Fair in Casablanca, Morocco, as well as designs for Tinkertoy packaging, a CENCO Instruments laboratory meter, and a Rheem Manufacturing Company water heater drain valve. By 1954, interest in MoMA's annual Good Design exhibitions was slowing down. During that year's winter market, in addition to the Good Design exhibit, the Merchandise Mart staged an exhibition called Good Companions: Today in Tradition, which showed “what the manufacturers of traditional furniture, fabrics and lamps are providing for the homes of today." The reality was that “Good Design," as defined by MoMA, was not popular either with the general public, or with the majority of home furnishing manufacturers, who, unlike MoMA, depended on public acceptance for their livelihood. Even today, traditional (antique) home furnishing styles far outsell so-called modern furnishings by a wide margin. So much for the home furnishings industry, but MoMA still wanted to expand its architectural idea of “Good Design” into what had become the exclusive territory of industrial designers: electromechanical products, major appliances, and automobiles (the latter at its 1951 8 Automobiles show). Accordingly, the jury for the 1955 winter market exhibition of “Good Design” included Arthur BecVar, head of design at General Electric in Louisville, Kentucky, and then president of the American Society of Industrial Designers (ASID). It was MoMA's first token recognition of organized design on any “Good Design" jury. BecVar, Edgar Kaufmann, Jr., and Just Lunning of Georg Jensen's New York store selected 400 products for this final Good Design exhibition, which Interiors magazine noted with surprise included a number of“industrial designs that hardly qualified as home furnishings," such as cameras, telephones, kitchen equipment, and household appliances.3 The historical dividing line between household furnishings (the former art industries) and mechanical equipment (the former artless industries) was gradually being erased, but the Good Design exhibitions were concluded with the resignation of Edgar Kaufmann, Jr., from MoMA in 1955.“Good Design" had run its course. However, in Germany, the legacy of the Bauhaus lived on. In 1953, it was resurrected by the Hochschule für Gestaltung (HfG, Institute for Form), which was founded in Ulm as the "New Bauhaus" and funded by the German government and the American High Commission to foster national economic recovery for Germany. Max Bill (1908–1994), a former Bauhaus student, was named director. The school initially focused on product design, but by 1956 it would abandon this in favor of pure theory. At the same time, Max Braun's sons, Artur and Erwin, were reorganizing the German appliance company Max had founded in 1921. They were inspired by the HfG in Ulm and initiated a corporate design program emulating the Bauhaus tradition, headed by Dieter Rams. The company's products—spare, undecorated, and geometric in form and neutral in color—soon began to be introduced internationally. ![](images/bea94daf4986f2f8d26a79d05861b1eae393662dae03d7db6d3f06d5fc6fc324.jpg) Braun Kitchen Machine, 1957, by Gerd Alfred Müller. In 1954, Henry Dreyfuss Associates had designed the interiors of Columbine, President Eisenhower's presidential plane, and in 1955 his design of the unique, spherical-shaped Model 84 canister vacuum cleaner (that floated on its own exhaust air) for the Hoover Company debuted. But Hoover balked at his high fees and, according to some engineers at Hoover, at his arrogance in insisting on impractical design changes. Hoover dropped him for a newer, younger, less costly, and local consulting firm, Smith, Scherr, & McDermott of Akron, Ohio, which had been founded in 1947. In Philadelphia, Philco introduced Harold Van Doren's design for the first refrigerator with a two-way opening door in 1955, with a V handle in the center that pulled left or right. Detroit firm Sundberg-Ferar designed IBM's 702 computer. Chicago industrial designer Robert Budlong passed away in 1955 at age 53, and his business was taken over by one of his associates, Ken Shory, Sr. (1921–1991); the business was renamed Ken Schory Associates. Schory would later partner with Tom Steinbach, and their firm would be named Schory-Steinbach Associates. Also in Chicago, Waltman Associates, headed by Chauncey “Chick" Waltman, was designing a sewing machine for Sears, Roebuck, and a three-way mixer for Nutone. Ann Swainson, the lady who initiated the Bureau of Design at Montgomery Ward in 1931, died in 1955 at the age of 55. Swainson remained head of the Bureau as Sewell Avery was decimating the company. Avery had led the firm through its phenomenal growth in the 1930s, but during and after the war, he was convinced there was going to be another Depression. He pulled back on any modernization or expansion. In 1953, as Sears was approaching its second billion-dollar mark, Montgomery Ward fell below one billion. By 1955, the Bureau was reduced to Swainson and an assistant, confined to a desk in a corridor of the warehouse building. That year, Avery would be ousted, but it was too late. The loss of her Bureau was too much for Swainson.4 In 1955, Dave Chapman would return to Montgomery Ward as a consultant, where he had worked from 1934 to 1936 under Swainson, to reorganize and reform the Bureau of Design, which would now be headed by Frederick W. Priess, one of Swainson's former designers. The department would survive until 1976, when Mobil acquired Sears. The U.S. Department of State in 1955 retained Russel Wright to survey possibilities for developing cottage industries in Southeast Asia. He explored native handicrafts and established centers of manufacturing for these products. George Nelson incorporated his office in New York as George Nelson Associates, Inc., and relocated his studio tò 251 Park Avenue South. He had opened his initial design studio in 1947. Clare Hodgman and Robert E. Bourke, colleagues from both Sears and Loewy organizations, joined to form an independent office, Hodgman and Bourke, Industrial Design Consultants, in New York in 1955. Their accounts would include Coca-Cola, Mack Trucks, GAF cameras, Volvo, Superior Coach, and Electrolux, SA. Hodgman lived in Mamaroneck and was awarded dozens of design patents. In 1955, the first retractable hardtop car by Brooks Stevens, the Gaylord Grand Prix, was introduced, as was the Ford Thunderbird, designed by George Walker and his styling staff. That year, Walker became vice president of styling at Ford, a position he would hold until 1961. But criticism of Detroit design was still rising. Henry Dreyfuss published his book Designing for People in 1955, which encapsulates his personal design philosophy on the title page: “We bear in mind that the object being worked upon is going to be ridden in, looked at, talked into, activated, operated, or in some other way used by people individually or en masse." His book included recollections of his career and illustrations of his most important work. Perhaps most importantly, it introduced “Joe and Josephine," diagrammatic charts of typical anthropometric males and females, in typical standing, sitting and reaching positions, each with appropriate average body dimensions indicated as if on a mechanical drawing, as well as comparable measurements of the largest (97.5) and smallest (2.5) percentiles of people. Joe and Josephine's children were also diagrammed. Dreyfuss had accumulated most of these measurements during World War II, when he measured large cohorts of military personnel in order to design equipment and control panels. The Joe and Josephine charts would be invaluable to the profession as scientific measurement tools. The book established Dreyfuss as a leader in what was at the time called "human factors," later better known as ergonomics: the specialty area of adapting product designs to human sizes and functional physical capabilities. Henry Dreyfuss Associates would later expand and improve the Joe and Josephine measurements into numerous detailed charts that would become essential industrial design tools. The next year, Bell Telephone Laboratories debuted a wallmounted telephone designed by HDA, which became popular in kitchens where counter space was at a premium. The postwar period was over. The United States became involved in the Vietnam War in 1956 and would see an escalation of that war over the next 20 years, complicated by the Cold War with the Soviet Union that would last for 35 years. These events not only dominated national priorities but also contributed to vast cultural, social, political, and generational changes in the country over this period. Despite this, the industrial design profession advanced and expanded, following the examples and standards set by the machine designers of the 1930s. The Cold War was raging in 1956 when Nikita Krushchev made his “We will bury you" speech and the Soviet Union broke the back of the Hungarian revolt. The United States was deeply invested in Central European problems, and European exhibits were the means for promoting U.S. interests. That same year, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates (WDTA) was selected by the Department of Commerce to design the permanent U.S. pavilion and exhibit in Moscow at Gorky Park. At the time, WDTA was also working on the interiors and furnishings for the new Air Force Academy and a new museum at West Point. Eliot Noyes was retained to develop a new IBM corporate design style to improve the quality of IBM products, graphics, exhibitions, packaging and architecture. The result was the first U.S. “house style” program, a comprehensive and consistent public visual image of a corporation, much of which is still being used by IBM todav. Also in 1956, the Ravmond Loewy Corporation was amended as Raymond Loewy and William Snaith: Ine., with William Snaith as managing partner. ![](images/29f3d2f21a455c35477696b76e9841c9f7c5dfbb83df689c1fb9c50f36535dcc.jpg) Dreyfuss's ergonomic chart, 1955 (reprinted from Designing for People by Henry Dreyfuss, by permission of Allworth Press, an imprint of Skyhorse Publishing, Inc.). In 195", the vear before Sputnik ignited the Cold War space race to the moon, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates (WDTA) designed the U.S. pavilions at both the Zagreb Fair in Zagreb. Croatia, and the Vienna Fair, both funded by the U.S. Information Agency (U'SIA). and both led and supervised by W. Dorwin Teague. Ir. Henry Drevfuss Associates designed the "deer" logo for John Deere: Sundberg-Ferar designed the Miracle Kitchen for Whirlpool with a robotic vacuum cleaner; the Ravmond Loewy Corporation designed the Alouette utility helicopter for the French firm, SNCASE; and Donald Deskey designed packaging for some of the most common and popular evervday household items, including Cheer detergent ( 1958) and Gleem toothpaste (1954). Sadly, Harold Van Doren, one of the founders of the American Society of Industrial Designers, passed away in 195" at age 62, and his Philadelphia firm was taken over by Harper Landell (1918-1997), a Van Doren emplovee often vears. The firm would be known as Harper Landell &: Associates until it would dissolve in 19\~5. Van Doren's papers can be found at Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. During the decade after the war, John Vassos became involved with Greek war relief efforts through the American Relief for Greek Democracy (ARGD), whose honorary chairperson was Eleanor Roosevelt. Vassos became one of the most honored Greek Americans and developed close friendships with other well-known Greeks such as Aristotle Onassis, who would marry Jackie Kennedy in 1968. Besides his work for RCA and other companies, Vassos designed murals for theaters and studios, including the Riviera, Syosset, and Rivoli theaters in New York; the Egyptian Theatre in Los Angeles; the Condado Beach Hotel in Puerto Rico: the WCAY studio in Philadelphia; and the Radio and TV Center in Washington, D.C. Vassos also designed trade and exhibition pavilions in New York, India, and Pakistan. Brooks Stevens Design Associates designed a range of product prototypes in 1957 for the Evinrude division of the Outboard Marine Company, including the 1+-foot-diameter Fisherman circular boat; the 16-foot Lark runabout; the Starflight cabin cruiser: the Housetloat houseboat; the Heli-bout, a combination helicopter boat; and the Showboat, a miniature version of a 19th-century Mississippi river packet. They were all built to attract attention at boat shows, and they accomplished that in spades, with considerable publicity, but they were never produced in quantity. In 1957, Jean Reinecke relocated to Los Angeles and opened a second office in Pasadena. California, where he designed the 3M Décor Dispenser Model C-15, a swooping plastic desktop tape dispenser in numerous colors and textures, that would be introduced in 1961, and which would be popular for the remainder of the century. He would lecture at a number of universities including MIT, Cal Tech, UCLA, Stanford, ITT, the Chicago Art Institute, and the University of Illinois. Arthur BecVar, head of GE's industrial design group of +5 for major appliances in Louisville, Kentucky, designed a new kitchen center with Robert W. Blee. The nine-foot-long grouping included a combination washer-dryer, a DisposAll equipped sink, a dishwasher, and a range with oven, all under a single, seamless, stainless-steel countertop. The Industrial Design Educators' Association (IDEA) was formed in 1957 for industrial design educators, who were denied membership in both the American Society of Industrial Designers (ASID) and the Industrial Designers Institute (IDI) because they were not active commercial design practitioners. The first president of IDEA was Joseph Carriero (1920- 1978), chair of the industrial design department at the Philadelphia Museum School of Art. In London, the International Council of Industrial Designers (ICSID) was formed, comprised of 23 professional and promotional design organizations from 17 countries. Peter Müller-Munk, of the ASID, was elected president of the new international organization, which still exists today. Over many years, ICSID would hold design seminars in many countries to promote the use of industrial design. At a 1954 talk to the local advertising club in Minneapolis, Brooks Stevens had described the mission of industrial design as "planned obsolescence," a phrase that would become almost as controversial in industrial design as "streamlining" had been. By 1956 Stevens was using the phrase frequently and defining it as “instilling in the buyer the desire to own something a little newer, a little better, a little sooner than is necessary."6Actually, to us in the 21st century, this sounds like exactly what happens with the latest and newest version of Apple's iPhone, or any new version of a popular product. Although Stevens admitted to originating the phrase, he did not, and he may not have known it, because he was a student at Cornell at the time when Elmo Calkins, the “Dean of Advertising” partner of Calkins & Holden advertising agency, in his 1932 book, What Consumer Engineering Really Is, wrote that obsoletism is another device for stimulating consumption. The consideration of style is a consideration in buying many things. Clothes go out of style and are replaced long before they wear out. That principle extends to other products—motor cars, bathrooms, radios, foods, refrigerators, furniture.? Calkins went on to say the principle of obsoletism was the solution for solving the Depression because buying things increases prosperity for business, consumers, and the country. In fact, that is precisely what happened. But in Vance Packard's 1957 book, The Hidden Persuaders, Packard (1914–1996) accused industry for “manipulating” consumers and “inducing" desire. He pointed to Stevens' "planned obsolescence," and by implication, all industrial designers, as the leading culprit in this "immoral" practice. In fact, he called Stevens "the crown prince of planned obsolescence." Packard's book reached a wide audience and was hailed by many as a noble cause, because the idea of secretly and deliberately manipulating consumers was considered underhanded in some way. Packard viewed the practice as wasteful, but producing only products that are functionally "necessary" because "old ones” were no longer usable might be morally “frugal, but would be totally unacceptable to modern consumers wanting something newer and better. Consumers have not just functional "needs," but also upward mobility "wants and aspirations." Packard, a reporter and writer, obviously knew little about business, design, or consumers, but it was a time when the public had become suspicious of industry in general, partly because anti-Vietnam War activists referred derisively to the "wartime military-industrial complex. Nevertheless, Packard's book put industrial design on the defensive, and in 1958, Dave Chapman, Walter Dorwin Teague, and Henry Dreyfuss mounted a campaign to publicly address critics. They openly spoke out against what they called "phony obsolescence" that added gimmicks and superfluous changes to products, but they agreed with "planned obsolescence" that resulted in genuine product improvements. Stevens himself argued that the item that is replaced by a new product often finds its way into a lower-priced secondary market and is purchased by someone who otherwise would not have been able to afford a new one. He saw it as essentially recycling of material goods that benefited the lower class at the same time as it produced a healthy economy. The controversy faded in a year or so, but it was an early warning that social issues were becoming more relevant to the public than technological or business issues. The generation of Depression survivors was aging. Many of them had since 1930 been without a decent standard of living and had struggled for five years after 1945 to acquire the material wealth and comfort of the middle class. Now their children, the so-called baby boomers, blessed since birth with all the comforts and security their parents provided, had the luxury of concerning themselves with social, political, and moral issues such as civil rights, clean air, clean water, and environmental concerns. These would soon become mainstream preoccupations with the boomer generation, along with the nuclear age. The first nuclear power plant, in Shippingport, Pennsylvania, went into service in 1957, and in 1958, the first nuclear-powered merchant vessel was launched. In 1958, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates designed the U.S. Atoms for Peace exhibit for the Atomic Energy Commission, held in the Palais des Nations in Geneva, Switzerland, and Brooks Stevens Design Associates designed a futuristic Oscar Meyer Wienermobile, about which he declared, “There's nothing more aerodynamic than a wiener." Machine Designer giant Norman Bel Geddes passed away in 1958 at age 65. Since his office closed in 1947, he had mostly returned to his first love, the theater, by designing stages and theaters, including a television stage for NBC. This shift in priority may have been his tribute to his daughter, who had become a famous stage and screen actress. His autobiography, Miracle in the Evening, which he wrote starting in 1957, would be edited by William Kelly and published posthumously in 1960. It is a wonderfully written story of his early life but is primarily about his personal experiences, with very little about his professional industrial design career, other than a listing of his designs and clients at the end of the book.8 By the time of Bel Geddes's death, his daughter, Barbara Bel Geddes (1922–2005), had become a famous actress in films such as I Remember Mama (1948), for which she won an Academy Award for the Best Supporting Actress, and Broadway productions such as The Moon Is Blue (1951–1953). Before his death, Bel Geddes had the satisfaction of seeing the Federal Interstate Highway System under construction, initiated by President Eisenhower in 1956. It was the full-scale embodiment of Bel Geddes's 1939 miniature Futurama concepts, and at \$25 billion over ten years, it was the largest public works project in American history throughout that time. The archives of Bel Geddes's theater and industrial design papers reside at the Harry Ransom Center at the University of Texas in Austin. The automotive industry was being threatened by foreign competition. From 1955 to 1959, as imports of Volkswagens, Karmann Ghias, Porsches and Mercedes from Germany; Toyotas and Nissans from Japan; Morris Mini Minors, Triumphs and MGs from England; and Saabs from Sweden increased from 1 percent to 10 percent of the U.S. market. Traditional Detroit brand names such as DeSoto disappeared, or merged to form Studebaker-Packard (1954) or American Motors (1954, Hudson/Nash). But Detroit continued with the same styling approach it had followed the previous ten years. In 1957, the first vice president of styling at Chrysler, Virgil Exner, had introduced a new Forward Look line of cars with tail fins even higher than General Motors. There was one exception to this pattern. In 1956, under George Walker, Ford staff, including Gordon Buehrig, chief body designer, had sought to create the elegance of its classic 1940 Lincoln Continental with the new Lincoln Continental Mark II. It had no excessive tail fins, and in fact it seemed to be part of the reason Ford outsold GM for the first time in history in 1957. In 1961, the design would win a national design award from the Industrial Designers Institute. On the other hand, in late 1957, Ford introduced the least successful car design since the 1934 Chrysler Airflow, this time the 1958 Edsel, a new brand name honoring Henry Ford's son, designed by vice president of styling George Walker and his staff after extensive market research. The vertical air intake in the Edsel's front nose, resembling an open-mouth human scream, seemed to be the offending feature, as the public had become more accustomed to horizontal grills and air intakes. The market failure would cost Ford \$350 million and force cancellation of the Edsel brand in 1959. In June of that year, Walter Dorwin Teague expressed the general consensus of most industrial designers when he spoke at a dinner meeting of the Detroit chapter of the Industrial Designers Institute: I agree heartily with millions of others that our characteristic car design has in recent years become lousy, ugly, ostentatious, impractical, and excessively costly; and what is worse from management's point of view, this kind of design has come a spectacular cropper in the market.9 ![](images/c44095fa212fe752ad956f111263e4744e1829ab02e38b89bc6378e7b8aae3ee.jpg) The driving force behind this Detroit design direction since 1927, Harley Earl, retired in December 1958, naming William Mitchell (1912-1988) his successor as vice president of styling at General Motors (GM). Earl's garish chrome-trimmed and tailfinned 20-year era had run its course. The last of Earl's creations Walter Dorwin Teague, ca. 1950s. would be the 1959 Cadillac El Dorado with tail fins 42 inches above the ground (about chest high). Fortunately, this would be the last gasp of tail fins. Mitchell favored smoothly sculptured, unadorned shapes, and GM's cars would be transformed into more sophisticated designs. However, Harley Earl, Inc., Earl's independent studio, continued operations, winning IDI national design awards in 1955 with the Sweepmaster for the Bissell Carpet Sweeper Company, in 1958 for the Secretary Model thermo-fax copying machine for 3M Company, and in 1959 for the Cinetronic Electric Eye 8 mm movie camera for Argus, a division of Sylvania Electric Products, Inc. Braun products in Germany, which had been transformed by its introduction of Bauhausstvle products in 1955, had attracted international attention in 1957 with the introduction of its Model KM 3/KM 31 Kitchen Machine. The Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) enthusiastically welcomed this resurgence of its favorite international style by creating a permanent displav of Braun products in 1958. In fact, many designers in the United States also praised and imitated the stvle because ofits simplicity, cleanness, and clarity. The products were obviously ofhigh quality, but thev cost more than similar American products, which limited their acceptance in the mass market. Perhaps more significantly, such characterless, colorless, spare design simply did not appeal to the U.S. mass market. In 1958, the Aluminum Companv of America (Alcoa) had initiated a series of advertisements promoting concept designs of well-known designers. In 1959, Alcoa presented the first industrial design awards, which included one for Peter Müller-Munk Associates (PMMA). PMMA also consulted with U.S. Steel regarding its newlv introduced vinvl-coated steel sheets with decorative patterns embossed into the vinyl, and which could be drawn or stamped like any sheet metal. By this time, according to a February 1959 Fortune magazine survey, there were 300 total industrial design organizations, and 180 total members of IDI and ASID combined (a significant decline in membership). One hundred twenty corporations had internal design departments, compared to 180 independent consulting offices. The obvious result was an increase in competition in the market and the lowering of the exorbitant fees of the big-name designers. The day when a Raymond Loewy could get \$50,000 for designing a cigarette package was gone forever. Retainer fees now ranged from \$500 to \$5,000 per month, depending on a firm's reputation. The 20) largest industrial design firms named by Fortune, half of which were led by original machine designers of the 1930s, included the number of employees where known: Raymond Loewy Corporation (200); Walter Dorwin Teague Associates (140), Henry Dreyfuss Associates (50); Dave Chapman, Inc. (50); Peter Müller-Munk Associates (30); Russel Wright (25); George Nelson (24); Harley Earl, Inc.; Brooks Stevens; and Donald Deskey. The remaining 10 were younger and smaller firms. Industrial designers were becoming part of the popular culture. The 1954 film Executive Suite starred William Holden as vice president of design and development Don Walling, who wins the presidency by vowing to replace the shoddy furniture produced by the company with quality products. In the 1959 film North by Northwest, Cary Grant meets a pretty girl, Eve Kendall, played by Eva Marie Saint, who is posing as an industrial designer. AT&T introduced its Princess phone in 1959, designed by Henry Dreyfuss Associates (HDA), with a petite size for teenage girls and an illuminated dial to facilitate bedroom usage. AT&T was the iPhone of the 1950s. HDA also designed the NASA/AT&T Bell Labs Mercury control room for the fledgling space program, interiors for the Lockheed Constellation aircraft, and a Strobonar photoflash for Honeywell. That year, HDA published Measure of Man, a book featuring Joe and Josephine that expanded the applications of ergonomic data to many more design situations and circumstances. Now, the increasingly chilly Cold War with the Soviet Union spawned a new, political market for industrial design. Some would call it propaganda.


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\13_Chapter_13.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `17f75e699f1a5b44ee9ae3684d00740000711361ef184c8f5426aa96ef0d480a`
