# Cold War, Mergers, and Computers In the summer of 1959, the U.S. Information Agency (USIA) mounted an exhibition in Moscow, the American National Exhibition, as the first of many such cultural exchanges between the Soviet Union and the United States. The exhibit, designed by George Nelson and Charles Eames, turned into the site of a vigorous Cold War debate between Soviet premier Nikita Krushchev and then-vice president Richard Nixon. Featured at the exhibition was an innovative geodesic dome by R. Buckminster Fuller, which attracted worldwide fascination bordering on a space-age fad. Nineteen similar such exhibits in the USSR by American designers over the next 32 years would be seen in a total of 87 cities across 12 time zones by an estimated 20 million Soviet citizens to promote U.S. national political objectives, and to win the hearts and minds of the public with personal cultural diplomacy. Eventually, by 1991, Soviet citizens would be convinced that Soviet promises of free consumer goods would never be realized, and they rejected communism for a more democratic government. Also in 1959, the Museum of Modern Art displayed three Structures by Buckminster Fuller, including his geodesic dome, plus other designs of his from 1927, which included a circular house on a central pole called a 4-D Utility Unit, and the development of a half plane, half car called the 4-D Zoomobile. In Los Angeles, the famous “Skyscraper" furniture designer, Paul T. Frankl, passed away in 1959 at age 73. He had left New York in 1934 to establish a gallery in Los Angeles, where his clients included many Hollywood stars of the late 1930s. He had applied his talents to many projects, from interior design, posters, greeting cards and theater sets, to selecting objects from Europe, the Orient, and Mexico for sale in his shops. His fortunes had shifted often in his career from prosperity to poverty, and his personal life ranged from depression over the death of his first wife, bitterness over divorce from his second wife, and emotional security with his third. A month after President John F. Kennedy was elected president in 1960 came the death of the most highly esteemed and famous pioneer of industrial design, Walter Dorwin Teague, who died on December 5 in Flemington, New Jersey, just two weeks shy of his 77th birthday. Just the year before, Teague had addressed the Royal Society of Arts in London, and in June of that same year, he had spoken to the Detroit chapter of the Industrial Designers Institute. In 1963, the American Society of Industrial Designers (ASID) would honor Teague, one of its founders, with the organization's first scholarship program, the Walter Dorwin Teague Scholarship, eligible to selected junior students majoring in industrial design. Teague's papers are archived at Syracuse University Library, Special Collections, and at his surviving original organization, now called Teague. His son, W. Dorwin Teague, who had worked with his father from 1934 to 1942 and had later rejoined the organization in 1953 as partner, now was number two in Teague's professional office, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates (WDTA), with Robert Harper as CEO and president. Dorwin Teague and other WDTA associates in 1960 received a national design award from the Industrial Designers Institute (IDI) for the design of the Euphorian dental chair for the Ritter Company. A functional patent for it is in Teague, Jr.'s name. That same year, WDTA also designed the new Polaroid Electric-Eye 900 camera and a landmark mathematical model for the U.S. Navy's Missiles and Quality Control System, still in use today. Another IDI award in 1960 went to Dave Chapman, Inc., in Chicago for its research report and recommendations on the design of facilities for U.S. schools, produced for the Educational Facilities Laboratories, Inc., and to William L. Mitchell and the General Motors Styling Staff for the Chevrolet Corvair. The latter, a so-called compact car, was a significant improvement in styling, with no tail fins, but its oversteering tendency earned it the wrath of Ralph Nader, who dubbed it “one of the nastiest-handling cars ever built." His 1965 book, Unsafe at Any Speed, would prod Congress, which in 1966 would pass the National Traffic and Motor Vehicle Act, empowering the government to set safety standards for new cars after 1968, which would soon be enacted. Designs of 1961 included the Tower Capri portable typewriter for Sears, Roebuck by the Sundberg-Ferar design firm in Detroit, which won an IDI national award; the IBM Selectric typewriter by Eliot Noyes, which by 1975 accounted for 75 percent of the U.S. electric typewriter market; and the Singer 660 sewing machine by Henry Dreyfuss Associates. HDA also designed the Civil War Information Building in Richmond, Virginia. George A. Beck, head of GE's industrial design light military electronics department, received one of Alcoa's annual industrial design awards. In a major corporate move, Eliot Noyes was retained by Westinghouse to dramatize its image, including logo and design standards, and to reorganize its internal design departments in order to better compete with General Electric. Also in 1961, Raymond Loewy established Raymond Loewy/William Snaith, Inc., and Raymond Loewy/William Snaith Associates, Inc., with William Snaith as president and Raymond Loewy as chairman of the board. In 1954, the Studebaker Corporation had merged with Packard to form Studebaker-Packard, and with its combined internal design staffs, as well as its financial difficulties, it felt that it no longer needed to pay Raymond Loewy's extravagant design fees, so it canceled his contract in 1956, even though Loewy had designed Studebaker cars for the previous 20 years. Loewy's Studebaker studio in South Bend shut down, and Robert Bourke relocated to Loewy's office in Chicago. But now, in 1961, Studebaker was in serious trouble, and the company engaged a new president, Sherwood E. Egbert, from the McCullough Company, where Egbert had worked with Brooks Stevens on a number of design projects. Egbert engaged Stevens to design the 1962 models of the company's mainstays, the Lark sedan and the Gran Turismo Hawk, with a meager budget of \$7 million to tool both cars. Stevens accomplished the task with minimal changes to the previous year's models, which had been designed by Loewy. But Egbert knew such minor changes were not enough to save the company and wanted to demonstrate that Studebaker could still produce an innovative design, so as a last ditch effort he rehired Raymond Loewy to design a sensational new sports car, which would become the 1962 Avanti. Loewy retreated to his Palm Springs home with designers Bob Andrews, Tom Kellogg, and John W. Ebstein. The resulting design, sleek, graceful, and unlike any car from Detroit, was an instant success, but it was too late for the company, which would fold in 1963. Still, the Avanti would be brought to life again and again by sequential private interests until 1991. John Tjaarda, the exceptional automotive designer of the 1936 Lincoln Zephyr, passed away in 1962 at age 65. In 1961, he had pursued an interest in solid-state rocketry and joined the Aerojet-General Corporation in Sacramento as a technical advisor. His son Tom Tjaarda (b. 1934) would follow in his dad's footsteps as an automotive designer and would establish his own automotive design office in Italy, Dimensione Design, in 1984.1 In 1962, Chauncey "Chick" Waltman also died at age 66. A well-known Chicago furniture and lamp designer starting in 1920, he joined the industrial design movement in the 1930s practicing as Waltman Associates at 165 Chicago Avenue. The office later moved to 310 E. Chicago Avenue. His son, Charles T. “Chubb” Waltman (1919–2006), joined him after World War II, and the office operated until about 1960. Also in 1962, H. Creston Doner chaired the Advisory Board of the Showcase National Corporation for the United States and Canada, and over a number of years, he was a consultant to the architects and designers of the 1964 World's Fair in New York, Expo 67 in Canada; HemisFair 68 in San Antonio; Tomorrowland in Disneyland, California; and the Bay Area Regional Transit (BART) system in 1969. ![](images/330dd2ba605140460ff00f8b1fc9006863f41c2ef82a649260e5b3be1ddfd532.jpg) Chauncey“Chick”Waltman, ca. 1950s (courtesy William Waltman). In the world of expositions, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates designed the U.S. Science Center at the 1962 Century 21 Exposition in Seattle, which was called by some “a virtual cathedral of science." The Spacearium held 750 people for a simulated voyage first through the solar system, then through the Milky Way Galaxy and beyond. In 1963, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates would design the Maxwell House Automatic Coffee Machine for the General Foods Corporation. The new International Council of Societies of Industrial Design (ICSID) convened its Third Congress in Paris, and the Industrial Designers Institute (IDI) held an exhibition at the Louvre, organized by Alfons Bach, who had moved his practice to Palm Beach, Florida, in 1959. Among the large selection of American designs were the 1962 Avanti by Loewy and the 1956 Lincoln Continental Mark II by George Walker and his staff at Ford. By 1963, international design was becoming a common practice. Sixteen American industrial design firms had offices abroad, and manufacturers from 22 foreign countries had contracts with American designers. The Cold War pitted the United States against the Soviet Union in providing a better standard of living to their respective citizens. After Russia's Luna I flight to within 3,700 miles of the moon, Soviet premier Nikita Krushchev had boldly predicted in 1959 that by 1966 the Soviet Union would “catch up” to America and would be distributing basic consumer goods to Russian citizens “free of charge." Industrial designers were well aware that they were now competing globally. At ASID's annual conference in 1962, Raymond Loewy had spoken of such competition: Nothing great has ever been achieved—whether by a nation, a business, an individual—without quality.... Now, we are at a point where that belief is being put to the test. Americans are in a massive race to maintain our leadership against other nations in trade, training, goods, and taste.² Raymond Loewy had just designed the interior and external livery for Air Force One when President Kennedy was assassinated in Dallas, Texas, in November 1963. The Boeing 707 was a specially modified Boeing C-137 Stratoliner. Loewy had met with Kennedy and conducted research in the National Archives for the assignment, for which he did not charge a fee. He found the uppercase typeface called Caslon, which was used widely spaced in the country's name in the first printed copy of the U.S. Declaration of Independence, and used it for the large lettering on each side of Air Force One's fuselage, reading, “United States of America." A presidential seal was on both sides of the fuselage, and an American flag on the tail. The plane took the president to Dallas and returned his body to Washington. The following year, at the request ofJacqueline Kennedy, Loewy designed the 1964 five-cent John Kennedy memorial postage stamp for the U.S. Postal Service. That same year, Loewy established Market Concepts, Inc., a subsidiary for confidential research operations. Now reorganized as Raymond Loewy/William Snaith, Inc., Loewy's office won a national Industrial Designers Institute (IDI) Award for the design of the Time-Master, made by the Dictaphone Corporation, as did William Mitchell of the General Motors Styling staff for the Pontiac Grand Prix. Kem Weber, the pioneer who began designing modern furniture in 1926 and was inspired by the Paris Exposition of 1925, passed away in California in 1963 at age 74. After the war, Weber had moved back to Santa Barbara, California, and worked mostly as an architect, building a handful of houses there and in nearby Montecito. By the late 1950s, his health had begun to fail. Also in 1963, Henry Dreyfuss Associates designed the first touch-button phone, the Touchtone 2500 for AT&T, and the Automatic 100 camera for Polaroid. In 1964, Ford's sporty Mustang won the Industrial Designers Institute's national design award. It was Ford's answer to GM's Corvette and was a huge success with the 16- to 24-yearold baby boomer crowd. George Walker had resigned in 1961, so the Mustang was designed by the new vice president of styling, Eugene Bordinat (1920–1987), and his staff, driven by Lee Iacocca, who had been named Ford vice president in 1960. Even as Studebaker was dying, Brooks Stevens, without Studebaker's agreement, had prototyped a promotional concept car for Studebaker, which was initially called the Mercebaker, a "contemporary classic" sports car based on the antique Mercedes SSK of the 1927–1929 period. Stevens' sons, Steve and David, built the prototype in six weeks during 1964 and were in the process of towing it to the auto show to create a sensation for Studebaker when Studebaker executives told Stevens they would not be showing it. But Stevens knew the director of the auto show, who agreed to display the prototype as a product of Brooks Stevens Associates. The car was so well received that Stevens funded the production of the car by his sons as a business enterprise, renaming it the Excalibur SSK Roadster, which enjoyed success as the first "replicar," a design based on a historically classic car.³ In April 1964, the New York World's Fair opened at Flushing Meadows in Queens, with the theme of "Peace through Understanding," and dedicated to “Man's Achievement on a Shrinking Globe in an Expanding Universe." The fair would run from April to October in 1964 and 1965. The grounds were dominated by a 12-story high, stainless steel, skeletonized model of the earth, appearing to float on a ring of fountains in the center of a circular lagoon. It was designed by Peter Müller-Munk Associates for U.S. Steel and was called the Unisphere, which still remains in Flushing Meadows today. Attendees traveled the fairgrounds on a monorail designed by Walter Dorwin Teague Associates, who also designed the Festival of Gas, including the House of the Future, featuring a gas-powered refrigerator and a gas oven designed for Norge. Most regarded Futurama II as the most popular exhibit, designed by Henry Dreyfuss Associates (HDA) for General Motors. Like its 1939 fair predecessor, designed by Norman Bel Geddes, it was a show in which visitors seated in moving chairs glided past elaborately detailed miniature 3-D model scenery, showing what life might be like 60 years in the "near future" of 2024. Bel Geddes, who had died six years earlier, would have been proud, but he may have wondered why, after 25 years, Dreyfuss hadn't come up with something better. Also in 1964, HDA designed the symbol for American Airlines, the Swinger camera for Polaroid, and the Trimline desk phone for AT&T, which would be accepted into the permanent collection of the Museum of Modern Art. George Nelson won a prestigious Alcoa award for Herman Miller's Action Office I, an innovative system of office furniture, but later it became known that Robert Propst had actually designed it. Like other major designers, Nelson claimed credit for all designs in his organization. Following in the footsteps of Raymond Loewy, Buckminster Fuller, now famous for his geodesic domes, was featured on the cover of Time magazine. Now it was time for the three separate industrial design organizations to get together. After much discussion and negotiation since 1958, the three industrial design organizations, the Industrial Designers Institute (IDI), the American Society of Industrial Designers (ASID), and the Industrial Design Education Association (IDEA), agreed to merge and form the Industrial Designers Society of America (IDSA) in 1965 to provide a single voice for industrial design in America. The first president of IDSA was Henry Dreyfuss, one of the founders of the SID in 1944, and the first chairman of the board was John Vassos, one of the founders of the ADI in 1938 (in 1951, it had become the IDI). Vassos had retired in 1964 as a consultant of RCA design, where Tucker Madawick had taken over as manager of radio, phonograph, tape and television design in 1959. ![](images/3373adc3b01cfd2b6a0614ded891866126b62e7c6d8d0236e376578faf749d51.jpg) John Vassos, ca. 1950s (courtesy Industrial Designers Society of America). IDSA membership at the time was estimated at 650. The first casualty was the IDI national design awards, which had been initiated in 1951. As we know, Dreyfuss did not like the idea of public awards because they insisted on identifying and crediting the specific designer by name. Big-name designers required that any design made in their firm must be credited to the principal's name, no matter who actually designed it. There would be no further national design awards after 1965 until 1980, when Dreyfuss would be gone. Russel Wright was invited to Japan in 1965 to work for more than 100 companies. Later, he would work with the Johnson administration to establish the Park Beautiful Program, forming an association with public land and its reclamation for public use. Alfonso Iannelli passed away in 1965 at the age of77 in Chicago. In 1955, he had sculpted the large-scale Rock of Gibraltar relief for the Prudential building (now called Prudential Plaza) in Chicago, which was his last major work. In 1982, the entire contents of Iannelli's studio would be purchased by Kelms Cott Gallery in Chicago. That same year, Egmont Arens passed away from a heart attack at age 78. He was one of the founders of both the Society of Industrial Designers (SID) in 1944 and the Package Design Council (PDC) in 1952. A month after his death, his widow, Mathilde, published a collection of his early poetry. She wrote that he would often say, “You know, I came to New York to be a poet." His papers are archived at Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. Winners of the 1966 Design in Housewares Award Program included Hamilton Beach's "hole-in-handle" electric knife and its Gourmet Center by Chapman, Goldsmith & Yamasaki, and West Bend's coffee percolator, broiler rotisserie, and Teflon-coated cookware by David Painter Industrial Designers.4 That same year, Raymond Loewy/William Snaith designed the Exxon logotype. George Nelson's office designed an exhibition, Hand Tools USA, for travel within the Soviet Union by the U.S. Information Agency (USIA) as a cultural exchange between the two Cold War countries in 1966. Another USIA exhibition, Industrial Design USA, would also be designed by Nelson's office in 1967. These exhibits, mentioned earlier, demonstrated the power of design as public diplomacy to change the hearts and minds of people and helped win the Cold War. In 1967, Raymond Loewy/William Snaith Associates, Inc., designed the trademark for the Shell Oil Company. That year, Raymond Loewy/William Snaith Associates, Inc., in Chicago was merged with Raymond Loewy/William Snaith Associates, Inc., in New YorkHenry Dreyfuss Associates designed a prototype videophone for AT&T. The HDA organization included James Conner, Niels Diffrient, and Donald Genaro. W. Dorwin Teague resigned from Walter Dorwin Teague Associates in 1967, which had been headed by Bob Harper since Walter Dorwin Teague passed away in 1960. Milton Immermann soon took over as president and CEO of WDTA, and Dorwin resigned to start his own business, Dorwin Teague, Inc., in Englewood Cliffs, New Jersey, which specialized in both industrial design and engineering.6 Peter Müller-Munk, head of Peter Müller-Munk Associates in Pittsburgh, died unexpectedly and tragically in 1967 at the age of 60. Müller-Munk had been instrumental in the formation of the International Council of Societies of Industrial Design. Joseph Platt, one of the founders of the Society of Industrial Designers in 1944, passed away at age 73 in 1968. That year, Henry Dreyfuss retired at age 64, the same year as the tragic assassinations of Martin Luther King, Jr., and Robert Kennedy. These events, the never-ending Vietnam War, antiwar student demonstrations, and desegregation struggles created a sense of national unrest and helplessness. They were emotional and traumatic times that would be lifted only temporarily in 1969, when U.S. astronauts first landed on the moon. The industrial design profession also suffered from mistrust by virtue of its prominent role in the corporate business world, which was now seen by many as being responsible for environmental damage, obscene profits, and neglect of global social responsibility. In 1968, R. Buckminster Fuller was awarded the World Medal of Architecture for his 250-foot-diameter, twenty-story-high geodesic dome that had housed the entire U.S. pavilion at Expo 67 in Montreal, Canada. Fortune magazine, which had introduced industrial design to the business world in 1934, now in 1968, exactly 34 years later, published an article, “The Decline of Industrial Designers," in February, claiming lack of innovation. This seemed to be more a matter of perception than reality. The profession continued as normal, even though many of its famous pioneers were aging or passing away, and design news was dominated by younger personalities, who were as innovative as ever. More likely, the headlines of civil unrest, campus rebellions, and other pressing social issues had pushed industrial designers and design off of the front pages. Many in IDSA were grappling with the organization's declining membership, financial problems, and its diminishing reputation among young designers, who were criticizing IDSA as an “old boy's club." There was a kernel of truth in this, since many of the “old guard” were longing to return to a time in their youth when leading industrial designers commanded top dollar, and were celebrated as national personalities. But this time had passed. Still, some of the "old guard” were still in the thick of things. In 1969, Detroit design firm Sundberg-Ferar designed the Bay Area Rapid Transit (BART) system in San Francisco, the first U.S. regional transit system since 1907. Frank Del Guidice now headed Walter Dorwin Teague Associates, and Raymond Loewy opened his third London, England, office. Nathan Horwitt designed wall clock No. 4601 for the Howard Miller Clock Company. Henry Dreyfuss Associates designed American Standard fixtures and fittings, and the model 500 helicopter for Hughes Aircraft. In Philadelphia, Clyde Shuler, designer of "tombstone" wooden radios of the early 1930s, passed away at age 77 in 1969. While regarded as passé at the time, the gothic shapes of his radios in the early 1930s were simply a logical extension of “skyscraper” furniture design of the 1920s. Shuler had taught industrial design at the Philadelphia College of Art since 1933 and had retired from there in 1952. He had established his own office, Clyde Schuler Associates, at 1717 Sansom Street, working on residential architecture and commercial design. By 1961, he was a partner in the office of his old friend, William Heyl Thompson. Boeing 747 “jumbo jets” made their first flights. Walter Dorwin Teague Associates (WDTA) designed Boeing interiors, and Henry Dreyfuss Associates (HDA) designed American Airlines interiors. Design in Steel design awards, initiated in 1963 by U.S. Steel, were awarded in 1969 to Henry Dreyfuss Associates for a transmission tower for the Southern California Edison Company, and to Sundberg-Ferar for a subway transit vehicle designed for the New York Metro Transportation Authority. ![](images/2bf05b3524e337b2ef4a172409dc75b75a6888f9e1b755f9da4fa257f4116c16.jpg) Carl Sundberg with Bay Area Regional Transit System, 1969 (courtesy Sundberg-Ferar, www.sundbergferar.com). ![](images/ab814f139a3536ac32085022eb6eaf4efff9dc00be32df39e9af761cd95ed6c6.jpg) Playmate cooler, by Marlan Polhemus of GYS. In Detroit, Harley Earl, the man who had most influenced automotive design over the previous 30 years, passed away at age 76 in 1969. They say he is still remembered at General Motors with this prayer: “Our Father, who art in Styling, Harley be thy Name." Amen to that. His private consulting firm had merged with Walter B. Ford II (1920–1991) Design Associates in 1964 to form Ford & Earl Design Associates. Ford had been in GM Styling under Earl. In 1970, Dave Chapman, Inc., of Chicago was reorganized with Paul Specht as president and partner in Goldsmith Yamasaki Specht, Inc. (GYS), which that year designed the ubiquitous Playmate plastic picnic cooler for the Igloo Corporation. Dreyfuss closed his Pasadena office in 1970, and his staff was relocated to 4 West 58th Street in New York, where Bill Purcell and Don Genaro were senior partners and Niels Diffrient and Jim Conner were junior partners. They moved the office to 888 Seventh Avenue with more space. In the early 1970s, Dreyfuss had sold his business to these four partners, and focused on international symbols by himself.7 ![](images/966d83eb4b3d60924a1321e9d8ba774f30566717153c0774fcfb0c7b88aa61fd.jpg) Henry Dreyfuss, ca. 1955 (courtesy Henry Dreyfuss Associates). Dreyfuss, representing the American National Standards Institute (ANSI), chaired the first meeting in 1971 of the International Organization of Standards' Technical Committee (ISO/ TC) in Berlin, which set international standards for 145 signs and symbols. In 1972, Dreyfuss would use these in his publication, the Symbol Sourcebook, a virtual encyclopedia of international symbols. HDA designed the Hyster lift equipment and the Flicker, a woman's razor for the American Safety Razor Company. Sadly, that same year, Henry Dreyfuss and his wife, Doris, both terminally ill, designed their own death by carbon monoxide at their home in South Pasadena. Until his death, Dreyfuss had been a faculty member in the engineering department at Caltech in Pasadena. The personal archives of Henry Dreyfuss would be donated to the Smithsonian in 1973. Henry Dreyfuss Associates, however, remained active and profitable. New associates Jim Ryan and Bill Crookes were hired. Industrial design now came under attack by Victor Papanek (1925–1998), chair of design at the Kansas City (Missouri) Art Institute, whose 1972 book, Design for the Real World, the book jacket of which characterized Papanek as “an ingenious designer who charges the industrial design establishment with criminal negligence of almost everybody," featured such declarations and accusations as, "It is about time that industrial design, as we have come to know it, should cease to exist," and, "Today, industrial design has put murder on a mass production basis." Papanek claimed the moral high ground in his book using his students' designs, which addressed child disability problems, created radio receivers made of juice cans and cottage industries for Third World countries, designed human-powered vehicles, and created packaging made of pea pods. Papanek's idealistic, socially conscious, and antibusiness philosophy appealed strongly to 20-year-old young adults who optimistically aspired to change the world, which to them appeared to be failing in many respects. They felt that their unique role, as a generation, was to challenge authority and the establishment to achieve change of any sort. Within a year, the Vietnam War would be over, conscription into the military would end, and the volunteer army began. The focus of youth would then be more on social and environmental issues. Over the years, change did occur in industrial design as well. Public design emphasis on profits and sales was reduced (although this still occurred), museum preoccupation with aesthetic purity was reduced (although this still occurred), and public commitment to solving social and environmental problems increased. The government increasingly became involved with legislation to address these social and environmental problems, and the design profession participated where it was relevant. In January 1973, the Industrial Designers Society of America (IDSA) relocated its national office from New York to Washington, D.C., where an expanding government was becoming activist in nature. IDSA was receiving grants for working with government programs, such as the Product Safety Commission and Barrier Free Design. IDSA at the time had a total membership of 723 and 10 regional chapters. New York, Chicago, and the West Coast were no longer the only centers of design. Manufacturers were moving south for lower wages, and small, local design offices were popping up all over the country. A 1973 study, "Industrial Design Education in the U.S.A.: A Factual Summary," by Joseph Carriero, chair of the Department of Design and Environmental Analysis at Cornell University, revealed that there were 41 higher education programs of industrial design, and approximately 2,000 students pursuing a career in industrial design, of which 363 were female and 1,737 male. There were also 21l graduate students pursuing master's degrees in 29 programs. Faculty members totaling 1882 were devoted to full-time teaching of industrial design, plus 129 part time, and 200 support people. That same year, Raymond Loewy/William Snaith Associates, Inc., designed the Skylab 2 space station for NASA, launched on May 25, 1973, a project Loewy regarded as the most important of his career. It was the first manned mission to Skylab, and the first U.S. orbital space station. Loewy emphasized habitability and comfort for the astronauts, with a wardroom for meals and relaxation, and a window to view the Earth and space. Skylab missions could last for months. Each astronaut had a private sleeping area the size of a walk-in closet, with a curtain, sleeping bag, and locker. That same year, Loewy signed a contract with the Soviet Union to be its consultant for mass production and over the next few years collaborated on the design of Russian automobiles, farm tractors, cameras, and hydrofoil vessels. Henry Dreyfuss Associates designed an open-sided street telephone booth for AT&T, a Bard Parker surgeon's scalpel, and the Polaroid SX-70 camera in 1973, and in 1974 published Humanscale 1/2/3, extensive ergonomic data and diagrams laminated in plastic for durability and frequent use for industrial designers to determine accurate dimensions for human use of products and equipment. President Nixon resigned as a result of the Watergate scandal, and Gerald Ford, vice president, took the office of president. It was in 1973 that Virgil Exner, who started in automotive design in 1933, passed away at age 64. He had a heart attack in 1956 but resumed work in 1957: In 1962 the president of Chrysler ordered him to reduce the size of their cars. The standard-size models Exner had already designed were downsized by associates over the objections of Exner. When they failed on the market, Exner was fired as the scapegoat. He retired in 1964 and was replaced by Elwood Engle (1917–1986). Exner continued consulting for various car companies from his office in Birmingham, Michigan, teaming up with his son, Virgil Exner, Jr., to design watercraft for the ![](images/b039f5bd25b66c7147bbae6cc9aa506ce07fbb13188f4ba9b23cb5cbb5c33072.jpg) Joseph “Jo” Sinel, ca. 1970s (Designscape magazine, February 1977). Buehler Corporation. He also designed “revival cars," including the revival of Stutz in the 1970s. The year 1975 saw the passing of Greta von Nessen in Murraysville, Pennsylvania, at age 75. She and her husband, Walter von Nessen (1889–1943), founded Nessen Studios in 1927 to design lamps. She continued the family studio, which survives to this day as Nessen Lamps, Inc. That same year, Jo Sinel, one of the founders of the Society of Industrial Designers, passed away at age 86. A New Zealander who was designing products in 1923 for an advertising agency in New York, and eventually worked for 55 such agencies, he became a noted industrial designer and educator. His papers are archived at the California College of Arts, where he taught for so many years, and where he received an honorary doctorate of fine arts. The Smithsonian's Renwick Gallery in Washington, D.C., in 1975 mounted a retrospective exhibition of the work of Raymond Loewy, which included his Studebaker Avanti. In 1974, Loewy's partner, William Snaith, had died, and the Loewy firm was nearing bankruptcy. By 1975, Loewy had already established Raymond Loewy International by merging all his coexisting organizations. That year, Henry Dreyfuss Associates designed the Athena 2000 sewing machine, the International Women's Year symbol for the United Nations, and in 1976 Polaroid's Pronto camera. The Concorde supersonic jet began service in 1976 with interiors designed by Raymond Loewy/William Snaith, and the Cooper-Hewitt Museum in the Andrew Carnegie mansion in New York became the National Design Museum, a branch of the Smithsonian. Russel Wright, one of the founders of the Society of Industrial Designers in 1944, passed away in 1976 at the age of 72. He had transformed his 79 acres of quarry land in Garrison, New York, into a natural scenic wonder called Manitoga. His papers are at Syracuse University Library, Special Collections, and at Manitoga, operated by the Russel Wright Design Center. Manitoga is designated as a National Historic Landmark by the U.S. Department of the Interior and is open to the public, with tours and hiking trails. Also in 1976, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates designed the Kenworth Sleeper truck, the first to house a sleeper cabin. The design enabled more effective and efficient long-haul driving. In 1977, the Raymond Loewy/William Snaith, Inc., office in New York closed under Chapter 11 bankruptcy, and industrial design operations were sold to former employee David Butler to form Lister and Butler, Inc. Loewy's offices in Paris, London, and Sweden remained open. In 1976, Raymond Loewy International had been sold to Sheridan Funding Corporation, James Sheridan, president. He established Museum Administration and Planning Services, Inc. (MAP), as a subsidiary and opened an office in Atlanta, Georgia. Function and Vision, Inc., was formed as "debtor in possession" to handle liquidation of Raymond Loewy International, with James Sheridan as president. Sheridan Associates was also established, specializing in interior design.8 George A. Beck, who began his career in 1931, passed away at age 69 in 1977 after retiring from General Electric; Eliot Noyes, the head of the Museum of Modern Art's first industrial design department in 1940, also passed away at age 67; and Otto Kuhler, designer of early streamlined 1930s steam locomotives, who had relocated in 1969 to Santa Fe, New Mexico, and in 1976 to Denver, Colorado, died at age 83. By 1977, the International Style architecture of Walter Gropius and Mies van der Rohe (both had died in 1969), which had been promoted by the Museum of Modern Art from 1932 throughout the 1960s, was coming under increased criticism as sterile, bland, and characterless, or worse, hostile to the natural environment and humanity. That year, the groundbreaking design of the Pompidou Center in Paris, a complex of public library, museum of modern art, and music center, violated all the International Style rules by its dramatic “inside out," hightech look, with exposed plumbing; electrical, ventilation and safety systems in bright color codes; and with exterior staircases, somewhat like antique urban fire escapes. Architects began abandoning the International Style by designing buildings that blended in with the surrounding buildings, rather than standing alone as rectangular, glass-box monuments to modern design. The term “postmodern” began to be applied to such architecture, which was generally described as witty, hybrid, eclectic, and concerned with issues of history, local context, metaphor, and ambiguity. It was a sea change in architectural style that would soon find its way into product design. Henry Dreyfuss Associates designed the Singer Touchtronic sewing machine in 1978. That year, Dave Chapman, who had worked as a designer at Chicago's Century of Progress Exhibition in 1933–1934 and founded Chicago's most successful industrial design firm in 1936, passed away at age 69, but his firm would survive under new management as Goldsmith Yamasaki Specht. Chapman's papers are at Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. Charles Eames, who dominated postwar furniture design, also died that year at age 71. Deskey, Inc., was founded in Cincinnati, Ohio, in 1978 as a division of Donald Deskey Associates of New York. During the 1970s, the computer was becoming the center of a technological and informational revolution. The first prototype personal computer, the Altos desktop, appeared in 1974; in 1975, 19-vear-old William Gates III and 22-year-old Paul Allen founded Microsoft; in 1977, Steven G. Wosniac and 21-year-old Steven Jobs founded Apple Computer in a California garage; in 1978, the first electronic typewriters with memory appeared; and in 1979, Sony introduced its Walkman. By 1983, the first cell phones would appear. These new technologies would generate hundreds of new consumer products, and when they entered mass production, industrial designers would design most of them. Technology continued to provide the raw material that industrial designers transformed into products for humans. By 1979, membership in the Industrial Designers Society of America (IDSA) would top 2,000. It had taken organizational steps over the previous few years to replace its leadership with younger members, and to address some of their concerns. IDSA was no longer a sort of small private club governed autocratically by an older generation, including many of the pioneers, to be sure, but by a more democratic representation of younger chapter chairmen on the board. Membership requirements were relaxed, automotive stylists were included, and it became professionally managed by specialists in association management, rather than by a closed-room board of past presidents. Chapters had doubled to 20, as had membership, and financial health was restored. There were 675 consulting offices nationwide, and over 6,000 industrial designers working within corporations. At the time, it was estimated that there were 10,000 industrial designers worldwide. Also in 1979, Raymond Loewy published Industrial Design, a comprehensive, illustrated book about his design career. He was the surviving member of the original Big Three founders of the profession, and therefore still a public figure who was featured in any historical review of industrial design. The Industrial Designers Society of America initiated annual national design awards in 1980, known as the Industrial Design Excellence Awards (IDEA), which were presented at IDSA's annual meeting that year, and which continue to this day. They were the first such awards since 1965, when the first IDSA president, Henry Dreyfuss, shut down the previous IDI awards. Also in 1980, Henry Dreyfuss Associates designed the Polaroid 600 Sun Camera and published Humanscale 4/5/6, additional ergonomic reference tools for industrial designers, following Humanscale 1/2/3, published in 1974. The final publication in this valuable series would be Humanscale 7/8/9, published in 1991. Helen Dryden, who was the “highest-paid woman artist of 1927” and a famous cover illustrator of Vogue magazine in the 1920s, passed away in 1981 at age 94. Since 1937, when she designed the interior of the Studebaker as Raymond Loewy designed the exterior, the only thing known about her was that in 1956 she was living in a \$10-a-week hotel room paid for by New York City's Welfare Department. At the time, she referred nostalgically to her (1925) \$200-a-month Tenth Street apartment in New York. Other pioneer designers were passing. Both Montgomery Ferar and his partner at Sundberg-Ferar since its founding in 1934, Carl W. Sundberg, died in 1982, both at age 72. Sundberg had retired in 1975. From 1970 to 1980, Ferar had taught industrial design at the Center for Creative Studies in Detroit. The firm they established continued active practice after their death and is still in business today. Raymond Dietrich, the well-known automotive designer, passed away at age 88 in 1982. He had left Chrysler in 1940; founded Ray Dietrich, Inc., in 1949; worked for Checker Cab in 1953; and for the Lincoln and Mercury divisions at Ford until his retirement to Albuquerque, New Mexico, in 1969. His wife, Marion, donated his papers and records in 1995 to the Gilmore Classic Car Club of America Museum at Hickory Corners, Michigan. In 1983, George Nelson installed an exhibit, Design since 1945, organized by Katherine Hiesinger, at the Philadelphia Museum of Art, along with a 247-page, illustrated catalog of the exhibition by Hiesinger. The exhibition and catalog were a sort of obituary of modern design or “Good Design" as defined by the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) from 1950 to 1955. At the time of the exhibit, museums were no longer trying to dictate good design but were focused on its history, a sure sign of age. As early as 1979, architectural and design critic Ada Louise Huxtable (1921–2013) had written about the difference between the Bauhaus/Museum of Modern Art concept of industrial design in the 1950s versus the pragmatic and successful reality of industrial design as developed and implemented by the designers of the Machine Age: Those who think they know how it was in those days [the 1950s] are in for a surprise. What was admired then is discarded now, and what was rejected is being revived.... Industrial design, that curious amalgam of promotion, marketing, technology, and taste invented by the 20th century to serve and shape a consumer economy ... [whose practitioners] shared the optimism of the architectural theorists ... who believed that the marriage of art and industry could provide answers for most of the ills of society, or at least put beauty and utility within the reach of all. But while the tastemakers were in hot pursuit of a mythical "machine art" realized largely in handmade prototypes or limited production for educated users, the industrial designers were successfully creating mass-produced objects for a universal market.9 However, the pragmatism, simplicity and repetition of so-called Good Design had its limits. The design community was searching, as it often had, for new styles, new looks, and new directions.


---

## FILE `分析报告\chapters\14_Chapter_14.txt`

- category: `other`
- sha256: `419db6bb07be63f5e4f13784145bf3e640d7203f17991758f9c8041174ae32e1`
- characters: 36846

# Postmodern and Passages Finally, after 30 years, designers were tiring of the black boxes and white cubes of Bauhausstyle modern design. It was Italians who broke the ice in 1981, when an avant-garde Milanese group founded an organization called Memphis. It created the design media event of the year with garish furniture designs with bold colors and laminates that emphasized surface decoration more than function. Presented to the beat of rock music in the manner of a fashion show, the designs were deliberately designed to be antifunctional, preposterous, and faddish. They were colorful, cheerful, and playful, the direct opposite of Bauhaus traditions. One of the designs, the Casablanca sideboard, would become the icon of Memphis. By 1984, some U.S. educators were seeking new theories to free design from the formal dictates of so-called modern design. They came up with likening design to language, which they called “product semantics," defined as, “The study of the symbolic qualities of man-made forms in the context of their use and the application of this knowledge to industrial design. It takes into account not only the physical and physiological functions, but the psychological, social, and cultural context, which we call the symbolic environment." Got that? Although this was a highly academic and theoretical theory, many designers began to abandon the formal “good design” of the 1950s. Many new designs were being described as “postmodern," which meant that they had departed from the formal, Bauhaus-style, simple, geometric style to more informal, expressive, humanistic, and playful styles. These included cameras that suggested humanoid facial features, modern furniture that imitated 18th-century styles such as Sheraton or Chippendale, teakettles with a little bird whistle on the spout, a bathroom shower squeegee with S-shaped forms suggesting waves, and travel irons that looked like miniature steamships. In 1990, Alessi would introduce a lemon juicer called the Juicy Salif that looked like an insect and would become a cult object. In effect, design now offered an infinite variety of styles that could just as easily be based on traditional historical forms as those of future science fiction. The “ideal” style was that which best fit the specific individual product's function and usage and its primary audience, the consumer. Ironically, these stylistic modifications were little different conceptually from the highly criticized (by elite modernists of the 1950s) streamlining or skyscraper styles of the 1930s, which were simply 50 years ahead of their time! One can only wonder what the remaining pioneer machine designers of the 1930s thought of this deja-vu development. Unfortunately, many of them were disappearing. R. Buckminster Fuller, inventor of the geodesic dome, passed away at age 88 in 1983. In 1985, he would be posthumously honored with the naming of a newly created form of carbon molecule (C-60) as "buckminsterfullerene" (nicknamed "buckyballs") and others in its class (fullerenes), such as “buckytubes," discovered in 1991, because the carbon molecule C-60 resembled a geodesic dome. Gordon W. Florian, who had started with General Electric in 1934, passed away in 1984 at age 75. He had designed a locomotive in 1940 for GE when he was assistant director to Ray Patten, but he left GE in 1946 to open his own office. He taught design at the Junior College of Connecticut, which became the University of Bridgeport after the war, and in 1949 he established a four-year industrial design program there. From 1950 to 1954 he lectured to engineering and business students at MIT. A lifelong artist of landscapes and a member of the Connecticut Watercolor Society, he exhibited all over the East Coast, including Silvermine. In 1984, he was named Fairfield (Connecticut) “Artist of the Year." Lurelle Guild, a prolific industrial designer since 1924, passed away at age 88 in his home in Darien, Connecticut, in 1985 after a long career. He had practiced design until the 1960s. His papers are at the Syracuse University Library, Special Collections, and his products are in various museums. He had been born in Syracuse and studied at the university. In the mid-1980s, Henry Dreyfuss Associates was coming apart. The office had moved to 423 West 54th Street. Partners were Don Genaro, Jim Conner, Bill Crookes, Gordon Sylvester, Jim Ryan, and Jack McGarvey. Several associates started a separate firm and took with them other employees and most of the client base. Bill Crookes bought out the remaining partners and what clients were left, mostly John Deere Agricultural Products, and moved the office to Ann Arbor, Michigan.¹ In 1985, Worldesign 85, the first Congress of the International Council of Societies of Industrial Design (ICSID), was held in the United States, hosted by the Industrial Designers Society of America (IDSA). International registrants of the conference voted Charles Eames the "Most Influential Designer of the 20th Century." John Vassos, one of the founders of the American Designers' Institute in 1938, and RCA design head from 1931, also passed away in 1985 at age 93. He had been president of the Silvermine Guild of Artists and Writers in Connecticut in 1936, 1940–1947, and 1949–1955, and had retired from heading RCA design in about 1959. In 1977, the last retrospective of his work was held at the Silvermine Guild in Connecticut. In poor health and nearly blind, Vassos remained a feisty old man until the end. Vassos's papers are also at the Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. The year 1986 saw the passing of more pioneer designers. John Morgan, Sears, Roebuck's first designer in 1934 with dozens of design patents, passed away at age 83 in McHenry, Illinois. After his retirement, he had continued his successful career in fine art. Ray Sandin, Hotpoint's first designer in 1935, died at age 76 after serving Hotpoint for 51 years. George Nelson, the man who alerted the business world to industrial design in 1934, passed away at age 78. He revolutionized furniture design at Herman Miller after the war and invented the concept of pedestrian malls. His papers are at the Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. Carl Otto is estimated to have died around this time, but there is virtually no information about him since he closed his office in 1956. He was last known to be in England. Raymond Loewy, one of the Big Three industrial designers, died in 1986 at age 93. He had retired in 1980 at the age of 87 and had returned to his native France, where he passed away at his Monte Carlo residence. In 1992, his second wife, Viola, and the British American Tobacco Company would establish the Raymond Loewy Foundation in Hamburg, Germany, to promote industrial design internationally. An annual award of €50,000 was granted annually to outstanding designers. Loewy's daughter, Laurence, in 1998 would establish Loewy Design in Atlanta, Georgia, to manage her father's interests in the United States. Laurence would die in 2008, and her husband, David Hagerman, would manage Loewy Design and the Loewy estate, which would catalog the Loewy archives and raise funds to open the Raymond Loewy Museum of Industrial Design. Collections of Loewy archives are in the Raymond Loewy Collection at the Hagley Museum in Wilmington, Delaware, and in the Manuscript Division of the Library of Congress in Washington, D.C. The Brooklyn Museum in 1986 published The Machine Age in America, 1918–1941 and organized a related exhibition in Brooklyn from October 17, 1986, to February 16, 1987. The exhibit would continue at the Museum of Art, Carnegie Institute, in Pittsburgh from April 4 to June 28, 1987; at the Los Angeles County Museum of Art from August 16 to October 18, 1987; and would conclude at the High Museum of Art, Atlanta, Georgia, from December 1, 1987, to February 14, 1988. The exhibition and book was a masterful retrospective of this important era of American modern design. In 1987, the National Endowment for the Arts presented a five-part television series on PBS, America by Design, exploring the history of American architecture and design. Raymond Loewy dominated industrial design history in the series. That same year, Jean Reinecke, who had opened his industrial design office in Chicago in 1930, died in Pasadena, California, at the age of 78. He had closed his Chicago office in 1974 and had continued designing until 1986 in his Pasadena office, where he was also on the Council of Advisors of the Art Center College of Design. In 1988, Edwin W. Fuerst, who established his first Toledo design studio in 1929, passed away. He had taught industrial design at Ohio State University from 1948 to 1949. In the mid– 1950s he became a freelance painter and moved to New England, becoming special assistant to the vice president of sales for the Plax Corporation in Hartford, Connecticut, from which he ![](images/4dcc4f87dffab2395328bf6faced9c6c1ff11cc7aaa49254bbb2a3527aa38725.jpg) Jean Reinecke, ca. 1970s (courtesy Barbra Reinecke Sedory). retired in 1966. George Sakier, one of the founders of the Society of Industrial Designers (SID), died at the age of 91, also in 1988. He had designed glassware for Fostoria Glass from 1929 to 1979, when he retired to continue his lifelong career as a painter of abstract landscapes, with natural elements reduced to geometric forms. His paintings included Red Mesa, #011, and Collage C008. Yale University initiated a George Sakier Prize for excellence in photography. Donald Deskey, a prominent industrial designer since 1926, and one of the founders of the Society of Industrial Designers, died in 1989 at the age of 94 in Vero Beach, Florida, the town to which he retired in 1975. In 1988, Deskey had exhibited a one-man show, Donald Deskey, American Modern Design, an exhibit of his furniture designs from the 1920s and 1930s, at Fifty/50, a Manhattan gallery. After the exhibit he donated his papers to the Cooper-Hewitt National Design Museum in New York. Several pioneer industrial designers passed away in 1990. Nathan Horwitt, the designer of the classic Museum Watch in 1947, passed away at his farm in Stockbridge, Massachusetts, at the age of 92. Between 1945 and 1948, his farm was used for training exercises of the Jewish underground force, the Irgun. Horwitt supported the creation of the state of Israel and served as a member of the American League for a Free Palestine. For these efforts, in 1980, he had been awarded the Jabotinsky Medal by the prime minister of Israel, Menachem Begin. Leo Jiranek, a prominent furniture designer since 1924, passed away at age 90. In the 1960s he had founded and was president of the Jiranek School of Furniture Design and Technology in New York City, and in 1984 he was awarded a doctor of arts degree by the Kendall College of Art and Design. Gordon Buehrig, noted automotive designer, died at age 86. He had retired from Ford in 1965 and taught for five years at the Art Center College of Design in Pasadena, California. In 1979, he designed the Buehrig motorcar, a limited-production carriage-roof coupe. In 1981, he was named by the Society of Automotive Historians as one of the most significant contributors to the development of motorcars. In the decade before his death, he directed precision car models for the Franklin Mint, and in 1990 he was inducted into the Automotive Hall of Fame. Robert Lepper, a faculty member in Carnegie Tech's first degree program in industrial design in 1934, died in Pittsburgh in 1991 at the age of 85. Lepper had remained on the faculty in the program until his retirement in 1975. Andy Warhol had been one of his students from 1945 to 1949. In 1986, Lepper was prominently included in the Brooklyn Museum's exhibition and book, The Machine Age in America, 1918–1941, which regarded his murals of the 1930s and 1940s as rivaling those of Diego Rivera. In 1989, Lepper had received the Industrial Design Society of America's (IDSA) educator award. In 1991, Business Week magazine sponsored the annual IDSA Industrial Design Excellence Awards (IDEA), then celebrating ten years of operation, and featured the awards in a major, illustrated article in the magazine. This annual recognition of industrial design in a major business magazine benefited both the manufacturers, the 800 to 1,000 design offices in the country, and the individual designers. The number of awards would vary from year to year, but by 1997 IDEA would confer a total of 141 gold, silver, and bronze awards. Graduate students at the Pratt Institute in Brooklyn formed the Association of Women Industrial Designers (AWID) in 1991. Their key collaborative project would be a 1994 exhibition at the Pratt Manhattan Gallery in New York and a related catalog with the title, Goddess is in the Details: Product Design by Women. H. Creston Doner, who opened a design office in Akron, Ohio, in 1932, and was a longtime design director at Libby-Owens-Ford Glass Company, passed away in 1991 in Toledo at age 88. He had retired from LOF after 30 years in 1968, and from 1970 he was a member of the Toledo Society of Communicating Arts. At the end of 1991, the Soviet Union also came to an end and collapsed to become a republic of individual states, ending the Cold War and leading to a better life for Russian citizens. Clare Hodgman, who started his career with General Motors in the early 1930s, died in 1992 at the age of 81. He had retired from Hodgman & Bourke in 1969 and moved to Daytona, Florida, where he excelled in contemporary watercolors. George Walker, who began his career in the automotive industry in 1928, died at the age of 97 in 1993. In 1959, he was a guest challenger on the TV panel show To Tell the Truth. After retiring from Ford in 1961, he moved to Gulf Stream, Florida, where he became mayor in 1976. By 1985, he was in Tucson, Arizona, where he was interviewed by the Ford Design History Center. In 1994, President Bill Clinton presented eight Presidential Awards for Design Excellence to designers, including industrial designers. The automobile industry rebounded in 1994 to capture 75 percent of the U.S. market share, and for the first time in 14 years led the world in car production. But many other corporations were in serious trouble. One hundred fifty-one Fortune 500 companies, or 30 percent, lost money in 1993. The previous decade had been a litany of mergers and acquisitions, as many companies sought to convert failing markets into cash. By this time, computer aided design (CAD) was driving the manufacturing, engineering, and industrial design of products. Installation of CAD technology was revolutionizing inhouse and consulting design firms across the industry. The historical restrictions of machine tools that controlled design were now replaced by computer-aided tooling that enabled designers to specify complex shapes without regard to those earlier physical restrictions. CAD proficiency was a prerequisite job requirement for all industrial designers and for anyone in the product development field. The days of hand-drawn renderings of product concepts were over, replaced by realistic computer-generated illustrations. In 1995, Stowe Myers, who had been hired by Norman Bel Geddes in 1932 to illustrate Bel Geddes's book, Horizons, died in 1995 at age 89 in New Orleans. He had been a member of the faculty at the Institute of Design of the Illinois Institute of Technology, where he taught a class entitled “Design Counsel." Myers retired in 1976 to paint and was named official artist of the U.S. Air Force in 1978. In 1988, he moved to New Orleans, Louisiana. His papers are at the Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. In 1995, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates designed Power Rangers toys. Brooks Stevens, one of the founders of the Society of Industrial Designers in 1944, passed away that same year at age 84. His son, Kipp K. Stevens (b. 1951), had joined Brooks Stevens Design Associates in 1978 as vice president and in 1979 had become president and CEO. From 1983 until his death, Brooks taught design at the Milwaukee Institute of Art and Design. In 1996, John M. Little, who had opened an industrial design office in Minneapolis in 1931, passed away in 1996 at the age of 90. He had joined Harold Van Doren's Toledo office in 1936 and managed it after the war. He was a lecturer at such universities as Harvard, Lehigh, Cincinnati, Toledo, and Wisconsin. Robert E. Bourke, designer of Studebakers for Loewy, passed away that same year at the age of 80. After his partner, Clare Hodgman, retired from their partnership, Hodgman & Bourke, in 1969, Bourke had moved his office to Westport, Connecticut, where his home was located. ![](images/c3c1898af301f7369525084b557f562ce85e51b8604916c913e767fe5686f092.jpg) Brooks Stevens (Brooks Stevens Archive, Milwaukee Art Museum, gift of the Brooks Stevens Family and the Milwaukee Institute of Art and Design). In Chicago, the firm of Goldsmith Yamasaki Specht (GYS), with Paul Specht as the last principal, was sold in 1997 to Sundberg-Ferar, for whom Specht served as senior design council until 2000. Sundberg-Ferar maintained four offices in the United States and one in the Far East. Also in 1997, the Cooper-Hewitt National Design Museum opened a retrospective, Henry Dreyfuss Directing Design: The Industrial Designer and His Work. Concurrently, Rizzoli International published Henry Dreyfuss, Industrial Designer: The Man in the Brown Suit, by Russell Flinchum, of the museum. A 1997 Time magazine article, Where the Jobs Are, predicted 76,000 new product design jobs by 2005 and called industrial design “one of the hottest professions in terms of job growth and compensation." The forecast was based on figures from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, which stated that industrial designers, then totaling 9,000, were among those related professions and were expected to number 21,000 by 2005. In 1999, Alfons Bach, who had established his furniture design firm in New York City in 1932, passed away at age 95. He had developed the Ridgeway Shopping Center in Stamford, Connecticut, in 1945, and after relocating to Palm Beach, Florida, in 1959, he developed the Palm Trail Plaza in Delray Beach. He retired to Pensacola, Florida, in 1992, where he continued his career as a watercolor artist. In 1997, he was honored at a reception for an exhibition of his work at the Visual Arts Gallery of Pensacola Junior College. An extensive archive of his drawings and papers is at the Cooper-Hewitt National Design Museum in New York. Belle Kogan, a successful female industrial designer who opened her own office in New York in 1932, died in 2000 in Israel at the age of 98. She had closed her office in 1970; moved to Israel under contract with K.V. Design, a subsidiary of the Koor Corporation; and set up a studio offering comprehensive design services. In 1972 she left the studio to work as a consultant. In the March 20, 2000, issue of Time magazine, an article, "The Rebirth of Design," subtitled, “The Redesigning of America," claimed that form was the dominant characteristic of many product designs. Forms were soft, voluptuous, highly ergonomic and even exotic. The "black boxes" of the 1970s were definitely out. Bauhaus geometric shapes were out. The color and form of functional details such as keys, levers, and switches had become the new decorative ornamentation of the era. The turn of the century was a big year for retrospectives. The Metropolitan Museum of Art opened an exhibition, American Modern, 1925–1940: Design for a New Age, which traveled the country for several years. The Bard Graduate Center for Studies in the Decorative Arts mounted another exhibit, Women Designers in the U.S.A., 1900–2000: Diversity and Difference, along with a related book, which included work by a number of female designers. NASA launched the International Space Station in 2000, with the crew quarters designed by Walter Dorwin Teague Associates (WDTA). The station is the area of a football field, weighs 861,804 pounds, and has been in continuous operation since 2000. In 2001, WDTA would work with Microsoft in the design of their first gaming console, the Xbox. By 2000, the Industrial Designers Society of America (IDSA) had 3,300 members in 28 chapters around the country, and by 2001, it had relocated to new headquarters near Dulles airport in Washington, D.C. In 2002, IDSA and the China Industrial Design Association (CIDA) sponsored a China-America education conference in Beijing. U.S. companies had been using Asian manufacturers for years to produce products at lower costs, and the number of U.S. designers traveling to China, South Korea, and Taiwan was increasing dramatically. China was producing thousands of industrial design graduates and competing with American practitioners. Arthur BecVar, who began his career in industrial design in Cleveland in 1935, died in 2003, at the age of 91. He had retired from General Electric as head of design in Louisville, Kentucky, in 1976, after more than 30 years of service. His papers are at the Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. Also in April 2003, the Cooper-Hewitt National Design Museum in New York tried to capture the current state of design with an exhibition, National Design Triennial: Inside Design Now. The museum found it difficult to categorize a dominant style in the eclectic menu of what many called “postmodern," so the catalog simply referred to the body of work as being "by contemporary American designers." Some designs strayed so far from the geometric Bauhaus style of the 1950s that a magazine editor remarked, "Ornament is back and we've got to relearn how to use it." W. Dorwin Teague, Jr., son of Walter Dorwin Teague, who started his career with his father in 1931, passed away at the age of 94 in 2004. He had relocated his Englewood Cliffs office to his home in the early 1970s and continued to design ski equipment, a water-powered toothbrush, a bicycle brake, and a snowmaking apparatus, among many others, until 1993, when he arranged for his papers to be donated to the Henry Ford Museum. Renowned designer John Barratt joined the WDTA team as CEO. Eugene "Bob" Gregorie, head of design at Ford from 1935 to 1946, also died in 2004 at the age of 96. In 1943 he had retired to design and sail his boat, and in the mid–1970s he had relocated to St. Augústine, Florida. In 2004, the annual Industrial Design Excellence Awards (IDEA) became international in scope, open to all designers around the world. Of the 1,300 entries, 305 were from other countries, clear recognition that industrial design had become an integrated global profession. A 2004 IDSA survey of 183 corporate design offices reported that design increased product sales by 47 percent and increased sales prices by 32 percent, while only increasing product cost by 24 percent. The numbers suggested that industrial design was still an essential business tool for manufacturing profitability. By 2006, the Industrial Design Excellence Awards ceremony at IDSA's annual conference was becoming quite sophisticated, with black ties optional, and with an ambience not unlike Hollywood's Oscar Awards. It was the number of gold awards to design teams from Asia that was a surprise: 25 percent of the total. This was compared to 8 percent in 2004 and 2005. Early in 2006, China's president, Hu Jintau, at a national conference on technology, had confirmed the country's new emphasis on design and innovation, stating, "Innovation is the core of the nation's competitiveness. Only with strong capacity for innovation can a country win the initiative in the international competition."² American educators teaching design in China confirmed that there was excitement in the air in China, reminiscent of the postwar U.S. attitude of“can do” in the 1950s and 1960s. Over the next few years, Asian design would increase in quality and quantity, and American designers would often be working in China, Taiwan, and South Korea.³ A permanent Raymond Loewy Gallery, emphasizing Loewy's transportation designs, opened in 2006 at the O. Winston Link Museum in Roanoke, Virginia. The museum was housed in the restored Norfolk & Western Railway Station, originally designed by Loewy's firm in 1949. Loewy's daughter, Laurence Loewy, was instrumental in establishing the gallery. That same year, Loewy was celebrated as “the Father of American Design" in a symposium held by the Hagley Museum in Wilmington, Delaware, where Loewy is prominent in the Pennsylvania Railroad Corporation records held by the museum. In 2007, the name of IDSA's Industrial Design Excellence Awards competition was changed to the International Design Excellence Awards to recognize that the competition, ![](images/dccd6baa4609fbd552872a184d4117760a1d15347e5278c5990e911f2ac5bca4.jpg) Viktor Schreckengost, ca. 2000 (photograph by Herb Ascherman). and industrial design in general, was now global in scope. In 2008, Adolph “Al" Lambach, Sr., who had begun his career as a furniture designer in Grand Rapids, Michigan, in 1932, died at the age of 96 in California. As a founding member of the American Designers' Institute (ADI) in 1938, he was a member of organized design, including IDSA, for a total of70 years, probably a record. He had worked as a freelance furniture designer until his retirement in 1982, when he carved replicas of famous ships for his collection and taught at the Art Center College of Design. He donated his many drawings to the industrial design collections at the Hagley Library in Wilmington, Delaware, and to Syracuse University Library, Special Collections. Also in 2008, Viktor Schreckengost, who developed an early industrial design program at the Cleveland Institute of Art in 1933, passed away at the age of 101. In 2000, he had been honored with a retrospective exhibition and catalog at the Cleveland Museum of Art, called Viktor Schreckengost and 20th Century Design. Soon after, the Viktor Schreckengost Foundation was established in Cleveland. In 2006, a Viktor Schreckengost National Centennial Exhibition celebrated his 100th birthday by highlighting his work in 140 simultaneous nationwide exhibitions. That year, he was also honored at the White House as a recipient of the National Medal of Arts for “enduring contributions to the artistic life" of the United States. By 2008, Business Week's annual publication of the IDEA international design awards noted that a strong sense of social responsibility ran through many of the winning entries, including a wheelchair that freed hands for disabled athletes; an inexpensive computer laptop for children in Third World countries; and a book that helped governments to design clearer, more useful ballots and polling place signage. Design buzzwords such as “green," "sustainability," "environmental, and "disability" permeated design competitions, conferences, education, and debates. In 2010, sponsorship of IDEA design awards would be taken over by Fast Company magazine. As Barack Obama was elected the first African-American president in 2008, professor Eric Anderson of Carnegie Mellon University was elected IDSA's first African-American president, and African-American industrial designer Charles Harrison (b. 1931) received a Lifetime Achievement Award at the White House as part of the Cooper-Hewitt National Design Museum awards program. Harrison, the first African-American executive hired at the company's Chicago headquarters, was hired by Sears in 1961 and worked there until his retirement in 1993. By 2009, there were about 50,000 industrial designers, of which about 3,500 were members of IDSA. Median annual salaries were around \$55,000. This was at the depth of the severe financial recession, when unemployment reached 10 percent and Chrysler and General Motors received bailout funds from the government. But industrial design is traditionally a recessionproof profession, and in 2009 there were over 20 position advertisements on the IDSA website, and thousands more posted on the Internet. IDSA had a staff of 12, an annual budget of about \$5 million, and 27 chapters in five national districts. By 2009, in the annual International Design Excellence Awards, there would be 1,631 entries, and 66 of 150 awards would go to 15 countries other than the United States. In 2011, the U.S. Postal Service issued stamps honoring 12 of the nation's most important and influential industrial designers with an image of a design created by each designer. They were Frederick Hurten Rhead (1936 Fiesta dinnerware), Walter Dorwin Teague (1934 Baby Brownie camera); Norman Bel Geddes (1940 Patriot radio), Raymond Loewy (1934 pencil sharpener), Donald Deskey (1927 table lamp), Gilbert Rohde (1933 clock), Greta Von Nessen (1951 Anywhere lamp), Russel Wright (1950 Highlight/Pinchware tableware), Henry Dreyfuss (1937 Model 302 AT&T telephone), Peter Müller-Munk (1935 Normandie pitcher), Dave Chapman (1947 streamlined sewing machines), and Elliot Noyes (1961 Selectric typewriter). They are all gone now, those extraordinary designers of the Machine Age, and many are forgotten. Their proud legacy is today's prospering profession of industrial design, which they invented in the 1930s, practically out of thin air, by transforming our everyday environment of 19th-century, unwieldy, unattractive, mechanical devices, from automobiles to toasters, office equipment, and kitchens, into sleek and attractive works of art that enhance our daily experiences emotionally and visually, as well as functionally. Today, we regard our man-made physical environment of mechanical, electronic, and digital devices as an unremarkable and a natural result of modern technology, as if they magically appeared out of nowhere. But in 1930, neither technology nor engineering knew how to shape the products it produced by the millions into forms that were designed to address human convenience, utilization, or emotional needs that made them attractive and desirable to consumers. Ordinary artists, the Machine Age designers of the 1930s, bravely ventured from their comfortable lives into a new world of complex, dirty factories, business offices, and brutal sales competition in order to transform everyday products into modern design, which encouraged people in the depths of the Depression to visualize a better future and lifted a failing manufacturing industry into profitability. A recovered economy led to the highest standard of living in the world after World War II, and the machine age designers established an educational system and business models for the new profession that became integrated into the modern world of manufacturing, production, and sales. Today's industrial designers perform the same services for manufacturers and consumers that the machine designers of the 1930s did, only better and faster. The process remains the same, except that instead of hand-drawn illustrations of proposed designs and manually prepared mechanical drawings, computers now perform these tasks with the instructions of designers. Prototype models are often not built by hand but generated into three dimensions by computers. Contrary to many pessimists in the 1960s, who concluded that all consumer products had already been “designed" to perfection, technology continues to generate new products that need to be shaped and modified for human use and understanding, and this is likely to continue indefinitely. Many of the organizations and designers mentioned in this book ceased operations over the years due to death or business closure, but, surprisingly, quite a few of the design organizations of the founders survive to this day, a tribute to the tenacity of their founders and a source of pride to their heirs. They continue to design the world about us. In 2006, Walter Dorwin Teague Associates, founded in 1926, now called Teague, in Seattle, Washington, celebrated its 80th anniversary as a leading force in industrial design and its 60-year working relationship with Boeing, with a Frank Gehry-designed Experience Music Project held in downtown Seattle, and an elegant publication of Teague history and designs, Design This Day, the same title as his 1940 book. In 201l, Teague's relationship continued with Boeing with the interior design of the 787. That same year, Teague acquired a Munichbased design studio to expand into the European market. Teague staff in 2012 totaled 250 designers and support staff. In 2012, the Art and Colour Section of General Motors that Harley Earl founded in 1927 celebrated its 85th anniversary, and it continues to design new cars each year, most of them still "streamlined," but no longer called that. Similar early corporate design organizations, such as at Ford, Chrysler, General Electric, Westinghouse, the Radio Corporation of America, Herman Miller, Owens-Illinois, American Standard, among others, are still operational, but many have changed names through mergers or acquisitions, and still others have gone out of business. Sundberg-Ferar, founded in 1934 by its namesakes, is a still a highly successful design firm in Walled Lake, Michigan, with a 15,000-square-foot studio and a 7,000-square-foot prototype shop, with a talented, multidisciplinary team led by seven key personnel. À recent design was a Precedent golf cart for Ingersoll Rand's Club Car division. Clients since 2003 include Coca-Cola, General Electric, General Motors, Procter & Gamble, Sears, and Whirlpool. Peter Müller-Munk Associates (PMMA), founded by its namesake in 1938, continues as an active design firm in Pittsburgh, well known for way-finding signage design at major airports and public spaces. Brooks Stevens, Inc., the organization founded in 1934 by its namesake, is still an innovative and successful industrial design firm with offices in Allenton, Wisconsin, and St. Paul, Minnesota, and with dozens of well-known clients including Kohler, Harley-Davidson, General Electric, Rubbermaid, and Herman Miller. In 2007, the firm merged with Ingenium Product Development, bringing together over 70 experts. Recent products included the 100 Series John Deere lawn tractor, the Remington Code Shaver, and Milwaukee's Tilt-Lok circular saw. As of 2005, with about 10 employees, Henry Dreyfuss Associates, founded in 1929, continued to operate, with major corporate clients, and was located in Ann Arbor, Michigan, with William Crookes as president. Nessen Lighting, founded by Walter Von Nessen in 1927 as Nessen Studios, still designs and manufactures historical and contemporary lamps in Mamaroneck, New York. The firm founded by Dave Chapman in 1936, Dave Chapman, Inc., had been reorganized in 1970 as Goldsmith Yamasaki Specht (GYS). In 1996, First Design, a design firm in Kansas City, Kansas, purchased GYS, Inc., which had survived with Paul Specht as the only remaining partner. A partner of First Design, David McCormick, became president of GYS and retained the Chicago Office until 1999, when it was sold to Sundberg-Ferar. Deskey Integrated Branding (DIB), currently a firm specializing in branding strategy with headquarters in Cincinnati, Ohio, was founded in 1978 as Deskey, Inc., a division of Donald Deskey Associates of New York, which had been founded in the mid–1940s by Donald Deskey. The companies were combined around 1990 into Deskey Associates, Inc. The New York office was closed in September of 2001. Family trusts of famous designers such as Norman Bel Geddes and Raymond Loewy still merchandize images and books about their namesakes, but they are no longer active design organizations. Ford and Earl Associates, founded in 1964 by Harley Earl and Walter B. Ford, is still a design firm in Troy, Michigan, but now specializes in architecture and interior design. This is a pretty good survival record of design firms founded by designers of the Machine Age. We also have their classic, iconic designs, their books, and perhaps most of all, their legacy of today's creative, innovative, and successful industrial design profession, which continues to shape our physical environment of tools, machines, furnishings and equipment in a manner appropriate to our use, needs, and desires. We will continue to be inspired by the courage of the first machine designers to envision the modern world when there was none, and to implement it into reality despite the Great Depression of the 1930s. It was an extraordinary accomplishment that is unlikely to ever be repeated. The guidebook of the 1939 World's Fair probably said it better than anyone could: The true poets of the twentieth century are the designers, architects, and the engineers who glimpse some inner vision, create some beautiful figment of the imagination, and then translate it into valid reality for the world to enjoy.⁴


---
